Scattering and a Plancherel formula for real spherical spaces
Patrick Delorme

Abstract
We establish the analog for real homogeneous spherical varieties of the Scattering Theorem of Sakellaridis and Venkatesh ( [ 39 ] , Theorem 7.3.1) for p p -adic wavefront spherical varieties. We use properties of the Harish-Chandra homomorphism of Knop for invariant differential operators on the spherical variety, special coverings of the variety and spectral projections. Our main result depend on an analog of the Discrete Series Conjecture of Sakellaridis and Venkatesh ( [ 39 ] , Conjecture 9.4.6). Their result quoted above depends on this Discrete series conjecture. Nous établissons l’analogue pour les espaces sphériques réels du Théorème de scattering de Sakellaridis et Venkatesh pour les espaces sphériques p p -adiques qui sont wavefront. Nous utilisons des propriétés de l’homomorphisme d’Harish-Chandra de Knop pour les opérateurs différentiels invariants sur la variété, des recouvrements spéciaux et des projections spectrales. Notre résultat principal dépend d”une conjecture qui est un analogue de la Discrete series conjecture de Sakellaridis et Venkatesh ( [ 39 ] , Conjecture 9.4.6) dont dépend leur résultat cité ci-dessus.



To Jacques Carmona

1
Introduction

Let
G
be the group of real points of an algebraic connected reductive group
\underline{G}
defined over
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathfrak{g}
its Lie algebra. If
\underline{J}
is a subgroup of
\underline{G}
defined over
\mathbb{R}
, we will denote by
J
the group of its real points and by
\mathfrak{j}
(resp.
\underline{\mathfrak{j}}
) its Lie algebra (resp. the Lie algebra of
\underline{J}
). Let
X
be the variety of real points of a unimodular homogeneous
\underline{G}
-spherical variety defined over
\mathbb{R}
,
\underline{X}
. Let
P
be a minimal parabolic subgroup of
G
.
We assume in this introduction that
P
has a unique open
P
-orbit
,
Px_{0}
in
X
. Let
H
be the stabilizer of
x_{0}
in
G
. Then with our hypothesis
X
identifies to
G/H
. We assume that
G/H
admits a
G
-invariant measure.
The abstract Plancherel formula, following essentially
[
5
]
, ends up with an isomorphism:

L^{2}(X)\simeq\int_{\hat{G}}^{\oplus}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}d\mu(\pi)

(1.1)

where
(\pi,{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi})
is a continuous unitary irreducible representation of
G
,
{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
is a multiplicity space endowed with a scalar product and contained in the space
{\mathcal{H}}^{-\infty,H}_{\pi,temp}
of tempered, in a suitable sense (cf.
[
24
]
), of
H
-fixed distribution vectors of
\pi
, and
\mu
is a Borel measure on the unitary dual
{\hat{G}}
of
G
. Moreover
{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
is finite dimensional (cf.
[
28
]
,
[
31
]
).

The goal is to explicit this formula up to the twisted discrete spectrum (see below) for other spherical spaces, namely the boundary degenerations of
X
, which form a finite family of real spherical spaces including
X
.

The strategy has been given in the
p
-adic case by Sakellaridis and Venkatesh in
[
39
]
. It is done by using the Bernstein maps (see
[
39
]
, section 11, which is conditional to their Discrete Series Conjecture, cf. l.c., 9.4.6) and by the introduction of what they call scattering operators. In
[
13
]
the Bernstein maps were introduced in the real case without conditions, based on
[
8
]
, and studied there. In this paper we introduce the scattering operators in the real case and essentially prove their unitarity: this is also conditional to an analog of the Discrete Series Conjecture of Sakellaridis and Venkatesh (see below). Then the main results follow easily in a rather similar way to
[
39
]
proof of Theorem 7.3.1 in section 14.3.

Let us recall some results of the joint work with B. Krötz and S. Souaifi,
[
8
]
, and the joint work with F. Knop, B. Krötz and H. Schlichtkrull,
[
13
]
. With
X
and
P
comes a certain choice (not necessarily unique) of a maximal split torus
A
in
P
. Let
A_{X}:=A/A\cap H
. Then attached to these data is a finite set
S
of rational characters of
A_{X}
called the simple spherical roots. Viewed as elements of the dual
\mathfrak{a}_{X}^{*}
of the Lie algebra of
A_{X}
,
S
is the basis of a root system whose Weyl group is denoted
W_{X}
and is called the little Weyl group of
X
. Then to each
I\subset S
is associated a boundary degeneration of
X
, which is, with our hypothesis of a unique open
P
-orbit, an homogeneous space for
G
,
X_{I}=G/H_{I}
(in particular
X_{S}=X
). Moreover if
A_{I}
is the intersection of the kernels of the elements of
I
, the neutral componant
A^{0}_{I}
of the Lie group
A_{I}
acts on
X_{I}
by a right action, denoted by a dot, commuting to the action of
G
.

Let
Z(\mathfrak{g})
be the center of the complex enveloping algebra of
\mathfrak{g}
. The theory of the constant term (cf.
[
8
]
which generalizes the work of Harish-Chandra,
[
16
]
, for the group case and Carmona,
[
10
]
, for symmetric spaces; see also
[
40
]
, chapter 12, for the group case) defines a map
f\mapsto f_{I}
from
Z(\mathfrak{g})
-finite tempered functions on
X
to
Z(\mathfrak{g})
-finite tempered functions on
X_{I}
. It allows us to define a map:

j_{I,\pi}^{*}:{\mathcal{H}}^{-\infty,H}_{\pi,temp}\to{\mathcal{H}}^{-\infty,H_{I}}_{\pi,temp},\eta\mapsto\eta_{I}.

Note that
A_{I}^{0}
acts on
{\mathcal{H}}^{-\infty,H}_{\pi,temp}
. In
[
13
]
, Theorem 9.5 (cf.
[
39
]
, Theorem 11.3.1 for the
p
-adic case) it is proved that the Plancherel formula gives rise to a natural isomorphism:

L^{2}(X_{I})\simeq\int^{\oplus}_{\hat{G}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}d\mu(\pi),

(1.2)

where for almost all
\pi
,
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}
is the
A^{0}_{I}
-span of
j_{I,\pi}^{*}({\mathcal{M}}_{\pi})
(cf.
[
13
]
, (9.2)). Moreover the scalar product on
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}
is obtained by some process of limit from the scalar product on
{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
.

We have the Maass-Selberg relations from
[
13
]
, Theorem 9.6:

The adjoint
j_{I,\pi}:{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
of
j_{I,\pi}^{*}
is isometric on each
A^{0}_{I}
-eigenspace of
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}
,
or equivalently if
\chi\in\hat{A^{0}_{I}}
and
p_{\pi,\chi}
is the orthogonal projection of
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}
to the eigenspace
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}^{\chi}
,
p_{\pi,\chi}\circ j^{*}_{I,\pi}:{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}
is isometric modulo its kernel.

(1.3)

Let us introduce the twisted discrete series (abbreviated by
td
) of
X_{I}
. Let
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
be the Lie algebra of
A_{I}
. If
\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
, let
\chi_{\lambda}
be the unitary character of the connected component
A_{I}^{0}
of the Lie group
A_{I}
, whose differential is
\lambda
. Let
L^{2}(X_{I},\lambda)
be the unitarily induced representation from
H_{I}A_{I}^{0}
to
G
of the character of
H_{I}A_{I}^{0}
trivial on
H_{I}
and equal to
\chi_{\lambda}
on
A_{I}^{0}
. Let
L^{2}(X_{I},\lambda)_{td}
be the Hilbert sum of the irreducible subrepresentations of
L^{2}(X_{I},\lambda)
, which are called twisted discrete series representations
or
td
corresponding to
\lambda
. The character
\lambda
is called the twisting character. Then one can define, after solving some problems of measurability:

L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}:=\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}^{\oplus}L^{2}(X_{I},\lambda)_{td}d\lambda.

Let
\underline{M}
be the maximal anisotropic subgroup over
\mathbb{R}
of the centralizer of
\underline{A}
in
\underline{G}
. For a suitable choice of a Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{t}
of
M
, one introduces
\mathfrak{j}=\mathfrak{t}+\mathfrak{a}
which is a Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
. One intoduces
\mathfrak{a}_{\underline{X}}:=(\mathfrak{j}/\mathfrak{j}\cap\mathfrak{h})_{\mathbb{C}}
. Notice that
\mathfrak{a}_{X}\subset\mathfrak{a}_{\underline{X}}
. One denotes by
\underline{W}
the Weyl group of
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}
relative to
\mathfrak{j}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

It is known that the real part of the Harish-Chandra parameters of the infinitesimal characters of the various
td
of
L^{2}(X_{I})
are contained in a lattice of
\mathfrak{j}^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}
(cf
[
30
]
, Theorem 1.1).
Contrary to the work
[
13
]
where the Bernstein morphisms for real spherical spaces (sse below) are introduced and studied without using any conjecture, we will use a conjecture on twisted discrete series which seems coherent with the Discrete series Conjecture of
[
39
]
, 9.4.6, namely we conjecture:

For almost all
\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
, either
L^{2}(X_{I},\lambda)_{td}
is reduced to zero or for any
td
in
L^{2}(X_{I},\lambda)
, there is a Harish-Chandra parameter for its infinitesimal character whose imaginary part is given by the differential of the twisting character
\lambda
.

We do not need to conjecture that twisted discrete series are given by ”toric families”. Our conjecture is true when
I=S
and
\mathfrak{a}_{S}
is trivial, or more generally if
X
is factorizable (cf.
[
39
]
, 9.4.1 for the definition), from
[
30
]
. It is true also for the real analogues of Examples 9.5.2, 9.5.3, 9.5.4 of [SV].

One chooses a Borel subgroup
\underline{B}
of
\underline{G}
as in
[
21
]
, Theorem 1, whose Lie algebra contains
\mathfrak{j}
and let
\rho_{\underline{X}}
be the opposite of the half sum of the roots of
\mathfrak{j}
in the Lie algebra of
\underline{B}
. Let
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
be the algebra of
\underline{G}
-invariant completely regular differential operators on
\underline{X}
(cf.
[
21
]
, section 3) which is contained in the center of tha algebra
\mathbb{D}(\underline{X})
of
\underline{G}
-invariant differential operators on
\underline{X}
(cf. l.c., Corollary 9.2). From l.c., Theorem 6.5, the algebra
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
is known to be isomorphic, by the so called Harish-Chandra homomorphism to the algebra of invariant polynomials on
\rho_{\underline{X}}+\mathfrak{a}^{*}_{X}
under a finite group
\underline{W}_{\underline{X}}
. This group is generated by a set of reflections given by a finite subset set
\underline{S}
of
\mathfrak{a}_{\underline{X}}^{*}
. Actually
\underline{S}
is a basis of a root system whose Weyl group is equal to
\underline{W}_{\underline{X}}
. The later is called the little Weyl group of
\underline{X}
.

Kuit and Sayag
[
34
]
have related the Harish-Chandra homomorphism for
\underline{X}
and
\underline{X}_{I}
to a morphism
\mathbb{D}(\underline{X})\to\mathbb{D}(\underline{X}_{I})
that was introduced by Raphael Beuzart-Plessis in
[
8
]
, appendix C.

We show that
\mathbb{D}(\underline{X}_{I})
acts on
L^{2}(X_{I})
as an algebra generated by essentially self adjoint operators with common core the space of
C^{\infty}
-vectors for
G
, which allows a joint spectral decomposition for the commutative subalgebra
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
.

The conjecture together with the description of
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
above, allows us to show that
L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}
is the image of the spectral projection for
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
attached to a part of its unitary spectrum. Here I use a result on
C^{*}
-algebras (cf. Proposition
9.1
), whose proof was communicated to me by Alain Connes. It says essentially that, at least in special situations, the spectral projection, associated to a Borel subset of the unitary dual, of an Hilbert integral of representations is equal to the Hilbert integral of the corresponding spectral projections.

Let us define the Bernstein morphisms, introduced in
[
39
]
in the
p
-adic case and in
[
13
]
in our situation. These are
G
-maps
i_{I}:L^{2}(X_{I})\to L^{2}(X)
given in the Plancherel isomorphisms (
1.1
) and (
1.2
) by the following Hilbert integrals of operators:

\int_{\hat{G}}^{\oplus}i_{I,\pi}d\mu(\pi):\int_{\hat{G}}^{\oplus}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}d\mu(\pi)\to\int_{\hat{G}}^{\oplus}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}d\mu(\pi),

where
i_{I,\pi}=Id_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}}\otimes j_{I,\pi}
.

The Bernstein morphisms are abstract versions of wave packets of Eisenstein integrals of Harish-Chandra (cf.
[
17
]
): abstract because the maps
j_{I,\pi}:{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
are not explicit.

In
[
13
]
, Theorem 11.1, it has been shown that:

\sum_{I\subset S}i_{I}(L^{2}(X_{I})_{td})=L^{2}(X).

Let us describe the scattering operators. First we introduce the restriction
i_{I,td}
of
i_{I}
to
L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}
. Using spectral projections for
Z(\mathfrak{g})
, and decomposition of
L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}
(resp.
L^{2}(X_{J})_{td})
in
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
(resp.
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{J})
)-eigenspaces, using
[
30
]
and the conjecture, one shows that:

For
I,J\subset S
, the equivariant
G
-map
i^{*}_{J,td}\circ i_{I,td}
from
L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}
to
L^{2}(X_{J})_{td}
is non zero if and only if
I
and
J
are conjugated by an element of
W_{X}
, which will be denoted by
I\approx J
.

(1.4)

Again, using spectral projections but for
A^{0}_{I}
and
A^{0}_{J}
, it is relatively easy to see that if
I\approx J
, then:

i^{*}_{J,td}\circ i_{I,td}=\sum_{\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}}S_{\mathfrak{w}},

(1.5)

where
W_{I,J}
is the set elements of
W_{X}
which conjugate
I
to
J
.
The operators
S_{\mathfrak{w}}\in Hom_{G}(L^{2}(X_{I})_{td},L^{2}(X_{J})_{td})
are the so-called scattering operators which satisfy:

S_{\mathfrak{w}}r(a_{I})f=r(a_{I}^{\mathfrak{w}})S_{\mathfrak{w}}f,f\in L^{2}(X_{I})_{td},a_{I}\in A_{I}^{0}

where the
r
denote the right normalized, hence unitary, actions of
A^{0}_{I}
and
A^{0}_{J}
.
The main result, that we will describe now, follows essentially, as in
[
39
]
, from the fact that the
S_{\mathfrak{w}}
are unitary operators. We will try to explain below the main ingredients of the proof of this unitarity. Notice that this unitarity allows us to complete the Maass-Selberg relations (cf. Corollary
8.10
)). Our main result is:

1.1 Theorem
.

(i) If
I,J,K\subset S
,
I\approx J\approx K
:

i_{J,td}\circ S_{\mathfrak{w}}=i_{I,td},\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J},

S_{\mathfrak{w}}\circ S_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}=S_{\mathfrak{w}\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}},\mathfrak{w}\in W_{J,K},\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}\in W_{I,J},

i^{*}_{J,td}\circ i_{I,td}=\sum_{\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}}S_{\mathfrak{w}}.

(ii) Let
c(I)
be equal to
\sum_{J\approx I}CardW_{I,J}
. Then the map

\sum_{I\subset S}\frac{i^{*}_{I,td}}{\sqrt{c(I)}}:L^{2}(X)\to\oplus_{I\subset S}L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}

is an isometric isomorphism onto the subspace of

(f_{I})\in\oplus_{I\subset S}L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}

satysfying for
I\approx J
:

S_{\mathfrak{w}}f_{I}=f_{J},\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}.

This is a Plancherel formula which is the exact real analog of Theorem 7.3.1 of
[
39
]
. It depends on our conjecture on the imaginary part on the infinitesimal character of the twisted discrete series as Theorem 7.3.1 of l.c. rests on the Discrete Series Conjecture. Our proof does not use the generic injectivity of
[
39
]
, section 14.2. Moreover we do not require
X
to be wavefront neither that
G
is split.

If one wants to be more explicit, one needs a better description of the
td
and a suitable definition of Eisenstein integrals, both things being avalaible for real reductive symmetric spaces (see
[
13
]
, section 15 for a quick proof of the explicit Plancherel formula for these spaces).

Now, let us try to give an idea of the proof of the unitarity of the scattering operators. On one hand, this is certainly too much for an introduction. On the other hand, it gives a detailed account of the whole proof, which might serve as a guideline. It explains also the main ingredients which are used.

To prove that the scattering operators,
S_{\mathfrak{w}},\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J},I\approx J\subset S
, are unitary, it is enough, like in
[
39
]
, proof of Theorem 14.3.1, to prove that one can find a decomposition of
L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}
as a Hilbert sum of
A^{0}_{I}
-invariant Hilbert subspaces
{\mathcal{H}}_{m},m\in{\mathcal{M}}_{I},
such that for all
m\in{\mathcal{M}}_{I}
:

\|S_{\mathfrak{w}}v\|\geq\|v\|,\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J},v\in{\mathcal{H}}_{m}.

(1.6)

The Maass-Selberg relations allow to choose the
{\mathcal{H}}_{m}
on which
i_{I}
is isometric. We take the
{\mathcal{H}}_{m}
small enough: each of them is equal to an isotypic component under
K
of a subspace of
L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}
defined by spectral projections under
G
and
A_{I}^{0}
.
If (
1.6
) was false, one would find
I_{0},J_{0}\subset S
,
\mathfrak{w}_{0}\in W_{I_{0},J_{0}}
, some
m\in{\mathcal{M}}_{I_{0}}
, some non zero element
v_{0}
of
{\mathcal{H}}_{m}
and
\delta>0
such that:

\|S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}v_{0}\|\leq(1-\delta)\|v_{0}\|.

(1.7)

Let
f_{0}=i_{I_{0}}v_{0}\in L^{2}(X)
, which is of the same norm than
v_{0}
by the properties of
{\mathcal{H}}_{m}
.

The idea is to show that
\|f_{0}\|
(=
\|v_{0}\|)
can be bounded by a finite sum of small terms compared to
\|v_{0}\|
and of a preponderant term
\|S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}v_{0}\|
. Using (
1.7
), this leads to a contradiction:
\|v_{0}\|<\|v_{0}\|
. For this, one chooses some
a\in A^{0}_{I_{0}}
in ”good position” with respect to
\mathfrak{w}_{0}
and then works with
v_{n}=r(a^{n})v_{0}
instead of
v_{0}
, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
large enough. Notice that
r(a^{n})v_{0}
is of same norm than
v_{0}
. One can show (cf. Proposition
8.2
) that for
f\in{\mathcal{H}}_{m}
:

f=\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I_{0}}^{*}}f_{\nu}d\nu,

(1.8)

where the
f_{\nu}
are finite sums of
Z(\mathfrak{g})
-eigenfunctions on
X
coming from the expression of
i_{I}
as an Hilbert integral of operators.

Sakellaridis and Venkatesh were looking to the restriction of
f
to neighborhoods of infinity in a smooth compactification of
X
. These neighborhoods were obtained by gluing open sets given by the Local Structure Theorem (cf. e.g.
[
39
]
, Theorem 2.3.4,
[
23
]
Theorem 7.1).

Instead we use coverings of
X
by open sets built also from the Local Structure Theorem.

Recall that we assume that there is only one open
P
-orbit in
X
. In the following of the introduction we assume moreover that
X
has a wonderful
\underline{G}
-equivariant compactification
\overline{X}
. The
G
-orbits in
\overline{X}
are in canonical bijection with
I\subset S
. We denote by
Y_{I}
the
G
-orbit in
\overline{X}
corresponding to
I
and
X_{I}
appears as the open
G
-orbit in the normal bundle of
Y_{I}
in
\overline{X}
. Moreover
Y_{\emptyset}
is the closed orbit and
Y_{S}=X
.

To get what we call a standard covering of
X
, one trims first
Y_{\emptyset}
, which is compact, in
X
by a finite number of translates of an open set in
X
, given by the Local Structure Theorem, for which there is an
\varepsilon
-parameter which measures the proximity to the boundary (see below).

One does the same with every
G
-orbit in
\overline{X}
of immediately greater dimension, taking into account that the union of the former open sets trims already a part of this orbit. This part has a complementary compact set in this orbit.

We end up with a finite family
U_{I,\mathfrak{i},\varepsilon_{I}},I\subset S,\varepsilon_{I}>0,\mathfrak{i}\in\mathfrak{I}\subset G
, of open subsets of
X
, relatively compact in
\overline{X}
. The
U_{I,\mathfrak{i},\varepsilon_{I}}
’s, when
I
describes the subsets of
S
and
\mathfrak{i}\in\mathfrak{I}
, cover
X
.

Let us describe more precisely
U_{I,\mathfrak{i},\varepsilon_{I}}
: it is the left translate by
\mathfrak{i}\in\mathfrak{I}\subset G
of a set
U_{I,\varepsilon_{I}}:=\Omega_{I}A_{I}^{-}(\varepsilon_{I})
, where
\Omega_{I}
is some relatively compact set in
P
and
A^{-}_{I}(\varepsilon_{I}):=\{a\in A_{I}||a^{\alpha}|<\varepsilon_{I},\alpha\in S\setminus I\}
. In particular,
U_{\mathfrak{i}}:=U_{I,\mathfrak{i},\varepsilon_{I}}
is a subset of the
\mathfrak{i}
-translate of the open
P
-orbit in
X
which identifies with the
\mathfrak{i}
-translate of the open
P
-orbit in each boundary degeneration of
X
.

With these identifications in mind, let
I\subset S
and
\mathfrak{i}\in\mathfrak{I}
, let
f_{\mathfrak{i}}
be the restriction of our

f
to
U_{\mathfrak{i}}
. Let
f_{\nu},\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
, be as in (
1.8
) and, for
I\subset J\subset S
, let

f_{\nu,\mathfrak{i}}^{I,J}:=\sum_{K,I\subset K\subset J}(-1)^{CardJ-CardK}f_{\nu,K,\mathfrak{i}},

where the lower index
K
denotes the constant term and the index
\mathfrak{i}
indicates the restriction to
U_{\mathfrak{i}}
. Here all the functions are viewed as functions on
U_{\mathfrak{i}}
, by the above identification.

Then one has (cf. (
8.13
)):

f_{\mathfrak{i}}=\sum_{J,I\subset J\subset S}f^{I,J}_{\mathfrak{i}},

(1.9)

where
f^{I,J}_{\mathfrak{i}}
is given by the integral over
i\mathfrak{a}_{I_{0}}^{*}
of
f^{I,J}_{\nu,\mathfrak{i}}
.

An important ingredient of the proof is what we call
the Main Inequality
(cf. Theorem
5.7
and section
8.3
) which gives a bound for
f_{\nu,\mathfrak{i}}^{I,J}(\mathfrak{i}\omega a_{I})
for
\omega\in\Omega_{I}
and
a_{I}\in A_{I}^{-}(\varepsilon)
.

One introduces a lower index
n
if
v_{0}
is replaced by
v_{n}=r(a^{n})v_{0}
and let
f_{n}=i_{I_{0}}v_{n}
.

As the
U_{\mathfrak{i}}
’s,
\mathfrak{i}\in\mathfrak{I},I\subset S
, cover
X
, in order to bound
\|f_{n}\|^{2}=\int_{X}|f_{n}(x)|^{2}dx
, one is reduced, in view of (
1.9
), to study the integrals

I^{I,J}_{\mathfrak{i},n}=(\int_{U_{\mathfrak{i}}}|f^{I,J}_{\mathfrak{i},n}(x)|^{2}dx)^{1/2}.

First, the integral is zero unless
CardJ>CardI_{0}
or
J\approx I_{0}
: this is seen by using (
1.4
) and the characterization of
td
by vanishing properties of the constant terms (cf.
[
8
]
, Theorem 6.12). Recall that
U_{\mathfrak{i}}=\mathfrak{i}\Omega_{I}A^{-}_{I}(\varepsilon_{I})
and the measure on
U_{\mathfrak{i}}
decomposes as a product of measures.

The case where
CardJ>CardI_{0}
or the case where
J\approx I_{0}
and
I\not\approx I_{0}
are treated using the main inequality and
I^{I,J}_{\mathfrak{i},n}
can be made small by reducing
\varepsilon_{I}
and taking
n
large. In both cases, an integral of a positive function on a subset of
A^{0}_{J}
is bounded by its integral on
A^{0}_{J}
and the Plancherel formula for
A^{0}_{J}
is used.

Notice that reducing one
\varepsilon_{I}

might require to change the family
U_{\mathfrak{j}}
and even
\mathfrak{J}
, for
CardJ>CardI
. An induction on
CardI
is necessary.

In the remaining case
I=J\approx I_{0}
. It is quite different.

Then, as
I\approx I_{0}
,
the function
f^{I,I}_{\mathfrak{i},n}
is expressed as
\sum_{\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I_{0},I}}S_{\mathfrak{w}}f_{n}
on
U_{\mathfrak{i}}
viewed as a subset of
X_{I}
. Let

S_{n}(x):=|\sum_{\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I_{0},I}}S_{\mathfrak{w}}f_{n}(x)|^{2}

Recall our choice of
\mathfrak{w}_{0}
and
J_{0}
in (
1.7
).

Let
a\in A^{0}_{I_{0}}
be chosen such that
\mathfrak{w}_{0}(a)\in A_{J_{0}}^{-}:=A_{J_{0}}^{-}(0)
.

One has:

If
\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I_{0},J_{0}}
is distinct from
\mathfrak{w}_{0}
, then the sequence of characteristic functions of
\mathfrak{w}(a^{n}A_{I_{0}}^{-})
tends simply to zero,

(1.10)

as the characteristic function of a convex cone translated by
n
-times a vector
\underline{x}
converges simply to zero if the vector
-\underline{x}
is not in the closure of the cone.

The square root of the integral of
S_{n}(x)
on the union of the
U_{\mathfrak{i}}
is then shown, using (
1.10
) and the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem, to be bounded when
n
is larget, up to a small term, by a factor close to
1
of the square root of the integral on
X_{J_{0}}
of
|S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}v_{0}|^{2}
. Here a decisive tool is the analog of the exponential mappings (or rather their inverses) of Sakellaridis and Venkatesh
[
39
]
, Proposition 4.3.1, which I got wit the help of Raphael Beuzart-Plessis (cf. Proposition (
3.6
)).

Summarizing we have shown that
\|v_{0}\|=\|f_{0}\|=\|f_{n}\|
is bounded by the sum of arbitrary small quantities plus, up to a factor close to
1
, by
\|S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}v_{0}\|
. The later is, from (
1.7
), less than
(1-\delta)\|v_{0}\|=(1-\delta)\|f_{0}\|
. This leads to the desired contradiction.

I thank warmly Alain Connes for his help on the proof of Proposition
9.1
on spectral projections, which is used at many places in the manuscript. I thank also warmly Raphael Beuzart-Plessis for many helpful discussions-explanations all over the preparation of this manuscript, including on fans, compactifications, boundary degenerations and the Harish-Chandra homomorphism of Knop. And also in this decisive help to correct an earlier version, allowing to introduce the exponential mapping of Sakellaridis Venkatesh in the real case. I thank Sofiane Souaifi for his help in reading an earlier version of this work. I thank also Bernhard Krötz for his help. I thank Job Kuit for helpful remarks.

2
Notation

If
M
is a set and
\sim
is an equivalence relation on
M
, then we denote by
[m]
the equivalence class of
m\in M
. Often
the equivalence class is obtained by orbits of a group
G
acting on
M
. More specifically if
X,Y
are sets and
G
is
a group which acts on
X
from the right and acts on
Y
from the left, then we obtain a left
G
-action on
X\times Y
by
g\cdot(x,y):=(x\cdot g^{-1},g\cdot y)
whose set of equivalence classes we denote by
X\times_{G}Y
. We often abbreviate and simply write

[x,y]
instead of
[(x,y)]
to denote the equivalence class of
(x,y)
.

If
A
is a subset of a topological space,
clA
or
\overline{A}
will denote its closure.

If
f,g
are nonnegative functions on a set
E
, we will write
f\approx g
or
f(x)\approx g(x),x\in E,
if there are constants
C,C^{\prime}>0
such that

Cg(x)\leq f(x)\leq C^{\prime}g(x),x\in E.

(2.1)

If
E,F
are complex topological vector spaces,
Hom(E,F)
will denote the vector space of continuous linear maps.

If
G
is a real Lie group,
G^{0}
will denote its connected component of the identity,

\mathfrak{g}
will denote its Lie algebra,
U(\mathfrak{g})
(resp.
S(\mathfrak{g})
) the enveloping algebra (resp. symmetric algebra) of
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
Z(\mathfrak{g})
the center of
U(\mathfrak{g})
.

We will denote algebraic varieties defined over
\mathbb{R}
by underlined letters.
If
\underline{X}
is an algebraic variety defined over
\mathbb{R}
,
\underline{X}
might also denote its set of complex points and
X
will denote its set of real points.

If
\underline{A}
is an
\mathbb{R}
-split torus,
X_{*}(\underline{A})
will denote its group of rational cocharacters and
X^{*}(\underline{A})
its group of rational characters defined over
\mathbb{R}
. The group
X_{*}(\underline{A})
will be viewed as a subset of the Lie algebra
\underline{\mathfrak{a}}
of
\underline{A}
, by using the differential at 1 of the cocharacters.

3
Review of compactifications of real spherical varieties

3.1
Real spherical varieties, compactifications, Local Structure Theorem

Let
\underline{G}
be a connected reductive algebraic group defined over
\mathbb{R}
. Let
\underline{X}
be an homogeneous variety under
\underline{G}
. We assume that
X
has a
G
-invariant measure on each
G
-orbit: we will say that
X
is
unimodular
.

Let
\underline{P}
be a parabolic subgroup of
\underline{G}
minimal among the parabolic subgroups defined over
\mathbb{R}
. Then
X
is real spherical if and only
P

has an open orbit in
X
,
X_{P}=Px_{0}
, with
x_{0}\in X
. We introduce some notations relative to
\underline{P}
. Some of them depend on choices.

If
\underline{H}
is the stabilizer in
\underline{G}
of
x_{0}
, one has
\underline{X}=\underline{G}/\underline{H}
and
X=(\underline{G}/\underline{H})(\mathbb{R})
.
Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be the set of
G
-orbits in
X
. Among them, there is
G/H
.
Then the Local Structure Theorem (cf.
[
26
]
, Theorems 2.3 and Theorem 2.8) asserts the existence of a parabolic subgroup
\underline{P}(X)
of
\underline{G}
defined over
\mathbb{R}
, said adapted to
\underline{P}
, with Levi decomposition
\underline{P}(X)=\underline{L}(X)\underline{U}(X)
and containing
\underline{P}
such that

\underline{P}(X)\cap\underline{H}=\underline{L}(X)\cap\underline{H},\underline{P}(X)x_{0}=\underline{P}x_{0}.

(3.1)

Let
\underline{L}_{n}
be the connected normal
\mathbb{R}
-subgroup of
\underline{L}(X)
such that the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{l}}_{n}
of
L_{n}
is the sum of all non compact, non abelian, simple ideals of the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{l}}(X)
of
L(X)
. One has

\underline{L}_{n}\subset\underline{L}(X)\cap\underline{H}.

(3.2)

Let
\underline{A}
be a maximal
\mathbb{R}
-split torus of
\underline{L}(X)
contained in
\underline{P}
. Let
\underline{M}
be the maximal anisotropic subgroup over
\mathbb{R}
of the centralizer of
\underline{A}
in
\underline{G}
. Let
\underline{N}
be the unipotent radical of
\underline{P}
. Then one has
P=MAN
and
\underline{M}\underline{A}
is a Levi subgroup of
\underline{P}
.
Let us show

\underline{L}(X)=\underline{L}_{n}\underline{M}\underline{A}=\underline{M}\underline{A}\underline{L}_{n},\underline{L}(X)x_{0}=\underline{M}\underline{A}x_{0}.

(3.3)

Let
\underline{A}_{\underline{L}(X)}
be the maximal
\mathbb{R}
-split torus of the center of
\underline{L}(X)
. Let
\underline{L}_{is}
be the maximal anisotropic subgroup over
\mathbb{R}
of
\underline{L}(X)
which commutes to
\underline{L}_{n}
. As
\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{a}\cap\mathfrak{l}_{n}+\mathfrak{a}_{L(X)}
,
\underline{L}_{is}
is contained in the centralizer of
\underline{A}
in
\underline{G}
. Hence
\underline{L}_{is}\subset\underline{M}
. As
\underline{L}(X)=\underline{L}_{n}\underline{L}_{is}\underline{A}_{L(X)}
and
\underline{L}_{n}
is normal in
\underline{L}(X)
, our claim follows.

One has:

\underline{P}x_{0}=\underline{P}(X)x_{0}.

(3.4)

Using (
3.1
), (
3.2
) and (
3.3
), one sees easily:

\underline{P}(X)\cap\underline{H}=\underline{L}_{n}((\underline{M}\underline{A})\cap\underline{H}).

(3.5)

Hence the obvious multiplication map defines an isomorphism of algebraic variety:

\underline{P}x_{0}\simeq\underline{U}(X)\times\underline{L}(X)/\underline{L}(X)\cap\underline{H}\simeq\underline{U}(X)\times\underline{M}\underline{A}/((\underline{M}\underline{A})\cap\underline{H}).

(3.6)

We define :

\underline{A}_{X}:=\underline{A}/\underline{A}\cap\underline{H}\subset\underline{G}/\underline{H}=\underline{X}.

(3.7)

which is an
\mathbb{R}
-split torus. As
\underline{A}=(\underline{A}\cap\underline{L}_{n})\underline{A}_{L(X)}
:

\underline{A}_{X}=\underline{A}_{\underline{L}(X)}/(\underline{A}_{\underline{L}(X)}\cap\underline{H}).

(3.8)

We want to describe more precisely
(\underline{M}\underline{A})\cap\underline{H})
.
We recall some facts fromÊ
[
8
]
, section 1 and Appendix B which are summarized in
[
13
]
, section 2. Define

\hat{\underline{M}}_{H}=\{m\in\underline{M}|mx_{0}\in\underline{A}_{X}\subset\underline{G}/\underline{H}\}

which is the isotropy subgroup of the action of
\underline{M}
on
\underline{L}/(\underline{L}\cap\underline{H})\underline{A}
.
Then
F_{M}:=\hat{\underline{M}}_{H}(\mathbb{R})/(\underline{M}\cap\underline{H})(\mathbb{R})\simeq\hat{M}_{H}/(M\cap H)
is a finite 2-group, which imbeds naturally in
A_{X}
.
Then

\underline{L}/\underline{L}_{H}\simeq\underline{M}/(\underline{M}\cap\underline{H})\times_{F_{M}}\underline{A}_{X}

(3.9)

and (cf.
[
8
]
, Proposition B.2):

[\underline{L}/(\underline{L}\cap\underline{H})](\mathbb{R})\simeq(M/(M\cap H))\times_{F_{M}}A_{X}.

From this it follows:

(\underline{M}\underline{A})\cap\underline{H}=\{ma|m\in\hat{\underline{M}}_{H},a\in\underline{A}\cap m^{-1}(\underline{A}\cap\underline{H})\},

(MA)\cap H=\{ma|m\in\hat{M}_{H},a\in A\cap m^{-1}(A\cap H)\}.

(3.10)

We will denote by
\rho
the half sum of the roots of
\mathfrak{a}
in
\mathfrak{u}(X)
, which goes through the quotient to
\mathfrak{a}_{X}
(cf.
[
24
]
, Lemma 4.2). With these data comes the set
S
of simple spherical roots (cf.
[
13
]
, section 2.2). It is a subset of the group of rational characters of
A_{X}
, but it is not necessarily a basis of this lattice.
We introduce a complete fan
{\mathcal{F}}
in
\mathfrak{a}_{X}^{-}:=\{\underline{x}\in\mathfrak{a}_{X}|\alpha(\underline{x})\leq 0,\alpha\in S\}
that we fix once and for all.
This is a set of simplicial cones covering
\mathfrak{a}_{X}^{-}
which satisfies in particular the following properties:

For every
C\in{\mathcal{F}}
there is a unique finite family
\check{C}
of cocharacters of
\underline{A}_{X}
which is free and such that

C\cap X_{*}(\underline{A}_{X})=\sum_{\check{\alpha}\in\check{C}}\mathbb{N}\check{\alpha},C=\sum_{\check{\alpha}\in\check{C}}\mathbb{R}^{+}\check{\alpha}.

Here
\check{\alpha}
is identified to an element of
\mathfrak{a}_{X}
by denoting in the same way the derivative in
0
of
t\mapsto\check{\alpha}(e^{t})
. Then one has:

\check{\alpha}(e^{t})=\exp(t\check{\alpha}).

Moreover the simplicial cones (
C_{i})_{i=1,\dots,n}
of maximal dimension are of dimension
\mathrm{dim}\,\mathfrak{a}_{X}
, their union is equal to
\mathfrak{a}_{X}^{-}
and, for each
i
,
\check{C_{i}}
is a basis of
X_{*}(\underline{A}_{X})
. Finally one requires that their two by two intersections are common faces.

(3.11)

Let
\underline{A}_{C}\subset\underline{A}_{X}
be the image of the morphism

j_{C}:\mathbb{C}^{*\check{C}}\to\underline{A}_{X},(z_{\check{\alpha}})\mapsto\prod\check{\alpha}(z_{\check{\alpha}}).

(3.12)

Then
j_{C}
is an isomorphism onto its image
\underline{A}_{C}
: this is obvious as
\check{C}
is free. With our convention,
A_{C}
is the group of real points of
\underline{A}_{C}
and
A_{C}^{0}
is the the identity component of this real Lie group.

Let
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}({\mathcal{F}})
be the partial compactification of
\underline{A}_{X}
attached to
{\mathcal{F}}
.

Following (
3.6
), we form

\overline{\underline{X}}_{P}({\mathcal{F}})=\underline{U}(X)\times(\underline{L}(X)/\underline{L}(X)\cap\underline{H})\times_{\underline{A}_{X}}\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}({\mathcal{F}}).

It follows of
[
23
]
, Theorem 7.1 and Corollary 7.12, that there exists a compact smooth
\underline{G}
-variety
\overline{\underline{X}}({\mathcal{F}})=\underline{G}\overline{\underline{X}}_{0}({\mathcal{F}})
containing
\overline{\underline{X}}_{0}({\mathcal{F}})
as an open set and such that
\overline{\underline{X}}({\mathcal{F}})(\mathbb{R})
is compact. We will denote
\overline{\underline{X}}({\mathcal{F}})
(resp.
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}({\mathcal{F}})
by
\overline{\underline{X}}
(res.
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}
) if there is no ambiguity.

Let
\overline{\underline{X}}_{P}
be the union of the open
\underline{P}
-orbits in the
\underline{G}
-orbits of
\overline{\underline{X}}
.
Then the Local Structure Theorem
[
23
]
Theorem 7.1, LST for short, for the compactification and (
3.9
) asserts:

The map
\underline{U}(X)\times[\underline{M}/\underline{M}_{H}\times_{F_{M}}\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}]\to\overline{\underline{X}}_{P}
,
(u,[m,a])\mapsto uma
is an isomorphism.

(3.13)

There are special points in
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}
,
y_{C}
,
C\subset{\mathcal{F}}
, defined by

y_{C}=lim_{t\to+\infty}(\exp t\underline{x})x_{0},

(3.14)

where
\underline{x}
is in the relative interior
C^{0}
of
C
in its linear span
\mathfrak{a}_{C}
.
The stabilizer in
\underline{A}_{X}
of
y_{C}
is equal to
\underline{A}_{C}
.

The
y_{C}
are the representatives of the
\underline{G}
-orbits (resp. open
\underline{P}
-orbits in the
\underline{G}
-orbits) in
\overline{\underline{X}}
and of the
\underline{A}_{X}
-orbits in
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}
(cf.
[
23
]
, Corollary 7.5). Hence every
\underline{G}
-orbit has an open
\underline{P}
-orbit. We will denote the
\underline{G}
-orbit (resp.
\underline{P}
-orbit) of
y_{C}
in
\overline{\underline{X}}

(resp
\overline{\underline{X}}_{P}
) by
\underline{Y}_{C}
(resp.
\underline{Y}_{C,P}
) and by
\underline{V}_{C}
the orbit of
y_{C}
in
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}
under
\underline{A}_{X}
.
We will denote by
\underline{Z}_{C}
the closure of
\underline{Y}_{C}
in
\overline{\underline{X}}
and by
\underline{W}_{C}
the closure of
\underline{V}_{C}
in
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}
.

Let
F_{M,C}:=F_{M}\cap\underline{A}_{C}
and
F_{M}^{C}:=F_{M}/F_{M,C}
. Let
\tilde{\underline{A}}_{C}
be the set of
a\in\underline{A}
whose projection in
\underline{A}_{X}
belongs to
\underline{A}_{C}
. As
\underline{A}_{C}
is the stabilizer of
y_{C}
in
\underline{A}_{X}
one deduces
from (
3.13
) that:

\underline{P}y_{C}\simeq\underline{U}(X)\times[[\underline{M}/\underline{M}_{H}F_{M,C}]\times_{F_{M}^{C}}[\underline{A}_{X}/\underline{A}_{C}]],

(3.15)

and the stabilizer
\hat{\underline{H}}_{C}
of
y_{C}
in
\underline{G}
satisfies:

\hat{\underline{H}}_{C}\cap\underline{M}\underline{A}=\{ma|m\in\hat{\underline{M}}_{H},a\in\underline{A}\cap m^{-1}\tilde{\underline{A}}_{C}\},

(3.16)

From this and and (
3.10
), one deduces:

\hat{\underline{H}}_{C}\cap\underline{M}\underline{A}=(\underline{H}\cap(\underline{M}\underline{A}))\tilde{\underline{A}}_{C}.

(3.17)

The group
\underline{L}_{n}
is contained in
\underline{H}
and commutes with
\underline{A}_{\underline{L}(X)}
. Hence it follows from (
3.8
) that all elements of
\underline{L}_{n}
fix all points of the curve in (
3.14
) hence also
y_{C}
. From this it follows that
\underline{L}_{n}\subset\hat{\underline{H}}_{C}
. As
\underline{U}(X)\underline{L}(X)=\underline{L}_{n}\underline{U}(X)\underline{L}_{is}\underline{A}_{L(X)}
and as
\underline{L}_{is}\underline{A}_{\underline{L}(X)}\subset\underline{M}\underline{A}
, one has
\underline{P}(X)\cap\hat{\underline{H}}_{C}=\underline{L}_{n}((\underline{U}(X)\underline{M}\underline{A})\cap\hat{\underline{H}}_{C})
.
From (
3.13
), one deduces:

\hat{\underline{H}}_{C}\cap\underline{P}(X)=\hat{\underline{H}}_{C}\cap\underline{L}(X)=\underline{L}_{n}(\hat{\underline{H}}_{C}\cap\underline{M}\underline{A}).

(3.18)

If
g\in G
, let
\underline{P}_{G}:=g\underline{P}g^{-1}
,
x_{g}=gx_{0}
. One can introduce similar objects related to these data, the corresponding groups will be denoted with a lower
g
index and there are obtained by conjugacy by
g
.

(3.19)

3.2
Normal bundles, boundary degenerations, exponential mappings

Let
C\in{\mathcal{F}}
. Let
\underline{X}_{C}
be the open
\underline{G}
-orbit in the normal bundle to
Z_{C}
in
\overline{\underline{X}}
,
N_{\underline{Z}_{C}}(\overline{\underline{X}})
. Let
\underline{V}_{C}
be the orbit of
y_{C}
under
\underline{A}_{X}
in
\overline{\underline{A}}_{\underline{X}}
. There are some properties of
\underline{X}_{C}
that we will describe in two lemmas.

3.1 Lemma
.

(i)
The map
j^{\prime}_{C}:\mathbb{C}^{*\check{C}}\to\underline{A}_{X},z\mapsto j_{C}(z^{-1})
(cf. (
3.12
) for the definition of
j_{C}
), extends by continuity to a differentiable map, denoted in the same way, from
\mathbb{C}^{\check{C}}
to
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}
with value
y_{C}
at
0
. Moreover the image of the differential at 0 of this extended map is a canonical supplementary to the tangent space at
y_{C}
of
\underline{V}_{C}
(resp.
\underline{Y}_{C}
) in the tangent space at
y_{C}
of
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}
(resp.
\overline{\underline{X}}
). It is denoted by
\underline{\mathfrak{a}}_{C,\mathbb{C}}
and it identifies with
\mathbb{C}^{\check{C}}
by this differential. This will be viewed as the fiber over
y_{C}
of the normal bundle in
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}
(resp.
\overline{\underline{X}}
) of
\underline{V}_{C}
(resp. of
\underline{Y}_{C}
).

(ii) As
\underline{A}_{C}
fixes
y_{C}
, it acts on the fiber over
y_{C}
of the normal bundle in
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}
of
\underline{V}_{C}
, that we have identified with
\underline{\mathfrak{a}}_{C,\mathbb{C}}
. We denote this action by a dot and when
\underline{A}_{C}
is identified to
\mathbb{C}^{*\check{C}}
by
j^{\prime}_{C}
, the action is given by multiplication, component by component.

(iii) Let
e_{C}=\sum_{\check{\alpha}\in\check{C}}\check{\alpha}\in C
that will be identified to its image in
\underline{\mathfrak{a}}_{C,\mathbb{C}}
. Then
x_{C}=(y_{C},e_{C})\in N_{\underline{V}_{C}}(\overline{\underline{A}}_{X})
is an element of
\underline{X}_{C}
.
The intersection of
\underline{X}_{C}
with the fiber over
y_{C}
of
N_{\underline{Y}_{C}}(\overline{\underline{X}})
is the open
\underline{A}_{C}
-orbit in this fiber, which contains
(y_{C},e_{C})
. Let
\underline{H}_{C}
be the stabilizer of
x_{C}
in
\underline{G}
.

(iv) For
h\in\hat{\underline{H}}_{C}
, there exists a unique
\rho(h)\in\underline{A}_{C}
such that
hx_{C}=\rho(h).x_{C}
. One has an exact sequence:

0\to\underline{H}_{C}\to\hat{\underline{H}}_{C}\to_{\rho}\underline{A}_{C}\to 0.

Proof.

(i) From LST (cf.(
3.13
)), the statements reduce to statements about
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}=\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}({\mathcal{F}})
. First if
{\mathcal{F}}
consists of the faces of only one simplicial cone
C_{0}
of maximal dimension, things are quite simple:
j^{\prime}_{C_{0}}
identifies
\underline{A}_{X}
(resp.
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}
) with
\mathbb{C}^{*\check{C}_{0}}
(resp.
\mathbb{C}^{\check{C}_{0}}
). Then
\underline{A}_{C}
identifies with
\mathbb{C}^{*\check{C}}\times\{(1,\dots,1)
},
y_{C}=((0,\dots 0),(1,\dots,1))\in\mathbb{C}^{\check{C}}\times\mathbb{C}^{*\check{C_{0}}\setminus\check{C}}
and

x_{0}=(1,\dots,1)
. Our claims are then obvious. It is necessary to introduce
z^{-1}
for the following reason. If
z=(z_{\check{\alpha}}),\check{\alpha}\in\check{C}
and
z_{\check{\alpha}}=e^{t_{\alpha}}
with
t_{\alpha}
goes to
-\infty
,
z
goest to zero and (cf. (
3.11
)),
j_{C}(z^{-1})=\exp(\sum_{\check{\alpha}\in\check{C}}-t_{\check{\alpha}}\check{\alpha})
goes to
y_{C}
from (
3.14
).

In the general case, let
C_{0}
be an element of
{\mathcal{F}}
of maximal dimension containing
C
. Let
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
be the subfan of
{\mathcal{F}}
consisting of the faces of
C_{0}
. Then
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}({\mathcal{F}}_{0})\subset\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}
. It is equal to the union of
\underline{V}_{C^{\prime}},C^{\prime}\subset C_{0}
. Let
C_{1}\in{\mathcal{F}}
. The closure of the
\underline{A}_{X}
-orbit
\underline{V}_{C_{1}}
in
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}
is equal to
\cup_{C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}},C_{1}\subset C^{\prime}}\underline{V}_{C^{\prime}}
. Let
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}({\mathcal{F}}_{0})^{c}
be the complementary set of
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}({\mathcal{F}}_{0})
in
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}({\mathcal{F}})
. It is the union of
\underline{V}_{C_{1}}
,
C_{1}\in{\mathcal{F}},C_{1}\not\subset C_{0}
. But if
C_{1}\not\subset C_{0}
, the closure of the
\underline{A}_{X}
-orbit
\underline{V}_{C_{1}}
in
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}
is clearly contained in
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}({\mathcal{F}}_{0})^{c}
. This implies easily that the complementary set of
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}({\mathcal{F}}_{0})
in
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}({\mathcal{F}})
is closed. Hence
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}({\mathcal{F}}_{0})
is open in
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}({\mathcal{F}})
and our claims on
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}
follow from the properties for
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}({\mathcal{F}}_{0})
that we have just proved.

(ii) is proved as (i) by reduction to the case where
{\mathcal{F}}
consists of the faces of one simplicial cone of maximal dimension.

(iii) From (i) and (ii), there is only one open nonempty
\underline{A}_{C}
-orbit in the fiber over
y_{C}
of
N_{\underline{Y}_{C}}(\overline{\underline{X}})
. Hence the intersection of
\underline{X}_{C}
with the fiber contains this open
\underline{A}_{C}
-orbit and from the description of the
\underline{A}_{C}
-action in (ii),
(y_{C},e_{C})
is an element of this open
\underline{A}_{C}
-orbit. From (
3.23
), this intersection is equal to the
\underline{A}_{C}
-orbit of
(y_{C},e_{C})
on which
\underline{A}_{C}
acts simply transitively (cf. (ii)). It proves (iii).

(iv)
If
h\in\hat{\underline{H}}_{C}
,
hx_{C}
is in the intersection of
\underline{X}_{C}
with the fiber of
N_{\underline{Y}_{C}}(\overline{\underline{X}})
over
y_{C}
. The existence and unicity of
\rho(h)
follows from (iii) and (ii). This implies the exact sequence.

3.2 Lemma
.

Let
C\in{\mathcal{F}}
.

(i) One has:

(a)
\underline{P}(X)x_{C}=\underline{P}x_{C},\underline{P}(X)\cap\underline{H}_{C}=\underline{L}(X)\cap\underline{H}_{C}=(\underline{M}\underline{A})\cap\underline{H}_{C})\underline{L}_{n}.

(b) Hence
\underline{P}(X)
is
\underline{P}
-adapted to
\underline{X}_{C}
.

(c)
(\underline{M}\underline{A})\cap\underline{H}_{C}=(\underline{M}\underline{A})\cap\underline{H}
,
\underline{L}(X)\cap\underline{H}_{C}=\underline{L}(X)\cap\underline{H}
.

(ii)
The group
\underline{H}_{C}
depends only on the set
I(C)
of elements of
S
orthogonal to
C
. We denote it by
\underline{H}_{I}
where
I=I(C)
. The
G
-space
X_{C}
is real spherical and the set of spherical roots of
X_{C}
,
S_{C}
, is equal to
I(C)
.

(iii) If
C,C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}
satisfy
I(C)=I(C^{\prime})
, there is a unique
\underline{G}
-equivariant isomorphism between
\underline{X}_{C}
and
\underline{X}_{C^{\prime}}
sending
x_{C}
to
x_{C^{\prime}}
.

(iv) Let
\underline{A}_{I(C)}
be the connected component of the intersection of the kernels of the roots in
I(C)
. Its Lie algebra is equal to the complexification of
\mathfrak{a}_{I(C)}=\{\underline{x}\in\mathfrak{a}_{X}|\alpha(\underline{x})=0,\alpha\in I(C)\}
. The group
\underline{A}_{I(C)}
acts on the right as a group of automorphisms of
\underline{X}_{C}
identified with
\underline{G}_{C}/\underline{H}_{C}
commuting with the action of
\underline{G}
. This action will be denoted by a dot.

(v) The neutral component
A^{0}_{I(C)}
, of the Lie group
A_{I(C)}
acts naturally on
X_{C}
and preserves the
G
-orbits.

Proof.

(i) Let us show first that
\underline{L}\cap\underline{H}
is contained in
\underline{H}_{C}
. We consider the path
\gamma(t)=\exp((-logt)e_{C})x_{0}\in\overline{\underline{X}}
,
t\in]0,1]
extended by
\gamma(0)=y_{C}
. Then using the properties of
j^{\prime}_{C}
of Lemma
3.1
(i) and the identification there, one sees that
\gamma(t)=j^{\prime}_{C}(t(1,\dots,1))
and that the right derivative in 0 of
\gamma(t)
is equal to
e_{C}
.

Using (
3.8
), one takes a representative of
\exp((-\log t)e_{C})x_{0}
in
\underline{A}_{\underline{L}(X)}
and one sees that
\underline{L}(X)\cap\underline{H}
acts trivially on
\gamma(t)
, hence on
x_{C}
, by going to the limit.
Hence
\underline{L}(X)\cap\underline{H}\subset\underline{H}_{C}
.
From this, (
3.3
) and (
3.2
), it follows:

\underline{P}(X)x_{C}=\underline{U}(X)\underline{M}\underline{A}x_{C}=\underline{P}x_{C}.

(3.20)

This proves the first equality of (a).
Now let
g\in\underline{P}(X)\cap\underline{H}_{C}
. Then, from (
3.3
),
g=umal
with
u\in\underline{U}(X)
,
m\in\underline{M}
,
a\in\underline{A}
,
l\in\underline{L}_{n}
and
umax_{C}=x_{C}
. In particular
umay_{C}=y_{C}
and from (
3.15
),
u=1
and
ma\in\underline{H}_{C}
. This proves the second part of (a).

Then (b) follows.

Let us proves (c). One has seen
(\underline{M}\underline{A})\cap\underline{H}\subset\underline{H}_{C}
. From
(\underline{M}\underline{A})\cap\underline{H}_{C}\subset\hat{\underline{H}}_{C}
and (
3.17
), one has :

(\underline{M}\underline{A})\cap\underline{H}_{C}\subset((\underline{M}\underline{A})\cap\underline{H})\tilde{\underline{A}}_{C}.

To achieve to prove the first equality of (c), it remains to remark that
\tilde{\underline{A}}_{C}\cap\underline{H}_{C}=\underline{A}\cap\underline{H}
, as the stabilizer of
e_{C}
in
\underline{A}_{C}
is trivial. Together with the last equality of (a), the second equality of (c) follows.

This achieves to prove (i).

(ii) As the dimension of the normal bundle to
\underline{Y}_{C}
in
\overline{\underline{X}}
is equal to the dimension of
\overline{\underline{X}}
, hence of
\underline{X}
, one has
\mathrm{dim}\,\underline{X}_{C}=\mathrm{dim}\,\underline{X}
and
\mathrm{dim}\,\underline{H}_{C}=\mathrm{dim}\,\underline{H}
. The limit in the Grassmaniann
lim_{t\to+\infty}e^{(-\log t)ade_{C}}\underline{\mathfrak{h}}
depends only on
I=I(C)
, as
e_{C}\in\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{--}
, (cf.
[
13
]
, (2.21)) and is denoted
\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{I}
.

One will first show that
\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{I}
is a subalgebra of the Lie algebra of
\underline{H}_{C}
. The
\underline{G}
-stabilizer of
\gamma(t)=\exp((-logt)e_{C})x_{0}
is equal to
\underline{H}_{t}=\exp((-logt)e_{C})\underline{H}\exp((-\log t)e_{C})^{-1}
. As
(\exp((-\log t)e_{C})x_{0})
tends to
y_{C}
when
t
tends to zero, this implies that
\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{I}
is contained in the Lie algebra of the stabilizer
\hat{H}_{C}
of
y_{C}
. One has seen that
\gamma
has
e_{C}
as right derivative in
0
.
One shows as in
[
13
]
, proof of Lemma 4.2, that
d/dt(\exp Y\gamma(t))_{|t=0}=e_{C}
for
Y\in\mathfrak{h}_{I}
. This implies as in l.c. that
\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{I}
is a Lie subalgebra of the Lie algebra of
\underline{H}_{C}
.

For reason of dimension,
\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{I}
is equal to the Lie algebra of
\underline{H}_{C}
. Hence the connected component of
\underline{H}_{C}
depends only on
I
.

On the other hand,
\underline{P}(X)\underline{H}^{0}_{C}
is open in
\underline{G}
(see for example
[
27
]
, proof of Proposition 3.2 (1), where it is shown that
\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{I}+\underline{\mathfrak{p}}=\underline{\mathfrak{g}}
) and, by unicity of the open
(\underline{P}(X),\underline{H}^{0}_{C})
-orbit in
\underline{G}
, one has
\underline{P}(X)\underline{H}_{C}=\underline{P}(X)\underline{H}_{C}^{0}
: if
h\in\underline{H}_{C}
,
\underline{P}(X)h\underline{H}_{C}^{0}=\underline{P}(X)\underline{H}_{C}^{0}h
is open and equal to
\underline{P}(X)\underline{H}_{C}^{0}
. From this, one sees that
\underline{H}_{C}=(\underline{P}(X)\cap\underline{H}_{C})\underline{H}_{C}^{0}
.
But from (i) a) and c)
\underline{P}(X)\cap\underline{H}_{C}=\underline{P}(X)\cap\underline{H}
and one has seen that
\underline{H}^{0}_{C}
only depends on
I
. This implies the first claim of (ii). This and (i) (a) show that the dimension of
Px_{C}
and
Px_{0}
are equal to the common dimension of
X_{C}
and
X
. Hence
X_{C}
is real spherical.

From our definition of simple spherical roots, the final statement of (ii) can be seen at the level of Lie algebras and follows from
[
27
]
, Proposition 3.2 (4).

(iii) follows from (ii).

(iv) From (iii), it is enough to prove this for a particular
C
with
I=I(C)
given. Let us first show that:

Let
I\subset S
. There exists
C\in{\mathcal{F}}
with
\mathfrak{a}_{C}=\mathfrak{a}_{I}
.

(3.21)

One has
\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{--}=\cup_{j}\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{--}\cap C_{j}
where
(C_{j})
is the family of elements of
{\mathcal{F}}
of maximal dimension. Hence one of these sets, say
C_{j_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{--}
, has a non empty interior in
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
. As for all
\check{c}\in\check{C}_{j_{0}}
and
\alpha\in S
,
\alpha(\check{c})\leq 0
, one sees, by writing an element of
C_{j_{0}}
as a linear combination with nonnegative cofficients of
\check{C_{j_{0}}}
that
C_{j_{0}}\cap\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{-}
is the face
C
of
C_{j_{0}}
generated by the
\check{c}\in\check{C}_{j_{0}}
such that
\alpha(\check{c})=0,\alpha\in I
. Hence
C\subset\mathfrak{a}_{I}
and has a non empty interior in
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
. Hence
\mathfrak{a}_{C}=\mathfrak{a}_{I}
and
\underline{A}_{C}=\underline{A}_{I}
. This proves our claim.

Let us take such a
C
, i.e.
\mathfrak{a}_{C}=\mathfrak{a}_{I(C)}
.

From the exact sequence in Lemma
3.1
, one knows that
\hat{\underline{H}}_{C}
normalizes
\underline{H}_{C}
, and, with our choice of
C
,
\underline{A}_{I}
is the quotient of
\hat{\underline{H}}_{C}
by
\underline{H}_{C}
. As
\underline{X}_{C}
is identified with
\underline{G}/\underline{H}_{C}
, (iv) follows.

(v) Going to real points in (iv), one sees that
\hat{H}_{I}/H_{I}
acts on
X_{I}
identified with
(\underline{G}/\underline{H}_{I})(\mathbb{R})
. Although the natural injective map

\hat{H}_{I}/H_{I}\to A_{I}
is not necessarily surjective, the image contains
A^{0}_{I}
. Hence
A_{I}^{0}
acts on
X_{C}
.

Let
C\in{\mathcal{F}}
. Let
N_{\underline{Y}_{C}}(\overline{\underline{X}})
(resp.
N_{\underline{Z}_{C}}(\overline{\underline{X}})
) be the normal bundle to the subvariety
\underline{Y}_{C}
(resp.
Z_{C}
) of
\overline{\underline{X}}

and let
N_{\underline{V}_{C}}(\overline{\underline{A}}_{X})
(resp.
N_{\underline{W}_{C}}(\overline{\underline{A}}_{X})
) be the normal bundle to
\underline{V}_{C}
(resp.
\underline{W}_{C}
) in
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}
, which, from (
3.13
), is a subset of
N_{\underline{Z}_{C}}(\overline{\underline{X}})
.
From the Local Structure Theorem (cf (
3.13
)), one has:

The map
\underline{U}(X)\times(\underline{M}/\underline{M}_{H}\times_{F_{M}}N_{\underline{W}_{C}}(\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}))\to N_{\underline{Z}_{C}}(\overline{\underline{X}})
:

(u,[m,a])\mapsto uma

is an open embedding with image, denoted by
N_{\underline{Z}_{C}}(\overline{\underline{X}})_{P}
, equal to the union of the open
\underline{P}
-orbits in the
\underline{G}
-orbits of
N_{\underline{Z}_{C}}(\overline{\underline{X}})
.

(3.22)

Let
{\mathcal{F}}(C)
be the fan which is the set of faces of the cones of
{\mathcal{F}}
of maximal dimension which have
C
has a face. Then the normal bundle to
\underline{W}_{C}
identifies to
\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}({\mathcal{F}}(C))
.
Hence, from the local structure theorem, one has

There is a natural isomorphism

e_{P,C}:\overline{\underline{X}}_{P}({\mathcal{F}}(C)):=\underline{U}(X)\times(\underline{M}/\underline{M}_{H}\times_{F_{M}}\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}({\mathcal{F}}(C))\to N_{\underline{Z}_{C}}(\overline{\underline{X}})_{P}.

(3.23)

We fix an auxiliary riemannian structure on
\overline{\underline{X}}
.

If
z\in Z_{C}
, let
N_{Z_{C}}(\overline{\underline{X}})_{z}
the fiber above
z
in
N_{Z_{C}}(\overline{\underline{X}})
.

Let
d>0
. If
Z_{1}
is an subset of
Z_{C}
, let us define
N^{r}(Z_{1},d)
be the set of elements of
\overline{X}({\mathcal{F}}(C)
which are at a distance strictly less than
d
of
Z_{1}
.

Notice that the
\underline{G}
-orbits in
\overline{\underline{X}}({\mathcal{F}}(C))
(resp.
N_{\underline{Z}_{C}}(\overline{\underline{X}})
) are in bijections with the elements of
{\mathcal{F}}(C)
.

3.3 Definition
.

A
C^{\infty}
map
\phi
from an open set
U_{1}
of
\overline{X}
to an open set
U_{2}
of
N_{Z_{C}}(\overline{X})
is said good if:

1) it induces the identity between the normal bundle of to
Z_{C\cap U_{1}}
to the normal bundle to
Z_{C\cap U_{2}}
.

2)
\phi
sends the intersection of a
\underline{G}
-orbit of
\overline{\underline{X}}({\mathcal{F}}(C))
with
U_{1}
in the corresponding
\underline{G}
-orbit in
\overline{\underline{X}}
.

The restriction of
e_{P,C}
to
\overline{X}_{P}
, still denoted in the same way, is good.

(3.24)

We want to characterize the property to be good in local coordinates.

3.4 Lemma
.

Let
C_{1},\dots,C_{m}
be the cones of maximal dimension of
{\mathcal{F}}
which contains
C
. If
C_{j}
is such a cone,
C^{j}
is the face of
C_{j}
such that
\check{C}^{j}=\check{C_{j}}\setminus\check{C}
.
We identify
A_{X}({\mathcal{F}}(C_{m}))
with
\mathbb{R}^{\check{C_{m}}}
as in the proof of Lemma
3.1
.

(i). For
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\Omega,\Omega^{\prime},
relatively compact open subsets of
U(X)
with
cl(\Omega)\subset\Omega^{\prime}

we define the following open subsets of
Z_{C}
:

Z_{j,\Omega,N}=\Omega M(]-N,N[)^{\check{C_{m}}\setminus\check{C}},Z^{\prime}_{j,\Omega^{\prime},N}=\Omega^{\prime}M(]-N-1,N+1[)^{\check{C_{j}}\setminus\check{C}},

If
g\in G
, the left translates by
g
of these sets will be denoted by a lower index
g
.
An element of
{Z^{\prime}}_{g,j,\tilde{\Omega},N}
will be denoted
gumz_{C^{m}}z_{C}
with
u\in\tilde{\Omega}
,
m\in M
,
z_{C^{j}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\check{C^{j}}}
.

(i) There exists
N\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and a finite covering of
Z_{C}
by a family of open sets
Z_{\mathfrak{c},j,\Omega,N}
for a finite set
\mathfrak{C}
of elements
\mathfrak{c}
of
G
. We will drop
\Omega
and
N
from the notation. We have
cl(Z_{\mathfrak{c},j})\subset Z^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{c},j}

(ii) Let
\varepsilon>0
and
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}
. Let
N(Z_{\mathfrak{c},j},\varepsilon)\subset\overline{X}
defined by:

N(Z_{\mathfrak{c},j},\varepsilon)=\mathfrak{c}\Omega M(]-N,N[)^{\check{C^{j}}}(]-\varepsilon,\varepsilon[)^{\check{C}}.

We define similarly
N(Z^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{c},j},\varepsilon)
.

An element of these sets will be denoted
\mathfrak{c}umz_{C^{j}}z_{C}
,
u\in\tilde{\Omega}
,
m\in M
,
z_{C^{j}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\check{C^{j}}}
,
z_{C}\in\mathbb{R}^{\check{C}}
.

(3.25)

The union
N(Z_{C},\varepsilon)
of the
N({Z}_{\mathfrak{c},j},\varepsilon)
, is a neighborhood of
Z_{C}
in
\overline{X}
. There exists
d>0
such that
N^{r}(Z_{C},d)\subset N(Z_{C},\varepsilon)
.

Proof.

(i) follows from the compacity of
Z_{C}
and from the fact that only a finite set of open sets given by the local structure theorem covers
\overline{X}
.

(ii) This follows from the fact that
N(Z_{C},\varepsilon)
is a neighborhood of
Z_{C}
in
\overline{X}
.

3.5 Lemma
.

(i) Let
\mathfrak{C}_{1}\subset\mathfrak{C}
and
\varepsilon>0
. A map
\phi
on
\cup_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}_{1},j}N(Z_{\mathfrak{c},j},\varepsilon)
to
N_{Z_{C}}(\overline{X})
is good if and only if for all
\mathfrak{c},j
with
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{c}umz_{C^{j}}z_{C}\in N(Z_{\mathfrak{c},j},\varepsilon)
, one has:

\phi(cumz_{C^{j}}z_{C})=(cumz_{C^{j}},z^{\prime}_{C}),

where the notation indicates that
(\mathfrak{c}umz_{C^{j}},z^{\prime}_{C})
is in the fiber in
N_{Z_{C}}(\overline{X})
, identified with
\mathbb{R}^{\check{C}}
, above
\mathfrak{c}umz_{C^{j}}
,
and
(z^{\prime}_{C})_{\check{\alpha}}=(z_{C})_{\check{\alpha}}(1+\varepsilon_{\check{\alpha}}(\mathfrak{c}umz_{C^{m}}z_{C}))
where
\varepsilon_{\check{\alpha}}
is
C^{\infty}
and vanishes for
z_{C}=0
.

(ii) One can replace in (i) the
Z_{\mathfrak{c},j}
by arbitrary open subsets of
Z^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{c},j}
containing
Z_{\mathfrak{c},j}
.

Proof.

(i) First, one sees easily that a map on
U_{1}\cup U^{\prime}_{1}
is good if its restrictions to
U_{1}
and
U^{\prime}_{1}
are good.
Then one reduces to prove the the claim when
\mathfrak{C}_{1}
is reduced to a singleton, where it can be checked in coordinates.

The proof of (ii) is similar.

3.6 Proposition
.

(i) There exists a good map
e_{C}
from
N^{r}(Z_{C},d_{C})
to
N_{Z_{C}}(\overline{X})
for some
d_{C}>0
.

(ii) We may and will take
d_{C}
such that
e_{C}
is a bijection onto its image.
The later is then an open neighborhood of
Z_{C}
in
N_{Z_{C}}(\overline{X})
.

(iii) Take
d_{0}=inf_{C\in{\mathcal{F}}}d_{C}
. Then
N^{r}(Z_{C},d_{0})\subset N^{r}(Z_{C^{\prime}},d_{0})
if
C^{\prime}\subset C
.

(iv) The good map
e_{C}
sends
N^{r}(Z_{C},d_{0})\cap X
into
X_{C}

Proof.

We use the notation of Lemma
3.4
.
To prove (i), in view of Lemma
3.4
(ii), it suffices to prove that there is a good map on
N(Z_{C},\varepsilon)
for some
\varepsilon>0
.

We label the family of open sets
Z_{\mathfrak{c},j}
(resp.
Z^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{c},j}
) by
1,\dots,r
.
We will prove by induction on
p
that there exist for
k=1,\dots p
, an open subset of
Z_{C}
,
Z_{k}^{p}
, with
cl(Z_{k})\subset Z_{k}^{p}\subset cl(Z_{k}^{p})\subset Z^{\prime}_{k}
,
\varepsilon_{p}>0
and a good map on:

\cup_{i=1,\dots,p}N(Z^{p}_{k},\varepsilon_{p}).

For
p=1
, we choose an arbitrary
\varepsilon_{1}
. As
cl(Z_{1})\subset Z^{\prime}_{1}
, one can choose
Z^{1}_{1}
with the required inclusion: if
Z^{\prime}_{1}=Z_{C}
, one takes
Z^{1}_{1}=Z_{C}
, otherwise one considers the distance
\alpha>0
of
cl(Z_{1})
to the complementary set of
Z^{\prime}_{1}
, which is compact and non empty. And one takes for
Z_{1}^{1}
the set of elements of
Z_{C}
which are at distance strictly greater than
\alpha/2
to this complementary set.

Finally if
Z_{1}=Z_{\mathfrak{c},m}
, one takes for
e_{1}
the restriction of
e_{P_{\mathfrak{c}},C}
to
Z_{1}^{1}
which is good as it follows from (
3.24
).

Suppose that the statement is true for
p
. If
p=r
(i) is proved. If
p<r
, let
U^{\prime}=\cup_{k=1,\dots,p}N(Z_{k}^{p},\varepsilon_{p})
. By the induction hypothesis, there is a good map
e
defined on
U^{\prime}
. As above one finds, for
k=0,\dots,p
,
Z_{k}^{p+1}
open subset of
Z_{C}
such that

cl(Z_{k})\subset Z_{k}^{p+1}\subset cl(Z_{k}^{p+1})\subset Z_{k}^{p}\subset Z^{\prime}_{k}.

Let
\varepsilon_{p+1}>0
with
\varepsilon_{p}>\varepsilon_{p+1}
. Let
U^{\prime\prime}=\cup_{k=1,\dots,p}N(Z_{k}^{p+1},\varepsilon_{p+1})
so that
cl(U^{\prime\prime})\subset U^{\prime}
. Similarly we find
Z_{p+1}^{p+1}
such that

cl(Z_{p+1})\subset Z_{p+1}^{p+1}\subset cl(Z_{p+1}^{p+1})\subset Z^{\prime}_{p+1}.

Let
V^{\prime\prime}=N(Z^{\prime}_{p+1},\varepsilon_{p+1}),V^{\prime}=N(Z_{p+1}^{p+1},\varepsilon_{p})
. One has
cl(V^{\prime\prime})\subset V^{\prime}
. If
Z_{p+1}=Z_{\mathfrak{c},j}
, the restriction of
e_{P_{\mathfrak{c}},C}
to
V^{\prime}
is a good map that we denote by
e^{\prime}
.
One checks easily:

V^{\prime}=(U^{\prime}\cap V^{\prime})\cup V^{\prime}\setminus cl(U^{\prime\prime}).

One uses a partition of unity for this open covering of
V^{\prime}
. One has
a
(resp.
b
) in
C^{\infty}(V^{\prime})
with support of
a
(resp.
b
) contained in
U^{\prime}\cap V^{\prime}
(resp.
V^{\prime}\setminus cl(U^{\prime\prime})
.

Using multiplication by a scalar on the fiber, one defines a map from
V^{\prime}
to
N_{Z_{C}}\overline{X}
by

\tilde{e}(x)=a(x)e(x)+b(x)e^{\prime}(x)),x\in V^{\prime}.

Using Lemma
3.5
(ii) for
e
and
e^{\prime}
, one sees that it is a good map. Moreover, as
b
vanishes on
U^{\prime}\cap V^{\prime\prime}
, one has:

\tilde{e}(x)=e(x),x\in V^{\prime\prime}\cap U^{\prime\prime}.

(3.26)

Let
e_{p+1}
be defined on
U^{\prime\prime}\cup V^{\prime\prime}
by:

e_{p+1}(x)=e_{p}(x),x\in U^{\prime\prime},e_{p+1}(x)=\tilde{e}(x),x\in V^{\prime\prime}

which is well defined due to (
3.26
).
Moreover
e_{p+1}
is good as it is the case for
e_{p}
and
\tilde{e}
.
Its properties allow to finish the proof of the induction step, as
U^{\prime\prime}\cup V^{\prime\prime}=\cup_{k=1,\dots,p+1}N(Z^{p+1}_{k},\varepsilon_{p+1})
. Then (i) follows from the final step for
p=r
.

(ii) From the definition one sees that the differential of
e_{C}
at any point of
Z_{C}
is invertible.
By shrinking
d_{C}
, one can assume that
e_{C}
is injective on
N^{r}(Z_{C},d_{C})
:
otherwise there would exists two sequences
(x_{n}),(x^{\prime}_{n})
in
\overline{X}
whose distance to
Z_{C}
tends to zero such that
x_{n}\not=x^{\prime}_{n}
and
e_{C}(x_{n})=e_{C}(x^{\prime}_{n})
. By extracting subsequences one can assume that
(x_{n})
(resp.
(x^{\prime}_{n})
) converges to
x
(resp.
x^{\prime}
) wich is in
Z_{C}
. Then
e_{C}(x)=e_{C}(x^{\prime})
. But one has also
e_{C}(x)=x,e_{C}(x^{\prime})=x^{\prime}
. Hence
x=x^{\prime}
. Using that
e_{C}
is a local diffeomorphism around
x
, one gets a contradiction which proves our claim. This is our final choice of
d_{C}
.

(iii) is clear.

(iv) follows from the definition of good maps.

3.7 Remark
.

The good map
e_{C}
will play the role of the (inverse of) the exponential mappings of
[
39
]
, section 4.3.

3.3
Open
P
-orbits, exponential mappings

From Lemma
3.2
(i) (a) , one has
\underline{P}x_{C}=\underline{U}(X)\underline{L}(X)x_{C}
. If
u\in\underline{U}(X),l\in\underline{L}(X)
and
ulx_{C}=x_{C}
, then
uly_{C}=y_{C}
. Then (
3.15
) implies
u=1
. From Lemma
3.2
(i) (c), one has
l\in\underline{L}(X)\cap\underline{H}
, and one has:

The map
\underline{U}(X)\times\underline{L}(X)/\underline{L}(X)\cap\underline{H}\to\underline{P}x_{C},(u,l)\mapsto ulx_{C}
is bijective.

(3.27)

Let us describe the open
P
-orbits in
X
. There is a subset
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
of the subgroup
A_{X,2}
of elements of order 2 of the real torus
A_{X}
which is a set of representatives of the open
P
-orbits in
X
(cf.
[
13
]
, Lemma 2.3).
We choose
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
such that it contains 1. In this identification of
A_{X}
with a subset of
X
,
1
corresponds to
x_{0}
.
Let us prove:

Let
w\in A_{X,2}
. The map
U(X)\times MA/((MA)\cap H)\to Pw,(u,ma)\mapsto umaw
is a bijection.

(3.28)

Let
\tilde{w}
a representative in
\underline{A}
of
w
. If
m\in M
and
a\in A
, one has
maw=w
if and only
ma\tilde{w}x_{0}=\tilde{w}x_{0}
, i.e.
\tilde{w}^{-1}ma\tilde{w}\in\underline{H}
. But
\tilde{w}\in\underline{A}
commutes with
MA
. Hence the last condition is fulfilled if and only if
ma\in(MA)\cap H
. Our claim follows from this and (
3.6
).

Let
{\mathcal{W}}:={\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}\cap G/H
, where
G/H
is viewed as a subset of
X
.
From
[
13
]
, section 5.1, one has:

The set
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}x_{C}
is a set of representatives of the open
P
-orbits in
X_{C}
for any
C\in{\mathcal{F}}
. If
w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
or
A_{X,2}
, we will denote
wx_{C}
by
w_{C}
.

(3.29)

As above one has:

If
w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
, the map
U(X)\times MA/((MA)\cap H)\to Pw_{C},(u,a)\mapsto uaw_{C}
is bijective.

(3.30)

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
(resp.
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}
) be the set of
G
-orbits in
X
(resp.
X_{C}
).
For
O\in{\mathcal{O}}
or
O\in{\mathcal{O}}_{C}
, let
{\mathcal{W}}_{O}={\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}\cap O
. We have partitions:

{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}=\bigsqcup_{O\in{\mathcal{O}}}{\mathcal{W}}_{O}=\bigsqcup_{O^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{C}}{\mathcal{W}}_{O^{\prime}}.

(3.31)

The second partition is a refinement of the first one, which follows from
[
13
]
Proposition 5.2.
This leads to the definition, for
O\in{\mathcal{O}}
, of a subset
{\mathcal{O}}_{C,O}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}
characterized by

{\mathcal{W}}_{O}=\bigsqcup_{O^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{C,O}}{\mathcal{W}}_{O^{\prime}}.

(3.32)

Let
{\mathcal{W}}^{G}_{\mathbb{R}}\subset{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
be a set of representatives of the
G
-orbits in
X
. We choose
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{G}
in such a way that it contains
1
.
We define similarly a set of representatives of the
G
-orbits in
X_{C}
,
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{G,C}
.

If
w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
, let
H_{C,w}
be the stabilizer in
G
of
w_{C}
.

The stabilizer in
P
of
w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
(resp.
w_{C}
)
is equal to
P\cap wHw^{-1}
(resp.
P\cap wH_{C}w^{-1}
). Hence these stabilizers are equal from Lemma
3.2
(i)
applied to
w\underline{H}w^{-1}
. Moreover
H_{C,w}=wH_{C}w^{-1}

(3.33)

We denote by
X_{C,P}
the union of the open
P
-orbits in
X_{C}
.
Let
C
be an elements of
{\mathcal{F}}
. Then, using (
3.33
), the map
e_{P,C}
from AA (
3.23
) induces a bijective map
\exp_{P,C}X_{P}\to X_{C,P}
, denoted in the same way, such that:

e_{P,C}(pw)=pw_{C},p\in P,w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}.

(3.34)

From
[
24
]
(4.1):

One may and will choose the invariant measure on
X_{C}
such that it is given on
U(X)(MA/((MA)\cap H))w_{C}
by the image of the measure
a^{-2\rho}dudmda
.

(3.35)

Hence:

The exponential mappings
\exp_{P,C}
are measure preserving.

(3.36)

The following is immediate:

Let
g\in G
and
P_{g}=gPg^{-1}
. Then
X_{P_{g}}=gX_{P}
and

e_{P_{g},C}(gx)=ge_{P,C}(x),x\in X_{P}.

(3.37)

3.4
Special coverings of
X

For
\varepsilon>0
and
C\in{\mathcal{F}}
, let us define the subsets of
A_{C}
:

A_{C}^{-}(\varepsilon):=\{j^{\prime}_{C}((z_{\check{\alpha}}))|z_{\check{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{R}^{*},|z_{\check{\alpha}}|<\varepsilon,\check{\alpha}\in\check{C}\}.

(3.38)

One has that
A_{C}^{-}(\varepsilon)\subset A_{C,2}\exp\mathfrak{a}_{X}^{-}
, where
A_{C,2}
is the subgroup of
A_{C}
of elements of order 2.

Let
C_{0}
be an element in
{\mathcal{F}}
of maximal dimension containing
C
. Let
C^{c}\in{\mathcal{F}}
be the element of
{\mathcal{F}}
with
\check{C^{c}}=\check{C}_{0}\setminus\check{C}
.

(3.39)

Then the torus
\underline{A}_{C^{c}}
, which might depend on
C_{0}
, satisfies

\underline{A}_{X}=\underline{A}_{C^{c}}\underline{A}_{C}

and
A_{C^{c}}A_{C}
is of finite index in
A_{X}
.

We choose
B^{{}^{\prime}C}\subset B^{C}
two relatively compact open neighborhoods of 1 in
A_{C^{c}}^{0}
such that
clB^{{}^{\prime}C}\subset B^{C}.

In particular
B^{C}A^{-}_{C}(\varepsilon)
is open in
A_{X}
.

(3.40)

Let
V^{\prime}\subset V
be two open relatively compact neighborhoods of 1 in
U(X)
such that
clV^{\prime}\subset V.

Let us define the following subsets of
X
:

U_{C,\varepsilon}:=VMB^{C}A_{C}^{-}(\varepsilon){\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}},U^{\prime}_{C,\varepsilon}:=V^{\prime}MB^{{}^{\prime}C}A_{C}^{-}(\varepsilon/2){\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}

(3.41)

These sets are open in
X_{P}
.

Let us study the open
P
-orbits in
Y_{C}
. From
[
13
]
, Remark 3.6 (b),
the open
P
-orbits of
Y_{C}
are the
P
-orbits of the
y_{w,C}=wy_{C}
,
w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
, but two different
w
might lead to the same orbit.

Let
\underline{x}
be an element of the relative interior
C^{0}
of
C
in
\mathfrak{a}_{C}
. From (
3.1
), we have
y_{C}=lim_{t\to+\infty}\exp(t\underline{x})x_{0}
. Recall our identification of
A_{X}
with a subset of
X
(cf. the beginning of the preceding subsection). With this identification, for
w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
,
w\exp(t\underline{x})x_{0}=\exp(t\underline{x})w
and:

y_{w,C}=lim_{t\to+\infty}\exp(t\underline{x})w,w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}},\underline{x}\in C^{0}.

(3.42)

We define
{\mathcal{W}}_{Y_{C}}:=\{y_{w,C}|w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}\}
. From LST, one sees that
G{\mathcal{W}}_{Y_{C}}=Y_{C}
.
Let

\Omega_{C}:=VMB^{C},\Omega^{\prime}_{C}:=V^{\prime}MB^{{}^{\prime}C}

U_{C}:=\Omega_{C}{\mathcal{W}}_{Y_{C}},U^{\prime}_{C}:=\Omega^{\prime}_{C}{\mathcal{W}}_{Y_{C}}.

(3.43)

The sets
U_{C},U^{\prime}_{C}
are open neighborhoods of
{\mathcal{W}}_{Y_{C}}
in
Y_{C}
.

Let us show:

The closure of
U^{\prime}_{C,\varepsilon}
in
\overline{X}
contains
U^{\prime}_{C}
and the closure of
U^{\prime}_{C,\varepsilon}
in
X
is contained in
U_{C,\varepsilon}
.

(3.44)

The first claim follows from (
3.42
). Let
x
be in the closure of
U^{\prime}_{C,\varepsilon}
in
X
. By the relative compactness of
V^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime C}
, the compactness of
M
and extraction of subsequences, one can assume that
x
is the limit of a sequence
(v_{n}m_{n}b_{n}a_{n}w)
where
w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
,
(v_{n})\subset V^{\prime}
(resp.
(m_{n})\subset M
, resp.
(b_{n})\subset B^{\prime C}
) is convergent and has limit in
V
(resp.
M
, resp.
B^{C}
) and
(a_{n})\subset A_{C}^{-}(\varepsilon/2)
. Hence
(a_{n}w)
is convergent in
X
and has its limit in
A_{C}^{-}(\varepsilon)w
, as the closure of
A_{C}^{-}(\varepsilon/2)
in
X
is easily seen to be contained in
A_{C}^{-}(\varepsilon)
. Our claim on the closure of
U^{\prime}_{C,\varepsilon}
follows.

If
g\in G
, one defines:
U_{C,g,\varepsilon}:=gU_{C,\varepsilon}
and similarly
U^{\prime}_{C,g,\varepsilon},U_{C,g},U^{\prime}_{C,g},U^{\prime}_{C,g}
.
They are defined like
U_{C,\varepsilon}
, etc., by using
P_{g}=gPg^{-1}
and
x_{g}=gx_{0}
instead of
P
and
x_{0}
.

(3.45)

The sets
U^{\prime}_{C,g,\varepsilon}
(resp.
U_{C,g,\varepsilon}
) will be said
\varepsilon
-substandard (resp.
\varepsilon
-standard) of type
C
. Similarly the translates of
U^{\prime}_{C}
(resp.
U_{C}
) will be said substandard (resp. standard) of type
C
. One has:

Let
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R},C}:=\{w_{C}|w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}\}
. The manifold
X_{C}
has a
G
-invariant measure such that its restriction to
\Omega_{C}MA_{C}{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R},C}
is the image of the measure
a_{C}^{-2\rho}d\omega da_{C}dw_{C}
where
d\omega
is a bounded measure on
\Omega_{C}
,
da_{C}
is a Haar measure on
A_{C}
and
dw_{C}
is the counting measure on
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R},C}
.

(3.46)

3.8 Lemma
.

(i) For any choice of the
\varepsilon_{C}>0,C\in{\mathcal{F}}
, there exists a covering of
X
by a finite family of
\varepsilon_{C}
-standard open subsets
U_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}}
,
C\in{\mathcal{F}},\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}
where for
C\in{\mathcal{F}}
,
\mathfrak{C}
is a finite subset of
G
.

(ii) It is possible to reduce
\varepsilon_{C}
for
C\in{\mathcal{F}}
with
\check{C}
of a given cardinal without changing
\varepsilon_{C}^{\prime}
for
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}
with
Card(\check{C^{\prime}})>Card(\check{C})
and without changing
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
for
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}
with
Card(\check{C^{\prime}})\geq Card(\check{C})
. This operation will be called ”reduction of
\varepsilon_{C}
” .

Proof.

(i) For this, we construct families of
\varepsilon_{C}
-substandard sets
U^{\prime}_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}},\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}
, by downward induction on
Card\check{C}
, such that:

The closure in
\overline{X}
of

\cup_{C\subset C^{\prime},\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}}U^{\prime}_{C^{\prime},\mathfrak{c}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{C^{\prime}}}
contains a neighborhood of
Z_{C}
in
\overline{X}
.

(3.47)

If
C=C_{0}
is an element of
{\mathcal{F}}
of maximal dimension, then
Y_{C_{0}}=Z_{C_{0}}
is compact. As
{\mathcal{W}}_{Y_{C_{0}}}\subset U^{\prime}_{C_{0}}
contains a set of representatives of the
G
-orbits in
Y_{C_{0}}
, by using translates, one can find a finite family of relatively compact open sets
U^{\prime}_{C_{0},\mathfrak{c}_{0}}=\mathfrak{c}_{0}U^{\prime}_{C_{0}},\mathfrak{c}_{0}\in\mathfrak{C}_{0}\subset G
, covering
Y_{C_{0}}
.

Let
\varepsilon_{C_{0}}>0
and one defines the
\varepsilon_{C_{0}}
-substandard sets
U^{\prime}_{C_{0},\mathfrak{c}_{0},\varepsilon_{C_{0}}}=\mathfrak{c}_{0}U^{\prime}_{C_{0},\varepsilon_{C_{0}}}
. The closure of
(]-\varepsilon_{C_{0}}/2[\cup]0,\varepsilon_{C_{0}}/2[)^{\check{C_{0}}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{\check{C_{0}}}
contains
B_{C_{0}}(\varepsilon_{C_{0}}/2):=(]-\varepsilon_{C_{0}}/2,\varepsilon_{C_{0}}/2[)^{\check{C_{0}}}
. Then, using the identification of an open subset of
\overline{A}_{X}
with
\mathbb{R}^{\check{C_{0}}}
(cf. the proof of Lemma
3.1
) and (
3.13
), one sees that the closure in
\overline{X}
of
U^{\prime}_{C_{0},\mathfrak{c}_{0},\varepsilon_{C_{0}}}
contains, with the notation of (
3.43
) the neighborhood
\mathfrak{c}_{0}V^{\prime}Mj^{\prime}_{C_{0}}(B_{C_{0}}(\varepsilon_{C_{0}}/2)){\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
in
\overline{X}
of
U^{\prime}_{C_{0},\mathfrak{c}_{0}}
. This achieves to prove that the
U^{\prime}_{C_{0},\mathfrak{c}_{0},\varepsilon_{C_{0}}},\mathfrak{c}_{0}\in\mathfrak{C}_{0}
satisfy (
3.47
).

Suppose that the construction has been done for all
C\in{\mathcal{F}}
with
\check{C}
of cardinal strictly greater than
n
. Let
C\in{\mathcal{F}}
with
\check{C}
of cardinal
n
. Let
{\mathcal{V}}
be an open neighborhood in
\overline{X}
of the union of the
Z_{C^{\prime}}
,
C\subsetneq C^{\prime}
which is contained in the closure in
\overline{X}
of

\cup_{C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}},C\subsetneq C^{\prime},\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}}U_{C^{\prime},\mathfrak{c}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{C^{\prime}}}
: its existence is given by the induction hypothesis applied to each
C^{\prime}
with
C\subsetneq C^{\prime}
. Let
F=\{x\in Z_{C},x\notin{\mathcal{V}}\}
, which is compact as
Z_{C}
is compact. Moreover it is contained in
Y_{C}
from the definition of
{\mathcal{V}}
and
F
, as
Z_{C}=Y_{C}\cup_{C\subsetneq C^{\prime}}Z_{C^{\prime}}
. One can find a finite covering of
F
by substandard subsets of
Y_{C}
,
U^{\prime}_{C,\mathfrak{c}},\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}
, and choose
\varepsilon_{C}
.

Let us show that the
U^{\prime}_{C^{\prime},\mathfrak{c}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{C^{\prime}}}
,
C\subset C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}},\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
satisfy (
3.47
). Recall our choice of
C_{0}
and
C^{c}
in (
3.39
). As above one has an identification of an open subset of
\overline{A}_{X}
with
\mathbb{R}^{\check{C_{0}}}
. One looks to the closure of
U^{\prime}_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}}
. Let us show that it contains an open neighborhood of
U^{\prime}_{C,\mathfrak{c}}
in
\overline{X}
using (
3.13
): it follows from the fact that
the closure of
(]-\varepsilon_{C}/2,0[\cup]0,\varepsilon_{C}/2[)^{\check{C}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{\check{C}}
contains
B_{C}(\varepsilon_{C}/2):=(]-\varepsilon_{C}/2,\varepsilon_{C}/2[)^{\check{C}}
and that
B^{\prime C}
is open in
A^{0}_{C^{c}}
. Then the closure of
U^{\prime}_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}}
in
\overline{X}
contains the neighborhood
\mathfrak{c}V^{\prime}MB^{{}^{\prime}C}j^{\prime}_{C}(B_{C}(\varepsilon_{C})){\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
in
\overline{X}
of
U^{\prime}_{C,\mathfrak{c}}
. Together with the property of
{\mathcal{V}}
, this implies (
3.47
).

From (
3.47
) for
C=\{0\}
, one sees that the closure of
\cup_{C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}},\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}}U^{\prime}_{C^{\prime},\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C^{\prime}}}
is a covering of
\overline{X}
.

If
x\in X
, by what we have just proved,
x
is in the closure of some
U^{\prime}_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}}
. Then by the second part of (
3.44
), one has:

\cup_{C\in{\mathcal{F}},\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}}U_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}}=X.

This achieves to prove (i).

(ii) follows by inspection of the proof of the inductive step in (i).

Let us prove:

Let
g\in G
. There exists
\varepsilon
such that
U_{C,g,\varepsilon}\subset N^{r}(Z_{C},d_{0})
.

(3.48)

If it was not true one would find a sequence
(x_{n})\in U_{C,g,1/n}
not in
N^{r}(Z_{C},d_{0})
. A subsequence
(y_{n})
of
(x_{n})
will converge to a point of
Z_{C}
. Then for
n
large
y_{n}
will be in
N^{r}(Z_{C},d_{0})
. A contradiction which proves our claim.

3.9 Lemma
.

There exists arbitrary small
\varepsilon_{C}
and a covering as in the previous lemma, such that
U_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}}\subset N^{r}(Z_{C},d_{0})
for all
C\in{\mathcal{F}}
and
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}
. Such a covering will be called a special covering.

Proof.

One starts with a covering as in the previous Lemma and use downward induction on
Card(\check{C})
as in the previous proof, using at each step ”reduction of
\varepsilon_{C}
” and (
3.48
).

4
Plancherel formula and temperedness

4.1
Review of the abstract Plancherel formula

Here we borrow part of
[
13
]
, section 6.3. We denote by
\hat{G}
the unitary dual of
G
and pick up for every equivalence class
[\pi]
a
representative
(\pi,{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi})
, i.e.
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}
is a Hilbert space and
\pi:G\to U({\mathcal{H}}_{\pi})
is an
irreducible continuous unitary representation in the equivalence class of
[\pi]
. Let
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{\infty}
be the space of
C^{\infty}
-vectors of
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}
. Let the notation be as in the previous section. We define

{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{-\infty X}:=Hom_{G}({\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{\infty},C^{\infty}(X)),{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{-\infty O}=Hom_{G}({\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{\infty},C^{\infty}(O)),O\in{\mathcal{O}}.

The space
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{-\infty X}
is finite dimensional since
X
is real spherical: it follows from

[
28
]
,
[
31
]
.

Notice that

{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{-\infty G/H}:=Hom({\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{\infty},C^{\infty}(G/H))
is a direct summand of
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{-\infty X}.

More precisely

{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{-\infty X}=\oplus_{O\in{\mathcal{O}}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{-\infty O}.

(4.1)

If
v\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{\infty}
,
\eta\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{-\infty X}
, let us define
m_{v,\eta}\in C^{\infty}(X)
by:

m_{v,\eta}=\eta(v).

Let
u\mapsto m_{u}
be the linear map
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{\infty}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{-\infty X}\to C^{\infty}(X)
, such that
m_{v\otimes\eta}=m_{v,\eta}
for
v\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{\infty}
,
\eta\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{-\infty X}
.
The abstract Plancherel Theorem for the unimodular real spherical space
X

asserts the following (see
[
36
]
,
[
14
]
, or
[
32
, Section 8]
) :
there exists a Borel measure
\mu

on
\hat{G}
and for
every
[\pi]\in\hat{G}
a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}\subset{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{-\infty X}
,
depending measurably on
[\pi]

such that the Fourier transform

\alpha_{S}:C_{c}^{\infty}(X)\to\int_{\hat{G}}^{\oplus}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}\ d\mu(\pi),

f\mapsto\alpha_{S}(f)=(\alpha_{\pi}(f))_{\pi\in\hat{G}},

extends to a unitary
G
-isomorphism,
\overline{\alpha}_{S}
from
L^{2}(X)
onto

\int_{\hat{G}}^{\oplus}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}d\mu(\pi)
, where
\alpha_{\pi}
is defined as follows: for
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
,
\alpha_{\pi}(f)\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
is characterized by:

(\alpha_{\pi}(f),v\otimes\eta)=\int_{X}f(x)\overline{m_{v,\eta}(x)}dx,v\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{\infty},\eta\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}.

(4.2)

Moreover the measure class of
\mu
is uniquely determined by
X
and we call
\mu
a
Plancherel measure

for
X
. We may and will assume that
\mu
is a probability measure. This will be a general convention for spectral measures used in the following.
Unique are also the
multiplicity spaces

{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}\subset{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{-\infty X}
, for almost all
\pi
,
together with their inner products up to positive scalar factor.

Also one has
