\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(X)}=\int_{\hat{G}}\|\alpha_{\pi}(f)\|^{2}\ d\mu(\pi),f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(X).

(4.3)

We introduce the adjoint of
\alpha_{\pi}
,
\beta_{\pi}:{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}\to{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(X)
and the adjoint of
\overline{\alpha_{S}}
,
\beta_{S}:\int_{\hat{G}}^{\oplus}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}\ d\mu(\pi)\to L^{2}(X)
. Notice that one deduces from (
4.2
):

\beta_{\pi}(v\otimes\eta)=m_{v,\eta},v\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\infty}_{\pi},\eta\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}.

(4.4)

One will also denote
\beta_{S}
simply by
\beta
. Later we will see that the image of
\beta_{\pi}
is in a proper functional subspace of
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}(X)
.
Let us use notation of Lemma
3.2
to define
A^{0}_{S}
and its right action on
X
denoted by a dot.
For almost all
\pi
,
A^{0}_{S}
acts unitarily on
{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
in such a way that it is compatible with the normalized action of
A^{0}_{S}
on
C^{\infty}(X)
denoted by a dot:

a.f(x):=a^{-\rho}f(a.x),x\in X,a\in A^{0}_{S}.

(4.5)

Hence:

For
\mu
-almost all
\pi
,
\alpha_{\pi}
and
\beta_{\pi}
, as well as
\alpha_{S}
and
\beta_{S}
, are
A^{0}_{S}
-maps and the above Plancherel formula for the action of
G
is also a Plancherel decomposition for the unitary representation of
G\times A^{0}_{S}
on
L^{2}(X)
.

(4.6)

4.1 Remark
.

Looking to the Plancherel formula for the
G
-orbits in
X
, one sees that one has an orthogonal decomposition

{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}=\oplus_{O\in{\mathcal{O}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{O}_{\pi}
, where
{\mathcal{M}}^{O}_{\pi}={\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{-\infty O}
.
One deduces the Plancherel decomposition for
O\subset X
from the one for
X
by simply replacing
{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}\subset{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{-\infty X}
by
{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}^{O}
.

4.2
Temperedness

If
(\pi,E)
is a continuous Banach representation of
G
, with defining norm
p
, there are (see
[
13
]
section 6.1) the Sobolev norms
p_{k}

on the space
E^{\infty}
of smooth vectors of
E
. We define the Laplace operator
\Delta
as
-\sum_{i}\underline{x}_{i}^{2}
for a suitable basis
\{\underline{x}_{1},\ldots,\underline{x}_{n}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
. More precisely
we take the basis such that the Laplace element
\Delta

satisfies

\Delta=-{\mathcal{C}}_{G}+2{\mathcal{C}}_{K}

with
{\mathcal{C}}_{G}
and
{\mathcal{C}}_{K}
appropriate Casimir elements (unique if
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{k}
are semisimple). For
R\in\mathbb{R}
, let
\Delta_{R}=\Delta+R^{2}
.
We define
R_{E}
as in l.c., Lemma 6.1 and fix
R>R_{E}
. We define for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
:

{}^{\Delta}p_{2k}(v)=p(\Delta_{R}^{k}v),v\in E^{\infty}.

If
E
is unitary, we can take
R=1
.
From l.c. (6.4), there exists a constant
C_{k}
, independent of
\pi
and
E
, but only on
R_{E}
and
R
, such that:

p_{2k}(v)\leq C_{k}\>^{\Delta}p_{2k+n^{*}}(v),v\in E^{\infty}

(4.7)

with
n^{*}=min\{k\in 2\mathbb{N}|1+\mathrm{dim}\,G\leq k\}
.

We have the notions of weight and equivalence of weights on a homogenous space from
[
5
]
, which extends to spaces with finitely many orbits like
X
. In particular if
w
is a weight on the space
X
with an action of
G
, it is a strictly positive function on
X
and if
\Omega
is a compact subset of
G
, there exist
C,C^{\prime}>0
with:

Cw(x)\leq w(gx)\leq C^{\prime}w(x),x\in X,g\in\Omega.

(4.8)

One has the volume weight
\mathbf{v}
attached to a ball
B
(cf. e.g.
[
24
]
, section 4):

\mathbf{v}(x)=vol_{X}(Bx).

Its equivalence class does not depend on the ball.

Let
A_{X,2}
the subgroup of elements of
A_{X}
which are of square 1. By
[
32
]
, Theorem 9.2,
one has the polar decomposition:

X=\Omega A_{X}^{-}A_{X,2},

(4.9)

where
\Omega
is a compact subset of
G
and where
A_{X}^{-}
denotes here
\{\exp X|X\in\mathfrak{a}_{X},\alpha(X)\leq 0,\alpha\in S\}
.

Let us define as in
[
24
]
, Proposition 3.4,
\mathbf{w}
by

\mathbf{w}(x):=\sup_{a\in A_{X}^{-}\textrm{ s.t.}\,x\in\Omega aA_{X,2}}\|\log(a)\|,x\in X,

(4.10)

Then, by l.c.,
\mathbf{w}
is a weight function, at least if
\Omega
is taken large enough, that we will take in the sequel.
From
[
32
]
, Proposition 9.4, one has for
N>\mathrm{dim}\,\mathfrak{a}_{X}
:

\int_{X}(1+\mathbf{w}(x))^{-N}\mathbf{v}(x)^{-1}dx<+\infty.

(4.11)

The following is borrowed from
[
13
]
, section 6.4. The following norms on
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
are attached to
a parameter
N\in\mathbb{R}
:

q_{N}(f):=\sup_{x\in X}|f(x)|\,\mathbf{v}(x)^{\frac{1}{2}}(1+\mathbf{w}(x))^{N},

p_{N}(f):=\left(\int_{X}|f(x)|^{2}(1+\mathbf{w}(x))^{N}\ dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(4.12)

For
N,k\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
L_{N,k}^{2}(X)
the completion of
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
with respect to

p_{N,k}
. The group
G
acts continuouly on
L^{2}_{-N}(X)
which is isometric to the continuous dual of
L^{2}_{N}(X)
.
For
N>0
, let
C_{temp,N}^{\infty}(X)
be the space of elements of
C^{\infty}(X)
for which the seminorms
p_{-N,k}
, or equivalently
q_{-N,k}
are finite for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, as these two families define the same topology (cf. l.c.). The elements of this space are said
N
-tempered. Then one has:

C_{temp,N}^{\infty}(X)
is the space of
C^{\infty}
-vectors of
L^{2}_{-N}(X)
.

(4.13)

For
k>{\mathrm{dim}\,G/2}
, we recall the inequality (cf. l.c., (6.10)):

q_{N}(f)\leq Cp_{N,k}(f),f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(X)

(4.14)

for a constant
C
only depending on
k
and
N
. This leads to the definition of
L^{2}_{N,-k}
as the continuous dual of
L^{2}_{-N,k}
with

p_{-N,-k}(f):=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\in L_{N,k}^{2}(X)\\
p_{N,k}(\phi)\leq 1\end{subarray}}\left|\int_{X}f(x)\phi(x)\ dx\right|\,.

(4.15)

We have the following slight generalization of Lemma 6.6 of
[
13
]
, whose proof extends easily since the proof of Lemma 6.2 of l.c. applies to finite multiples of unitary irreducible representations.

4.2 Lemma
.

Let
(\pi,{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi})
be a continuous unitary irreducible representation of
G
. Let

{\mathcal{M}}\subset{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{-\infty X}
be a finite dimensional space of
N
-tempered morphisms, i.e. which are continuous
G
-equivariant maps from
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{\infty}
to
C^{\infty}_{N,temp}(X)
. In particular, for all
\eta\in{\mathcal{M}}
,
p_{-N}(m_{v,\eta})<\infty
. Then for each
2k^{\prime}>n^{*}=min\{p\in 2\mathbb{N}|1+\mathrm{dim}\,G\leq p\}
,
there exists a constant
C=C_{k^{\prime}}>0
, independent of
\pi
and
{\mathcal{M}}
, such that:

p_{-N}(m_{u})\leq Cp_{-N,-2k^{\prime}+n^{*}}(m_{\Delta_{1}^{k^{\prime}}u}),u\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{\infty}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}.

(4.16)

We define:

N_{X}:=2\mathrm{dim}\,\mathfrak{a}_{X}+1,k_{X}:={\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{dim}\,\mathfrak{g}}.

(4.17)

Then (see
[
13
]
, section 6.6) for all integers
N\geq N_{X}
,
k>k_{X}
, one has:

for
\mu
-almost all

\pi\in\hat{G}
, the
\pi
-Fourier transform

\alpha_{\pi}:C_{c}^{\infty}(X)\to{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}

extends continuously to a map
\alpha_{\pi,N,k}:L_{N,k}^{2}(X)\to{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
.

(4.18)

Moreover the corresponding inclusion:

L_{N,k}^{2}(X)\to\int_{\hat{G}}^{\oplus}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}d\mu(\pi)

(4.19)

is Hilbert-Schmidt (in the sequel HS for short).

We will also consider the adjoint of
\alpha_{\pi,N,k}
:

\beta_{\pi,N,k}:{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}\to L^{2}_{-N,-k}(X).

(4.20)

The fact that (
4.19
) is HS then
translates into the
a priori bound

\int_{\hat{G}}\|\alpha_{\pi,N,k}\|^{2}_{HS}\ d\mu(\pi)<\infty.

(4.21)

One has

\|\beta_{\pi\,N,k}\|=\|\alpha_{\pi\,N,k}\|\leq\|\alpha_{\pi,N,k}\|_{HS}=\|\beta_{\pi,N,k}\|_{HS}.

In the sequel
N
will denote an integer greater or equal to
N_{X}
. We will take
k=2\mathrm{dim}\,\mathfrak{g}
and we will write
\alpha_{\pi},\beta_{\pi}
instead of

\alpha_{\pi,N,k},\beta_{\pi,N,k}
.

(4.22)

Then:

For
\mu
-almost all
\pi\in\hat{G}
, for all
u\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
,

p_{-N,-k}(\beta_{\pi}(u))\leq\|\beta_{\pi}\|\|u\|=\|\alpha_{\pi}\|\|u\|.

(4.23)

From
[
5
]
, before Lemma 3.1, one will deduce:

For
\mu
-almost all
\pi\in{\hat{G}}
and all
u\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\infty}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
,
m_{u}
is in
{L^{2}_{-N}(X)}^{\infty}=C^{\infty}_{temp,N}(X)
(cf. (
4.13
)) and the map
u\mapsto m_{u}
is continuous from
{\mathcal{H}}^{\infty}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
to
C^{\infty}_{temp,N}(X)
. In particular, all
\eta\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
are
N
-tempered.

(4.24)

The map
\beta_{\pi}
is a continuous intertwining operator between
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
and
L^{2}_{-N}(X)
. Our claim follows by going to
C^{\infty}
-vectors as, from (
4.4
), one has
\beta_{\pi}(u)=m_{u}
for
u\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\infty}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
.

Thus one can apply Lemma
4.2
to the
m_{u}
,
u\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{\infty}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
for
\mu
-almost all
\pi
.
One deduces from Lemma
4.2
, where we take
k^{\prime}
such that
2k^{\prime}=n^{*}+k
(recall that
k=2\mathrm{dim}\,\mathfrak{g}
and
n^{*}
is even), and from (
4.23
) that:

There exists
C>0
and
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for
\mu
-almost all
\pi
, one has:

p_{-N}(\beta_{\pi}(u))\leq C\|\alpha_{\pi}\|\|\Delta_{1}^{k^{\prime}}u\|,u\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{\infty}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}.

(4.25)

5
Constant term

5.1
First properties of the constant term

Let
C\in{\mathcal{F}}
. We have similar definitions and results for
X_{C}
like in the preceding section for
X
.

Let
Z(\mathfrak{g})
be the center of the enveloping algebra
U(\mathfrak{g})
of
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Recall (cf.
[
8
]
, section 4.2) that
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X)
is the space of
Z(\mathfrak{g})
-finite elements of
C_{temp,N}^{\infty}(X)
. This space is equipped with the topology given by the seminorms of
C_{temp,N}^{\infty}(X)
. Let
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp}(X)
(resp.
C^{\infty}_{temp}(X)
) be the union of the
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X)
(resp.
C^{\infty}_{temp,N}(X)
),
N\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
. We have similar definitions for
X_{C}
.
Recall the following characteristic property of the constant term.

5.1 Theorem
.

(cf.
[
8
]
, Theorem 6.9). Let
C\in{\mathcal{F}}
.

(i) Let
f\in{\mathcal{A}}_{temp}(X)
. Then there exists a unique element
f_{C}
of
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp}(X_{C})
such that for all
\underline{x}
in the relative interior
C^{0}
of
C
in
\mathfrak{a}_{C}
and
w\in A_{X,2}
one has:

\lim_{t\to+\infty}e^{-t\rho(\underline{x})}\left(f(g\exp(t\underline{x})w))-f_{C}(g\exp(t\underline{x})w_{C})\right)=0,g\in G.

(ii) If
f
is supported on
O\in{\mathcal{O}}
,
f_{C}
is supported on
\cup_{O^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{C,O}}O^{\prime}.

(iii) The map
f\mapsto f_{C}
is
G
and
A^{0}_{C}
-equivariant.

Proof.

One has to define
f_{C}
on each
G
-orbit of
X_{C}
. When this orbit is
G/H_{C}
identified with
G/H_{I}
, one defines
f_{C}
on
G/H_{C}
as the map
(f_{|G/H})_{I}
of l.c.. It has the right property for
w\in{\mathcal{W}}
. From l.c., Theorem 6.9, one can change
{\mathcal{W}}
without changing
f_{C}
. Hence it has the right property for
w\in A_{X,2}\cap G/H
. Let
w\in{\mathcal{W}}^{G,C}_{\mathbb{R}}
. To define
f_{C}
on
Gw_{C}
, one proceeds as above by replacing
G/H
by
Gw\subset X
,
H
by
wHw^{-1}
and
H_{C}
by
wH_{C}w^{-1}
. Then
f_{C}
satisfies the asymptotic property of (i). Uniqueness follows from the properties of exponential polynomials as in the proof of unicity in Lemma 6.5 of
[
8
]
.

(ii) is immediate from the definitions.

(iii) follows from the uniqueness property in (i).

5.2 Remark
.

In view of the theorem, as far as the constant term is concerned, one can change the base point by any element of
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
or
A_{X,2}.

These will be the only changes of base point that we will do. Although this is not needed, in section
11
, we will prove that the constant term map does not depend on any choice. The proof was suggested a long time ago by Y. Sakellaridis.

5.2
Uniform estimates

5.3 Definition
.

Let
\mathfrak{t}
be a Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{m}
. The choice of
\mathfrak{t}
will be made more precise later. Then
\mathfrak{j}:=\mathfrak{t}\oplus\mathfrak{a}
is a Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
. Let
\underline{W}
be the Weyl group of the system of roots of
\mathfrak{j}_{\mathbb{C}}
in
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}
.
If
\lambda\in\mathfrak{j}_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}

or
\lambda\in\mathfrak{j}_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}/{\underline{W}}
, let
\chi_{\lambda}
be the character of
Z(\mathfrak{g})
corresponding to
\lambda
via the Harish-Chandra homomorphism which identifies
Z(\mathfrak{g})
with the polynomial algebra
S(\mathfrak{j}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\underline{W}}
.
If
\chi
is a character of
Z(\mathfrak{g})
of the form
\chi_{\lambda}
, we will say that
\lambda\in\mathfrak{j}_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
is an Harish-Chandra parameter (sometimes abreviated to parameter) of
\chi
. The set of Harish-Chandra parameters of
\chi
is a
\underline{W}
-orbit.
Let
\mathfrak{j}_{\mathbb{R}}=i\mathfrak{t}+\mathfrak{a}
. An element of
\mathfrak{j}_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
is said real (resp.imaginary) if it is real (resp. imaginary) on
\mathfrak{j}_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X,\lambda)
be
the space of elements
f\in{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X)
such that:

L_{u}f=\chi_{\lambda}(u)f,u\in Z(\mathfrak{g}).

One will say that
f
is
\lambda
-eigen for
Z(\mathfrak{g})
or
Z(\mathfrak{g})
-eigen for the eigenvalue (of parameter)
\lambda
.
Let
I
be a subset of
S
. We denote by
N_{I}
the integer
N_{1}
, which depends on
I
, defined in
[
8
]
, Theorem 8.10.
In view of l.c., Theorem 8.10, and the definition of
f_{C}
in the proof of Theorem
5.1
, one has

5.4 Theorem
.

(Constant term’s uniform approximation)

(i) Let
N\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
C\in{\mathcal{F}}
. Let
{\mathcal{C}}
be a compact subset of
C^{0}+\mathfrak{a}_{S}\subset\mathfrak{a}_{I(C)}^{--}
and let
\Omega
be a compact subset of
G
. Then there exist
\varepsilon>0
and a continuous seminorm
p
on
C_{temp,N}^{\infty}(X)
such that, for all
\lambda\in\mathfrak{j}^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}/{\underline{W}}
,
\underline{x}\in{\mathcal{C}}
,
f\in{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X,\lambda)
and
w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
:

(a_{X}\exp(t\underline{x}))^{-\rho}|f(ga_{X}\exp(t\underline{x})w)-f_{C}(ga_{X}\exp(t\underline{x})w_{C})|\leq e^{-\varepsilon t}(1+\|\log a_{X}\|)^{N}p(f),

a_{X}\in A_{X}^{-},\underline{x}\in{\mathcal{C}},g\in\Omega,t\geq 0.

(ii) Let
I=I(C)
.
Let
q
be a continuous seminorm on
C_{temp,N+N_{I}}^{\infty}(X_{C})
. Then there exists a continuous seminorm
p
on
C_{temp,N}^{\infty}(X)
such that:

q(f_{C})\leq p(f),\qquad f\in{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X,\lambda),\lambda\in\mathfrak{j}_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}/{\underline{W}}\,.

One is also interested in the decomposition of
f_{C}
in
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
-generalized eigenvectors which exists by definition of
f_{C}
or
f_{I}
(cf.
[
8
]
, (6.1)). From
[
8
]
, (8.7), the possible eigenvalues
are limited and the norm of the spectral projections are bounded by a polynomial in
\|\lambda\|
(cf.
[
8
]
, Proposition 8.6).

We need a Lemma:

5.5 Lemma
.

There exist some elements
z_{i},i=1,\dots,n,
of
Z(\mathfrak{g})
, and
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that

p(\lambda):=\sum_{i=1,\dots,n}|z_{i}(\lambda)|\approx\|\lambda\|^{N_{0}},\lambda\in\mathfrak{j}^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}.

Proof.

Let
u_{1},\dots u_{n}
be algebraically independent generators of
Z(\mathfrak{g})
identified with
S(\mathfrak{j})^{\underline{W}}
.
We can choose them homogeneous. Take for
z_{i}
a suitable power of
u_{i}
in such a way that these powers are of the same degree
N_{0}
.
Then

p(\lambda)=\|\lambda\|^{N_{0}}p(\lambda/\|\lambda\|).

But the continuous function
p
does not vanish on the unit sphere, as its only zero is for
\lambda=0
.

Our claim follows.

Together with the preceding remarks, Theorem
5.4
implies:

5.6 Corollary
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathfrak{j}_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}/{\underline{W}}
and
f\in{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X,\lambda)
.
With the notation of Theorem
5.4
, if
f_{C}=\sum_{\nu}f_{C,\nu}
is the decomposition of
f_{C}
in generalized
A_{I}
-eigenvectors, one has
f_{C,\nu}\in C_{temp,N+N_{I}}^{\infty}(X_{C})
and, if
q
is a continuous seminorm on
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N+N_{I}}(X_{C})
, there exists a continuous seminorm
p^{\prime}
on
{\mathcal{A}}_{N,temp}(X)
, independent on
\lambda
,
f
and
\nu
, such that:

q(f_{C,\nu})\leq p^{\prime}(f),\qquad f\in{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X,\lambda),\lambda\in\mathfrak{j}_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}/{\underline{W}}\,.

5.3
Main inequality

Let
C,D\in{\mathcal{F}}
with
D\subset C
. If
a\in A_{C}
we write
a_{C}=a_{C,0}\exp(\log a_{C})
with
\log a_{C}\in\mathfrak{a}_{C}
,
a_{C,0}\in A_{C,2}
where
A_{C,2}
is the group of elements of
A_{C}
whose squares are equal to 1.
Let
(C,D)
be the closed cone generated by
\check{C}\setminus\check{D}
and let us write
A_{C,D}
, (resp.
\mathfrak{a}_{C,D}
) instead of
A_{(C,D)}
(resp.
\mathfrak{a}_{(C,D)}
). One has
\mathfrak{a}_{C}=\mathfrak{a}_{D}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{C,D}
. If
a_{C}\in A_{C}
,
\log a_{C,D}
will denote the projection of
\log a_{C}
on
\mathfrak{a}_{C,D}
parallel to
\mathfrak{a}_{D}
.
From Lemma
3.2
(v), there is a canonical action of
A_{C}^{0}
and even of
A_{I(C)}^{0}
on
X_{C}
. One has the normalized right action of
A^{0}_{I(C)}
on
C^{\infty}(X_{C})
denoted by a dot:

a.f(x):=a^{-\rho}f(a.x),x\in X_{C},a\in A^{0}_{I(C)}.

(5.1)

5.7 Theorem
.

(Main inequality). Let
C,D\in{\mathcal{F}}
with
D\subset C
. Let
V
be a relatively compact open neighborhood of
1
in
U(X)
and
B^{C}\subset A_{C^{c}}^{0}
be as in (
3.40
). Let
g\in G
. Then

(i) For
f\in{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X_{D})
, the equality:

f^{C,D}_{g}(x):=\sum_{C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}},D\subset C^{\prime}\subset C}(-1)^{Card\check{D}-Card\check{C^{\prime}}}f_{C^{\prime}}(e_{P_{g},C^{\prime}}(x)),x\in X_{P_{g}}

defines a map
f^{C,D}_{g}
on
X_{P_{g}}
, the later being equal to
gX_{P}
(cf. (
3.37
)).

(ii) There exists a continuous seminorm
q
on
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X_{D})
and
\alpha>0
such that, for all
w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
, ,
f\in{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X_{D})
,
Z(\mathfrak{g})
-eigen, which transforms under a unitary character of
A^{0}_{D}
by the normalized right action of
A^{0}_{D}
, one has:

|f^{C,D}_{g}(gvma^{C}a_{C}w)|\leq a_{C}^{\rho}e^{-\alpha\|\log a_{C,D}\|}q(f),m\in M,v\in V,a^{C}\in B^{C},a_{C}\in A_{C}^{-}(D),

where
A^{-}_{C}(D)=\{a_{C}\in A_{C}|\log a_{C,D}\in(C,D)\}
.

Proof.

(i) is clear.

(ii) Let
f^{CD}=f^{C,D}_{1}
. Using (
3.37
), one sees easily the equality
f^{C,D}_{g}(gx)=(L_{g^{-1}}f)^{C,D}(x)
for
x\in X_{P}
. As the semi norm
f\mapsto q(L_{g^{-1}}f)
is bounded by a multiple (depending on
g
) of
q
, it is enough to prove the claim for
g=1
, that we assume from now. First if
C=D
, as
f
transforms under a unitary character by the normalized right action of
A^{0}_{D}=A^{0}_{C}
, one has

|f(vma_{C}a^{C}w)|\leq a_{C}^{\rho}Sup_{v\in V,m\in M,a_{0}\in A_{C,2},a^{C}\in B^{C}}|f(vma_{0}a^{C}w)|,v\in V,a^{C}\in B^{C}.

Our claim follows as
f
is bounded by a seminorm
q_{-N}
for
X_{D}
and as
V
,
M
,
B^{C}
and
A_{C,2}
are bounded.

Let us assume from now on that
D\not=C
. One denotes by
U
the intersection of the unit sphere of
\mathfrak{a}_{C,D}
with the closed cone
(C,D)
. Let
\underline{x}_{0}\in U
and
\check{I}_{0}=\{\check{\alpha}\in\check{C}\setminus\check{D}|(\underline{x}_{0})_{\check{\alpha}}>0\}
. The set
\check{I}_{0}
is not empty as
\underline{x}_{0}
is non zero and
\check{C}\setminus\check{D}
is a basis of
\mathfrak{a}_{C,D}
. Let
\check{J}_{0}
be the complementary set of
\check{I}_{0}
in
\check{C}
. Let
V(\underline{x}_{0})
be the set
\{\underline{x}\in U|\underline{x}_{\check{\alpha}}\geq(\underline{x}_{0})_{\check{\alpha}}/2,\alpha\in\check{I}_{0}\}
which is a compact neighborhood of
\underline{x}_{0}
in
U
.

We will use the fact that for any finite non empty set
E
, one has:

\sum_{A\subset E}(-1)^{CardA}=0.

As
\check{I}_{0}
is not empty, for
K\in{\mathcal{F}}
such that
D\subset K\subset C
and
\check{I}_{0}\subset\check{K}
one has:

\sum_{L\in{\mathcal{F}},D\subset L\subset K,\check{L}\cap\check{J}_{0}=\check{K}\cap\check{J}_{0}}(-1)^{Card\check{D}-Card\check{L}}f_{K}(vma^{C}a_{C}w_{K})=0

for
v\in V,m\in M,a^{C}\in B^{C},a_{C}\in A_{C}^{-}(D).

Let
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}
with
D\subset C^{\prime}\subset C
. There exists a unique pair
(K,L)
of elements of
{\mathcal{F}}
such that
L=C^{\prime}
and
D\subset L\subset K\subset C
with
\check{K}
containing
\check{I_{0}}
and
\check{K}\cap\check{J_{0}}=\check{L}\cap\check{J_{0}}
(in fact
\check{K}=\check{L}\cup\check{I_{0}}
).
Thus the sum over
C^{\prime}
in the definition of
f_{C,D}
can be replaced by the sum over such
K,L
and we can substract the zero sums of the previous equality to get:

f^{C,D}(vma^{C}a_{C}w)=\sum_{K\in{\mathcal{F}},D\subset K\subset C,\check{I}_{0}\subset\check{K}}

\sum_{L\in{\mathcal{F}},D\subset L\subset K,\check{L}\cap\check{J}_{0}=\check{K}\cap\check{J}_{0}}(-1)^{Card\check{D}-Card\check{L}}(f_{L}(vma^{C}a_{C}w_{L})-f_{K}(vma^{C}a_{C}w_{K})).

(5.2)

One has
a_{C}=a_{C,0}\exp\underline{z}\exp(\log a_{C,D})
, where
\underline{z}\in\mathfrak{a}_{D}
.
As
f_{L}
and
f_{K}
transform by the same unitary character of
A^{0}_{D}
for the normalized action of
A^{0}_{D}
, one has:

|a_{C}^{-\rho}(f_{L}(vma^{C}a_{C}w_{L})-f_{K}(vma^{C}a_{C}w_{K}))|=

|e^{-\rho(\log a_{C,D})}(f_{L}(vma^{C}a_{C,0}\exp(\log a_{C,D})w_{L})-f_{K}(vma^{C}a_{C,0}\exp(\log a_{C,D})w_{K}))|.

One wants to use Theorem
5.4
for
X_{L}
to get a bound for

\Delta_{K,L}(v,m,a^{C},a_{C,2},\underline{x},t):=e^{-t\rho(\underline{x})}|f_{L}(vma^{C}a_{C,0}\exp(t\underline{x})w_{L})-f_{K}(vma^{C}a_{C,0}\exp(t\underline{x})w_{K})|,

v\in V,m\in M,a^{C}\in B^{C},a_{C,0}\in A_{C,2},\underline{x}\in V(\underline{x}_{0})\subset\mathfrak{a}_{C,D},t>0.

Let
S_{L}\subset S
be the set of simple spherical roots for
X_{L}
. It is equal to the set
I(L)
of elements of
S
orthogonal to
L
(cf. Lemma
3.2
(ii)), and
\mathfrak{a}_{I(L)}
is the orthogonal of
I(L)
in
\mathfrak{a}_{X}
. This implies:

\mathfrak{a}_{L}\subset\mathfrak{a}_{S_{L}}.

As
\check{I}_{0}\subset\check{K}
, one has:

\underline{x}_{0}=\sum_{\check{\alpha}\in\check{I}_{0}}x_{0,\check{\alpha}}\check{\alpha}\in K,

with the coefficients
x_{0,\check{\alpha}}
strictly positive. Let
\underline{z}=\sum_{\check{\alpha}\in\check{J}_{0}\cap\check{L}}z_{\check{\alpha}}\check{\alpha}
with coefficients strictly positive. Then
\underline{z}\in L\subset\mathfrak{a}_{S_{L}}
. Moreover, as
\check{J_{0}}\cap\check{L}=\check{J}_{0}\cap\check{K}
and
\check{K}=(\check{J}_{0}\cap\check{K})\cup\check{I_{0}}
, one has
\underline{x}_{0}+\underline{z}\in K^{0}
. Altogether we have shown:

\underline{x}_{0}\in K^{0}+\mathfrak{a}_{S_{L}}.

We have the notion of
A^{-}_{X}
for
X_{L}
, denoted by
A^{-}_{X,L}.

One takes, in Theorem
5.4
for
X_{L}
,
\Omega=(cl(V))M(cl(B^{C}))A_{C,2}
and let
{\mathcal{C}}_{K}\subset K^{0}+\mathfrak{a}_{S_{L}}
be equal to the set
\{\underline{x}_{0}/2\}
.
We set
\underline{y}=\underline{x}-\underline{x}_{0}/2
which is easily seen to be in
C\subset\mathfrak{a}_{X}^{-}
. One has
a_{X_{L}}:=\exp t\underline{y}\in A_{X}^{-}\subset A_{X_{L}}^{-}
(there are less roots for
X_{L}
) . Then

\exp t\underline{x}=(\exp t\underline{y})(\exp t\underline{x}_{0}/2).

Hence Theorem
5.4
applies to
f_{L}
, which is
Z(\mathfrak{g})
-eigen like
f
(cf. Theorem
5.1
(iii)). But
f_{L}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N+N_{L}}(X_{L})
where
N_{L}
has been defined before Theorem
5.4
. From Theorem
5.4
(i) for
X_{L}
, one gets, changing
N
by
N+N_{L}
,
a continuous seminorm
p_{L}
on
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N+N_{L}}(X_{L})
and
\varepsilon>0
such that

\Delta_{K,L}(v,m,a^{C},a_{C,2},\underline{x},t)\leq e^{-\varepsilon t}(1+t\|\underline{y}\|)^{N+N_{L}}p_{L}(f_{L}),

v\in V,m\in M,a^{C}\in B^{C},a_{C,2}\in A_{C,2},\underline{x}\in V(\underline{x}_{0})\subset\mathfrak{a}_{C,D},t>0.

As
\underline{y}=\underline{x}-x_{0}/2
, one has
\|y\|\leq 2
and the right hand side of the previous inequality is bounded by a constant times
e^{-\varepsilon^{\prime}t}p_{L}(f_{L})
for
0<\varepsilon^{\prime}<\varepsilon
.

We get, from Theorem
5.4
(ii), a continuous seminorm
q
on
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X_{D})
such
p_{L}(f_{L})\leq q(f)
.
Using that
e^{-t}=e^{-t\|\underline{x}\|}
, that a finite number of
V(\underline{x}_{0})
covers
U
and summing the bounds for all pairs
(K,L)
as in (
5.2
), we get (ii).

5.4
Invariant differential operators

Let
C\in{\mathcal{F}}
and
\mathbb{D}(X_{C})
(resp.
\mathbb{D}(\underline{X}_{C})
) be the algebra of
G
-invariant (resp.
\underline{G}
-invariant) differential operators on
X_{C}
(resp. algebraic differential operators on
\underline{X}_{C}
).
Then

\mathbb{D}(X_{C})=\oplus_{O\in{\mathcal{O}}_{C}}\mathbb{D}(O),

(5.3)

where
\mathbb{D}(O)
is the algebra of
G
-invariant differential operators on the
G
-orbit
O
in
X_{C}
. Notice that, from Lemma
3.2
(i),
X_{C}
depends only on
I=S\cap C^{\perp}
. Hence we will replace the lower index
C
by the lower index
I
.

We will first be interested to
O=G/H_{I}
. The other orbits are treated similarly by a change of base point.

Recall that
U(\mathfrak{g})
is the enveloping algebra of the complex Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Let
U_{H_{I}}(\mathfrak{g}):=\{u\in U(\mathfrak{g})|Ad(h)u-u\in U(\mathfrak{g})\mathfrak{h}_{I},h\in H_{I}\}
. From
[
8
]
, Lemma C.1, the right action induces a natural isomorphism

\mathbb{D}(G/H_{I})\simeq U_{H_{I}}(\mathfrak{g})/U(\mathfrak{g})\mathfrak{h}_{I}.

Similarly, the algebra of
\underline{G}
-invariant algebraic differential operators on
\underline{G}/\underline{H}_{I}
identifies with
U_{\underline{H}_{I}}(\mathfrak{g}):=\{u\in U(\mathfrak{g})|Ad(h)u-u\in U(\mathfrak{g})\underline{\mathfrak{h}}_{I},h\in\underline{H}_{I}\}
.
Hence
\mathbb{D}(\underline{G}/\underline{H}_{I})
identifies with a subalgebra of
\mathbb{D}(G/H_{I}).

Let
\mathfrak{b}:=\mathfrak{a}+\mathfrak{m}+\mathfrak{u}
,
where
\mathfrak{u}
is the Lie algebra of
U(X)
, and
\mathfrak{b}_{H}:=\mathfrak{a}\cap\mathfrak{h}+\mathfrak{m}\cap\mathfrak{h}
. Let
I
be as above. Let
U_{I}(\mathfrak{b})=\{u\in U(\mathfrak{b})|Ad(h)u-u\in U(\mathfrak{g})\underline{\mathfrak{h}_{I}},h\in\underline{H}_{I}\}
. From
[
8
]
, (C.8), there is an isomorphism

p_{I}:U_{I}(\mathfrak{b})/U(\mathfrak{b})\mathfrak{b}_{H}(\subset U(\mathfrak{b})/U(\mathfrak{b})\mathfrak{b}_{H})\to\mathbb{D}(\underline{X}_{I}),

(5.4)

given by the right regular action
u\mapsto R_{u}
. In the sequel, we identify
\mathbb{D}(\underline{X}_{I})
to this subalgebra of
U(\mathfrak{b})/U(\mathfrak{b})\mathfrak{b}_{H}
. The space
U_{I}(\mathfrak{b})/U(\mathfrak{b})\mathfrak{b}_{H}
is a module over
\mathfrak{a}_{X}
for the adjoint action. Then, from Theorem C.5 of
[
8
]
, one has:

(i) Let
I
be as above. For any
u\in\mathbb{D}(\underline{X})
and
\underline{x}\in\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{--}
, the limit
\mu_{I}(u):=lim_{t\to+\infty}e^{tad\underline{x}}u
exists and is independent on the choice of
\underline{x}\in\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{--}
. Hence
\mu_{I}(u)
is of weight 0 under
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
. It defines an injective algebra morphism
\mu_{I}
:
\mathbb{D}(\underline{X})\to\mathbb{D}(\underline{X}_{I})
.

(ii) For any nonzero
u\in\mathbb{D}(\underline{X})
, the
\mathfrak{a}_{X}
-weights of
\mu_{I}(u)
and
u
are non positive on
\mathfrak{a}_{X}^{-}
and the
\mathfrak{a}_{X}
-weights of
\mu_{I}(u)-u
are negative on
\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{--}
. Moreover,
\mu_{I}(u)
is the projection of
u
on the zero
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
-weight space.

(5.5)

As a corollary, one sees that
\mathfrak{a}_{S}
commutes to
u\in\mathbb{D}(\underline{X})
: as
\mathfrak{a}_{S}\subset\mathfrak{a}_{X}^{-}
, (
5.5
) (ii), for
I=S
, implies that the
\mathfrak{a}_{S}
-weights of
u
are zero.
More generally:

The space
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
is contained in the center of
\mathbb{D}(\underline{X}_{I})
.

(5.6)

Let
\underline{B}
be a Borel subgroup of
\underline{G}
that is contained in
\underline{P}
. The local structure theorem then yields a parabolic subgroup
\underline{R}
of
\underline{G}
and a Levi-decomposition
\underline{R}=\underline{L}_{R}\underline{N}_{R}
so that
\underline{R}\cap\underline{H}
=
\underline{L}_{R}\cap\underline{H}
is a normal subgroup of
\underline{L}_{R}
and
\underline{L}_{R}/(\underline{L}_{R}\cap\underline{H})
is a torus. By
[
23
]
, Lemma 9.3,
\underline{L}_{R}
may be chosen so that
\underline{A}\subset\underline{L}_{R}
. Let now
\mathfrak{t}
be a maximal abelian subalgebra of
\mathfrak{m}\cap{\mathfrak{l}_{R}}
. Then
\mathfrak{j}:=\mathfrak{a}\oplus\mathfrak{t}
is a Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
. This is our choice of
\mathfrak{j}
. Without loss of generality we may assume that
\underline{\mathfrak{j}}=\underline{\mathfrak{a}}\oplus\underline{\mathfrak{t}}
is contained in the Lie algebra of
\underline{B}
. Let
\rho_{\underline{X}}
be the half-sum of the roots of
\mathfrak{j}
in the Lie algebra of
\underline{B}
.
Following
[
21
]
, section 3, Definition, let
\mathfrak{U}(\underline{X})
be the algebra of completely regular differential operators on
\underline{X}
and let
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
its subalgebra of
\underline{G}
-invariant differential operators (cf. l.c. Corollary 6.3). The algebra
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
is a subalgebra of the center of
\mathbb{D}(\underline{X})
(cf. l.c.
Corollary 9.2).
Recall that
\underline{W}
is the Weyl group of
(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{j}_{\mathbb{C}})
.
Let us define

\mathfrak{a}_{\underline{X}}=(\mathfrak{j}/(\mathfrak{j}\cap\mathfrak{h})_{\mathbb{C}}.

(5.7)

Let
I\subset S
. The Knop homomorphism (cf
[
21
]
, Corollary 6.3 and Theorem 6.5)
\gamma_{\underline{X}}
(resp.
\gamma_{\underline{X}_{I}}
) is an isomorphism of
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
(resp.
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
) with
\mathbb{C}[\rho_{\underline{X}}+\mathfrak{a}_{\underline{X}}^{*}]^{\underline{W}_{\underline{X}}}
( resp.
\mathbb{C}[\rho_{\underline{X}}+\mathfrak{a}_{\underline{X}}^{*}]^{\underline{W}_{\underline{X}_{I}}}
). Here
\underline{W}_{\underline{X}}
(resp.
\underline{W}_{\underline{X}_{I}}
) is a subgroup of
\{\mathfrak{w}\in\underline{W}|\mathfrak{w}(\rho_{\underline{X}}+\mathfrak{a}_{\underline{X}}^{*})=\rho_{\underline{X}}+\mathfrak{a}_{\underline{X}}^{*}\}
which acts as a finite reflection group on
\mathfrak{a}^{*}_{\underline{X}}
.
Moreover
\underline{W}_{\underline{X}}
is the Weyl group of a root system with a basis that we denote by
\underline{S}
. The root system is denoted by
<\underline{S}>
. Moreover, up to normalization, the set of non zero restrictions to
\mathfrak{a}_{X}
of the elements of
\underline{S}
is equal to
S
(cf.
[
23
]
, (10.9) and Theorem 10.14 (i)).

(5.8)

5.8 Lemma
.

(i)
\mathfrak{a}_{X_{I}}\subset\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
.

(ii)
The group
\underline{W}_{\underline{X}_{I}}
leaves fixed every element of
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
.

Proof.

One is reduced to prove the claim for
I=S
.

Let us show that
\overline{\underline{X}}
is a pseudofree
\underline{G}
-variety. From
[
21
]
, Lemma 2.4, one has to show that the map
j:\underline{X}\to Grass_{\mathrm{dim}\,LieH}(\mathfrak{g})
,
x\mapsto LieG_{x}
, extends smoothly to
\overline{\underline{X}}
. By
\underline{G}
-equivariance, this might be seen on an open set given by the Local Structure Theorem. As
\overline{\underline{X}}
is also smooth, it follows that:

The algebra
\mathfrak{U}(\underline{X})
is identical with the algebra of global sections of the sheaf
\mathfrak{U}_{\overline{\underline{X}}}
of algebraic differential operators on
\overline{\underline{X}}
which locally are in the algebra generated by elements of the Lie algebra
\underline{\mathfrak{g}}
and multiplication by regular functions.

(5.9)

As
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
acts on the right on
\underline{X}
, it acts on the right on
\overline{\underline{X}}
. Hence using Definition in section 3 of l.c., one sees that
\mathfrak{a}_{S}\subset\mathfrak{U}(\underline{X})
hence is contained in
\mathfrak{Z}(X)
. This proves the firs claim.

As a result
\mathfrak{a}_{S}\subset k[\rho_{\underline{X}}+\mathfrak{a}_{\underline{X}}^{*}]^{W_{\underline{X}}}
. Hence
W_{\underline{X}}
leaves invariant the elements of
\mathfrak{a}_{S}
.
This implies the Lemma.

From
[
34
]
, Proposition 8.14, one has:

\gamma_{\underline{X}}=\gamma_{\underline{X}_{\emptyset}}\circ\mu_{\emptyset}.

(5.10)

Now let
\gamma
be the Harish-Chandra homomorphism:
\gamma:Z(\mathfrak{g})\to\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{j}_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}]^{\underline{W}}
and
r
the restriction map:
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{j}_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}]\to\mathbb{C}[\rho+\mathfrak{a}_{\underline{X}}^{*}]
.
and let
p:Z(\mathfrak{g})\to\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
be the canonical map. Then:

r\circ\gamma=\gamma_{\underline{X}}\circ p.

(5.11)

5.9 Lemma
.

Let
u
be in
\mathbb{D}(\underline{X}):=\mathbb{D}(\underline{X}_{S})
. There exist
u_{1},\dots,u_{n}
in
U(\mathfrak{g})
such that

|uf(x)|\leq\sum_{i}|L_{u_{i}}f(x)|,x\in X,f\in C^{\infty}(X).

Proof.

It is enough to prove this for
f\in C^{\infty}(G/H)
. The other orbits are treated by a change of base point.
From (
4.9
) one has
G/H=\Omega A_{X}^{-}F
where
F
is a subset of
A_{X,2}
and
\Omega
is compact.
Let us prove our claim first for
x=\omega a\in\Omega A_{X}^{-}
.
We use the notation of (
5.4
). Express the action of
u
as the right action of
v:=p_{S}^{-1}(u)
.
Let us decompose
v
in
\mathfrak{a}_{X}
-weights,
v=\sum_{\lambda}v_{\lambda}
.
Let
\overline{v}_{\lambda}
be a representative in
U(\mathfrak{b})
of
v_{\lambda}
. Let
x\mapsto x^{t}
be the principal antiautomorphism of
U(\mathfrak{g})
. As for
u\in U(\mathfrak{b})\mathfrak{b}_{H}
and
h\in C^{\infty}(X)
.
One has:

(R_{u}h)(a)=0,a\in A_{X},

one deduces:

R_{v}f(\omega a)=\sum_{\lambda}a^{\lambda}[L_{\overline{v_{\lambda}}^{t}}(L_{\omega^{-1}}f)](a).

Hence:

R_{v}f(\omega a)=\sum_{\lambda}a^{\lambda}[L_{\omega^{-1}}L_{Ad(\omega)\overline{v}_{\lambda}}f](a).

There exist
v_{1},\dots,v_{j}\in U(\mathfrak{g})
and
c_{\lambda,1},\dots c_{\lambda,j}
, bounded functions on
\Omega
such that:

Ad(\omega)\overline{v}_{\lambda}=c_{\lambda,1}(\omega)v_{1}+\dots+c_{\lambda,j}(\omega)v_{j}.

Hence

R_{v}f(\omega)=\sum_{\lambda,i}a^{\lambda}c_{\lambda,i}(\omega)L_{v_{i}}f(\omega).

As
a^{\lambda}\leq 1
, ouf claim follows.
For general
x=\omega af
,
f\in F
, we use what we has just proved, replacing
H
by
fHf^{-1}.

5.10 Proposition
.

(i) The algebra
\mathbb{D}(\underline{X})
acts continuously on the space
L^{2}(X)^{\infty}
of
C^{\infty}
-vectors of
L^{2}(X)
.

(ii) The algebra
\mathbb{D}(\underline{X})
acts continuously on
C^{\infty}_{temp,N}(X)
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X)
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from the previous lemma.

5.11 Lemma
.

Let
D(\underline{X})
be the algebra of algebraic differential operators on
\underline{X}
.

There exists an antiautomorphism of
D(\underline{X})
,
D\mapsto^{t}D
such that for every
f_{1},f_{2}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G/H)
:

\int_{G/H}(Df_{1})f_{2}\omega=\int_{G/H}f_{1}(^{t}Df_{2})\omega.

Proof.

Let
\underline{x}
be an algebraic vector field vector field on
\underline{X}
.
Let
f_{1},f_{2}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G/H)
.
Then

\int_{G/H}{\mathcal{L}}_{\underline{x}}(f_{1}f_{2}\omega)=0

as, by the Cartan formula,
{\mathcal{L}}_{\underline{x}}(f_{1}f_{2}\omega)=di_{\underline{x}}(f_{1}f_{2}\omega)
, where
{\mathcal{L}}_{\underline{x}}
is the Lie derivative and
i_{\underline{x}}
is the contraction by
\underline{x}
.
This implies

\int_{G/H}(\underline{x}f_{1})f_{2}\omega=-\int_{G/H}f_{1}{\mathcal{L}}_{\underline{x}}(f_{2}\omega).

But as
\omega
is nowhere vanishing,
{\mathcal{L}}_{\underline{x}}f_{2}\omega
is of the form
(z^{\prime}f_{2})\omega
where
z^{\prime}\in D(\underline{X})
is of order 1.
This proves the existence of
{}^{t}D
for
D
an algebraic vector field. The unicity is clear.
Then one proves the existence of
{}^{t}D
by first localizing and using Lemma 1 in Lecture 1, section 2 of
[
4
]
. Then one glues the result by unicity.
The fact that it gives rise to an antiautomorphism follows from unicity.

5.12 Lemma
.

If
D\in\mathfrak{U}(\underline{X})
, then
{}^{t}D\in\mathfrak{U}(\underline{X})
.

Proof.

This follows easily from (
5.9
).

5.13 Lemma
.

If
D\in\mathbb{D}(\underline{X})
, then
{}^{t}D\in\mathbb{D}(\underline{X})

Proof.

Using the definition of
{}^{t}D
, one sees easily that for all
f_{1},f_{2}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G/H),g\in G
, one has:

\int_{G/H}f_{1}(L_{g}\>^{t}Df_{2})\omega=\int_{G/H}f_{1}(^{t}DL_{g}f_{2})\omega.

The Lemma follows.

Let
x\to\bar{x}
be the complex conjugacy on
\underline{X}
and if
f\in{\mathcal{O}}(\underline{X})
, let
f^{+}(x)=\bar{f(\bar{x}})
which is in
{\mathcal{O}}(\underline{X})
.
We define, for an algebraic differential operator
D
,
D^{+}f=(Df^{+})^{+}
. It is easily seen that it is an algebraic differential operator on
\underline{X}
: this follows from the definition (see
[
4
]
, Lecture 1, section 2). Moreover one sees easily that:

If
D\in\mathbb{D}(\underline{X})
,
D^{+}
is in
\mathbb{D}(\underline{X})
.

(5.12)

Using the description of
\mathfrak{U}(\underline{X})
in the proof of Lemma
5.12
, one sees:

If
D\in\mathfrak{U}(\underline{X})
,
D^{+}
is in
\mathfrak{U}(\underline{X})
.

(5.13)

By using coordinate systems as in
[
4
]
, Lecture 1, Lemma in section 2:

D^{+}f(x)=\overline{D(\overline{f})}(x),x\in X,f\in C^{\infty}(X).

(5.14)

If
D
is a differential operator on
X
with
C^{\infty}
-coefficients, there is a unique differential operator
D^{*}
on
X
, called formal adjoint of
D
such that:
for all
f_{1},f_{2}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G/H),g\in G
, one has:

\int_{G/H}(Df_{1})\overline{f_{2}}\omega=\int_{G/H}f_{1}\overline{D^{*}f_{2}}\omega.

5.14 Proposition
.

(i) Let
D\in\mathbb{D}(\underline{X})
, then
(^{t}D)^{+}\in\mathbb{D}(\underline{X})
is the formal adjoint of
D
viewed as a differential operator on
X
.

(ii)If
D\in\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
, then its formal adjoint is also in
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
.

Proof.

(i) follows from Lemma
5.11
and (
5.14
).
(ii) follows from Lemma
5.12
and
5.13
.

5.15 Proposition
.

If
u\in\mathbb{D}(\underline{X})
, let
u^{*}\in\mathbb{D}(\underline{X})
be the corresponding element to its formal adjoint as a differential operator on
G/H
.

(i) If
u\in\mathbb{D}(\underline{X})
satisfies
u=u^{*}
then
u
is essentially self-adjoint with operator core
L^{2}(G/H)^{\infty}
. The domain of its closure
D_{u}
is
{\mathcal{D}}_{u}:=\{f\in L^{2}(G/H)|uf\in L^{2}(G/H)\}
.

(ii) The autodjoint elements of
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
have mutually commuting spectral decompositions. In particular if
D_{1},\dots,D_{n}
are self-adjoint operators of
L^{2}(X)
corresponding to elements of the algebra
\mathfrak{Z}_{\mathbb{R}}(\underline{X})
of self-adjoint elements of
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
, the 1-parameter groups
U_{1}(t),\dots U_{n}(t)
of unitary operators that they generate
commute and commute to the action of
G
.

(iii) There exists such a finite family, generating the algebra
\mathfrak{Z}_{\mathbb{R}}(\underline{X})
of self-adjoint elements of
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
. It might be taken to contain a basis of
i\mathfrak{a}_{S}
.

(iv) The restriction map induces a bijection between the set of
*
-characters of
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
and the set of real characters of
\mathfrak{Z}_{\mathbb{R}}(\underline{X})
.

Proof.

(i) is proved like Proposition 1.4 of
[
1
]
which completes the proof of Lemma 9 in
[
38
]
. We prefer to give here a complete proof.

Let
u
be as in (i), in particular
u=u^{*}
. Let
{\mathcal{D}}:=\{f\in L^{2}(G/H)|uf\in L^{2}(G/H)\}
and
D
be the operator
D:{\mathcal{D}}\to L^{2}(G/H)
,
f\mapsto uf
. It is easy to see that
D
is closed: if
(f_{n})
in
{\mathcal{D}}
is such that
(f_{n})
(resp.
(uf_{n})
) converges to
f
(resp.
g
) in
L^{2}(X)
, one sees easily that
uf=g
as a distribution, hence
(f,g)
is in the graph of
D
.

Let
D_{1}
be the restriction of
D
to
L^{2}(X)^{\infty}
. The graph of
D_{1}
is dense in the graph of
D
: this is seen by using a convolution by a Dirac sequence in
C_{c}^{\infty}(G)
which commutes with
u
as
u\in\mathbb{D}(\underline{X})
. This shows that
D_{1}
is a core of
D
.
Recall the definition of the adjoint
D^{*}
of
D
. Its domain
{\mathcal{D}}_{*}
is the space of
f\in L^{2}(X)
such that there exists
f^{*}\in L^{2}(X)
with
(f,Dg)=(f^{*},g),g\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
Here
(.,.)
denotes the
L^{2}
-scalar product. Then
D^{*}f=f^{*}
. By the density above, one has also:

{\mathcal{D}}_{*}=\{f\in L^{2}(X)|\exists f^{*}\in L^{2}(X),(f,Dg)=(f^{*},g),g\in L^{2}(X)^{\infty}\}

(5.15)

Restricting to
g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
and using
u^{*}=u
, one sees that
f^{*}
is equal to
uf
as a distribution, hence
{\mathcal{D}}_{*}\subset{\mathcal{D}}
.

In order to prove the reverse inclusion, let us first prove that:

The space
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
is dense in
L^{2}(X)^{\infty}
.

(5.16)

This follows from the theorem of Dixmier-Malliavin on the Garding space: from this theorem, it is enough to express an element
\phi\star f

, where
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G)
and
f\in L^{2}(X)
, as a limit in
L^{2}(X)
of of a sequence of elements of
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
. But this follows from the fact that
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
is dense in
L^{2}(X)
and stable by convolution by
\phi
.

From Proposition
5.10
,
L^{2}(X)^{\infty}\subset{\mathcal{D}}
and
D
acts continuously on
L^{2}(X)^{\infty}
. Let us show:

(Df,g)=(f,Dg),f,g\in L^{2}(X)^{\infty}.

(5.17)

As it is clearly true for
f,g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
, this follows by continuity and density.

Let
f\in{\mathcal{D}}
and let
f^{*}
be the element of
L^{2}(X)
which coincides with
Df
as a distribution. Let
(\phi_{n})
be a Dirac sequence in
C_{c}^{\infty}(G)
. Because of the
G
-invariance of
D
, one has, for
h\in C^{\infty}(X)
:

D(\phi_{n}\star h)=\phi_{n}\star Dh.
More generally it is true for distributions.
Let
f\in{\mathcal{D}}
and
g\in L^{2}(X)^{\infty}
.
Then one has from (
5.17
):

(\phi_{n}\star f,Dg)=(D(\phi_{n}\star f),g)

=(\phi_{n}\star Df,g)

=(\phi_{n}\star f^{*},g).

Since the left hand side converges to
(f,Dg)
while the right hand side converges to
(f^{*},g)
, one has

(f,Dg)=(f^{*},g),g\in L^{2}(X)^{\infty}.

It follows from (
5.15
) that
f\in{\mathcal{D}}_{*}
. This achieves to prove that
{\mathcal{D}}={\mathcal{D}}_{*}
and
D=D^{*}
.
(i) follows.

As
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
is commutative and commute to the action of
G
, the first part of (ii) follows from
[
35
]
, Corollary 9.2. The second part is clear.

(iii) Let
(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
be a set of generators of the polynomial algebra
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
which might be taken to contain a basis of
i\mathfrak{a}_{S}
.
Let
D_{i}
(resp.
D^{\prime}_{i}
) be the self-adjoint operator corresponding to
u_{i}+u_{i}^{*}
, (resp to
i(u_{i}-u_{i}^{*})
). The family of
D_{i},D^{\prime}_{i}
is easily seen to have the required property.

(iv) From (iii), one sees that the restriction map is injective. The
\mathbb{C}
-linear extension to
\mathfrak{Z}(X)
of a real character of
\mathfrak{Z}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
is easily seen, by direct computation, to be a
*
-character of
\mathfrak{Z}(X)
. This proves that the restriction map is bijective.

5.16 Remark
.

(i)
When we will be interested in the spectral decomposition of elements in
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
, we will introduce a set of generators
D_{1},\dots,D_{n}
of
\mathfrak{Z}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)
as in (iii) of the above Lemma and we will introduce, with the notation of (ii) of this Lemma, the unitary representation of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, given by
(t_{1},\dots t_{n})\mapsto U_{1}(t_{1})\dots U_{n}(t_{n})
.
The decomposition of this representation into irreducible ones will give the spectral decomposition of
\mathfrak{Z}(X)
into
*
-characters.

(ii) By changing the base point, the preceeding proposition is valid when one replaces
G/H
by any other
G
-orbit in
X
.

5.17 Proposition
.

For
\mu
-almost all
\pi
in
\hat{G}
,
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
acts on
{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
,
\mathfrak{Z}_{\mathbb{R}}(\underline{X})
acts by selfadjoint operators in
{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
(cf. section
4.1
for notation) and the map
\alpha_{\pi}
is a
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
-map.

Proof.

This is seen by considering the decomposition of the representation of
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})\times G
(resp.
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})_{\mathbb{R}}\times G
) on
L^{2}(X)
.

5.18 Proposition
.

Let
I\subset S
,
f\in{\mathcal{A}}_{temp}(X)
and
u\in\mathbb{D}(\underline{X})
. Then:

(uf)_{I}=\mu_{I}(u)f_{I},

where
\mu_{I}(u)\in\mathbb{D}(\underline{X}_{I})
is defined in (
5.5
).

Proof.

By changing the base point, it is only necessary to prove the statement for
f\in C^{\infty}(Gx_{0})
.

Let
u^{\prime}=\mu_{I}(u)
.
Let
\underline{x}\in\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{--}
. We identify
u
with an element of
U_{S}(\mathfrak{b})/U(\mathfrak{b})\mathfrak{b}_{H}
(cf. (
5.4
)).
Then

uf(\exp t\underline{x})=R_{u}f(\exp t\underline{x})=[L_{Ad(\exp t\underline{x})u^{t}}f](\exp t\underline{x}).

(5.18)

From (
5.5
), one has:

Ad(\exp t\underline{x})u=u^{\prime}+\sum_{\lambda<0}e^{\lambda t}v_{\lambda},

(5.19)

where
v_{\lambda}\in U(\mathfrak{b})/U(\mathfrak{b})\mathfrak{b}_{H}
.

As
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
is in the center of
\mathbb{D}(\underline{X}_{I})
,
Ad(\exp t\underline{x})u^{\prime}=u^{\prime}
, hence also
Ad(\exp t\underline{x})(u^{\prime})^{t}=(u^{\prime})^{t}
and
(u^{\prime})^{t}f_{I}(\exp t\underline{x})=(L_{(u^{\prime})^{t}}f_{I})(\exp t\underline{x})
.
Using (
5.18
) and (
5.19
), one has:

e^{-t\rho(\underline{x})}((uf)(\exp t\underline{x})-({u^{\prime}}f_{I})(\exp t\underline{x}))=A+B,

where

A=e^{-t\rho(\underline{x})}[(L_{(u^{\prime})^{t}}f)(\exp t\underline{x})-(L_{(u^{\prime})^{t}}f_{I})(\exp t\underline{x})]

and

B=\sum_{\lambda<0}e^{\lambda t}e^{-t\rho(\underline{x})}(L_{v_{\lambda}^{t}}f)(\exp t\underline{x}),

Let us show that:

lim_{t\to+\infty}e^{-t\rho(\underline{x})}((uf)(\exp t\underline{x})-(u^{\prime}f_{I})(\exp t\underline{x}))=0.

(5.20)

By definition of the temperedness,
B
tends to zero.
Also
A
tends to zero, by the properties of the constant term of
L_{(u^{\prime})^{t}}f
.
Then using left translates by
g\in G
and the
G
-equivariance of the constant term, one can replace
\exp t\underline{x}
by
g\exp t\underline{x}
in (
5.20
). Then
u^{\prime}f_{I}
has the same asymptotic property than
(uf)_{I}
(cf. Theorem
5.1
).
But
\mathbb{D}(\underline{X}_{I})
acts on
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp}(X_{I})
(cf. Lemma
5.10
). Hence
u^{\prime}f_{I}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{temp}(Gx_{0})
. From Theorem
5.1
, one deduces

u^{\prime}f_{I}=(uf)_{I}.

We say that
f\in C^{\infty}(X)
is an eigenfunction for
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
, if there is a
\lambda\in\rho+\mathfrak{a}_{\underline{X}}^{*}
such that

uf=\gamma_{\underline{X}}(u)(\lambda)f,u\in\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}).

One says that
f
is a
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
-eigenfunction for the eigenvalue (of parameter)
\lambda
.

It follows from (
5.11
),

f
is
Z(\mathfrak{g})
-eigen for the eigenvalue
\lambda
.

(5.21)

6
Review of the Bernstein maps and of the twisted discrete spectrum

6.1
Plancherel formula for
X_{I}
from the one for
X
and the Maass-Selberg relations

Let
I\subset S
and
C\in{\mathcal{F}}
with
I(C)=I
. We will write
X_{I}
instead of
X_{C}
as
X_{C}
does not depends only on
I
(cf Lemma
3.2
). More generally, we will change the lower index
C
by the lower index
I
. From (
3.21
), one can choose
C\in{\mathcal{F}}
with
\mathfrak{a}_{C}=\mathfrak{a}_{I}
.

Let
(\pi,{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi})
be a unitary irreducible representation of
G
and
\eta
in the space
{\mathcal{H}}^{-\infty X}_{\pi,temp}
of continuous
G
-equivariant morphisms from
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{\infty}
to
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp}(X)
.
Its constant term
\eta_{I}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{-\infty X_{I}}_{\pi,temp}
is defined as follows.

\eta_{I}(v)=\eta(v)_{I},v\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{\infty}.

(6.1)

Let
{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}\subset{\mathcal{H}}^{-\infty X}_{\pi,temp}
be as in section
4.1
.
Recall, from Remark
4.1
, the decomposition:

{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}=\oplus_{O\in{\mathcal{O}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{O}_{\pi},

where

{\mathcal{M}}^{O}_{\pi}={\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{-\infty O}.

Recall that
A^{0}_{I}
acts on the right on
X_{I}
(cf. Lemma
3.2
(v)). Using the definition of
\mathbf{w}
(cf. (
4.10
)), one sees that the action of every element of
A^{0}_{I}
on
\mathbf{w}
is bounded by
\mathbf{w}+C
, where
C
is a positive constant. Using the seminorms
p_{N}
of (
4.12
), one sees that that
A_{I}^{0}
acts on
C^{\infty}_{temp}(X_{I})
, hence on
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi,temp}^{-\infty X_{I}}
. For
\mu
-almost all
\pi
in
{\hat{G}}
and all
O\in{\mathcal{O}}
, let
{\mathcal{M}}^{O}_{I,\pi}
be the linear span of the of the
a.\eta_{I}
,
a\in A^{0}_{I}
,
\eta\in{\mathcal{M}}^{O}_{\pi}
. From Theorem
5.1
(iii), it is a subspace of
\oplus_{O^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{I,O}}{\mathcal{H}}^{-\infty O^{\prime}}_{\pi,temp}
.

Let

{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}:=\oplus_{O\in{\mathcal{O}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{O}_{I,\pi}

(6.2)

6.1 Theorem
.

(
[
13
]
, Theorem 9.5) Let
I\subset S
. For
\mu
-almost all
\pi
in
{\hat{G}}
, there exists a scalar product on the finite dimensional space
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}
such that
A^{0}_{I}
acts unitarily on it and such that the map

\alpha_{I}:C_{c}^{\infty}(X_{I})\to\int_{\hat{G}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}d\mu(\pi)

defined as follows:

For

f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(X_{I})
,
\alpha_{I}(f)=(\alpha_{I,\pi}(f))_{\pi\in{\hat{G}}}
where
\alpha_{I,\pi}(f)\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}
is characterized by:

(\alpha_{I,\pi}(f),v\otimes\eta)=\int_{X_{I}}f(x)\overline{m_{v,\eta}(x)}dx,v\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{\infty},\eta\in{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}.,

(6.3)

extends to a unitary isomorphism
\overline{\alpha}_{I}
of representations of
G\times A_{I}^{0}
.

We will denote by
\beta_{I}:\int_{\hat{G}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi,I}d\mu(\pi)\to L^{2}(X_{I})
the adjoint of
\overline{\alpha_{I}}
.

6.2 Remark
.

From Proposition
5.17
,
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
acts on
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}
for
\mu
-almost all
\pi
in
{\hat{G}}
.

We denote for historical reasons ( cf.
[
39
]
, the definition of
i_{I}
in section 11) by
j_{I,\pi}^{*}
the map
{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}
,
\eta\mapsto\eta_{I}
. Using the scalar products on
{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}
, we denote by
j_{I,\pi}
the adjoint of
j_{I,\pi}^{*}
:

j_{I,\pi}:{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}.

(6.4)

We then have the Maass-Selberg relations:

6.3 Theorem
.

(cf.
[
13
]
, Theorem 9.6).
For
\mu
-almost all
\pi
in
{\hat{G}}
, let
\lambda
be an
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
-weight of
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}
. In particular it is imaginary on
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
. Let
p_{\pi,\lambda}
be the orthogonal projection onto the
\lambda
-eigenspace
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\lambda}
, hence parallel to the others. Then
p_{\pi,\lambda}j^{*}_{I,\pi}
is a surjective partial isometry (i.e. an isometry when one goes through the quotient by its kernel) and
j_{I,\pi}
restricted to
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\lambda}
is an isometry.

We have the following corollary which follows from the next lemma applied to
H_{I}
instead of
H
.

6.4 Corollary
.

The norm of
j_{I,\pi}
and
j_{I,\pi}^{*}
are bounded by the order of
\underline{W}
.

6.5 Lemma
.

Let
(\pi,{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi})
be an irreducible unitary representation of
G
. Let
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{-\infty H}
be the space of
H
-fixed distribution vectors of
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}
, which identifies with
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{-\infty G/H}
on which
A^{0}_{S}
acts unitarily hence
\mathfrak{a}_{S}
acts semisimply. The number of
\mathfrak{a}_{S}
-weights in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{-\infty H}
is at most
Card\>\underline{W}
.

Proof.

The algebra
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
acts on
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{-\infty H}
which is finite dimensional. The possible parameters for the eigenvalues of
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
acting on this space are related to the Harish-Chandra parameter of the infinitesimal character of
\pi
(cf. (
5.21
)). As
\mathfrak{a}_{S}\subset\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
, the possible
\mathfrak{a}_{S}
-weights are of the form
\Lambda_{|\mathfrak{a}_{s}}
, where
\Lambda
is among the Harish-Chandra parameters of the infinitesimal character. The Lemma follows.

6.2
Bernstein maps

For
\mu
-almost all
\pi
in
{\hat{G}}
, we define:

i_{I,\pi}:{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi},i_{I,\pi}=Id_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}}\otimes j_{I,\pi}.

Let
i^{*}_{I,\pi}
be its adjoint. They define measurable families of operators: we give a detail proof for
i_{I,\pi}
in Proposition
10.3
. The result for
i^{*}_{I,\pi}
follows by adjunction.
Then we have bounded operators (cf
[
13
]
, Proposition 10.2)

i_{I}=\int^{\oplus}_{\hat{G}}i_{I,\pi}d\mu(\pi):\int^{\oplus}_{\hat{G}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}d\mu(\pi)\to\int^{\oplus}_{\hat{G}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}d\mu(\pi).

(6.5)

The adjoint
i_{I}^{*}
of
i_{I}
satisfies:

i_{I}^{*}=\int^{\oplus}_{\hat{G}}i^{*}_{I,\pi}d\mu(\pi).

(6.6)

In the sequel, if we have two linear maps
a
,
b
, we will denote
ab
instead of
a\circ b
, when it is defined.

If
I\subset J\subset S
, one can introduce similarly, by replacing
S
by
J
:

i_{I}^{J}:\int^{\oplus}_{\hat{G}}i^{J}_{I,\pi}d\mu(\pi):\int^{\oplus}_{\hat{G}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}d\mu(\pi)\to\int^{\oplus}_{\hat{G}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{J,\pi}d\mu(\pi).

Using the transitivity of the constant term (cf.
[
8
]
, Proposition 7.1) and taking adjoints, one gets:

i_{I}=i_{J}i_{I}^{J}.

(6.7)

6.3
L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}

Let us recall the definition of a
td
(abbreviation for twisted discrete series). If
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
, let
\chi_{\nu}\in{\hat{A}}_{I}^{0}
be the unitary character of
A_{I}^{0}
whose differential is equal to
\nu
. Recall our convention to replace the index
C
by
I
. If
w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{G,I}
,
w_{I}\in X_{I}
is defined in (
3.29
) and the
G
-orbit
Gw_{I}
identifies with
G/H_{I,w}
where
\underline{H}_{I,w}=w\underline{H}_{I}w^{-1}
(cf. (
3.33
)). Let
L^{2}(Gw_{I},\nu)
be the space of the induced representation to
G
of the unitary character of
H_{I,w}A_{I}^{0}
trivial on
H_{I,w}
and equal to
\chi_{\nu}
on
A^{0}_{I}
. Let

L^{2}(X_{I},\nu):=\oplus_{w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{G,I}}L^{2}(Gw_{I},\nu).

We define
L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)_{td}
as the Hilbert sum of the irreducible subrepresentations of
G
in
L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)
. Then
L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)_{td}
is a unitary representation of
G
which decomposes in a (possibly infinite) Hilbert sum of multiples of unitary irreducible representations of
G
. If
(\pi,{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi})\in\hat{G}
, one has
Hom_{G}({\mathcal{H}}_{\pi},L^{2}(Gw_{I},\nu))\subset Hom_{G}({\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{\infty},L^{2}(Gw_{I},\nu)^{\infty})

which is contained in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{-\infty,H_{I,w}}
. Hence the multiplicity of
\pi
in
L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)_{td}
is finite.

For
w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{G,I}
, by inducing by stage from
H_{w,I}
to
G
through
H_{w,I}A_{I}^{0}
, one gets:

L^{2}(Gw_{I})\simeq\int^{\oplus}_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}L^{2}(Gw_{I},\nu)d\nu.

Summing over
w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{G,I}
, one gets:

L^{2}(X_{I})\simeq\int^{\oplus}_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)d\nu.

(6.8)

By a proof similar to the proof of Proposition
5.10
, one gets

\mathbb{D}(\underline{X}_{I})
acts continuously on
L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)^{\infty}
,
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
.

(6.9)

and also on
L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)^{\infty}_{td}
as it commutes to the action of
G
.

6.6 Definition
.

A twisted discrete series (denoted by
td
) for
X_{I}
is an irreducible subrepresentation of
L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)_{td}
, for some
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
, such that
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
acts by a character on its space of
C^{\infty}
-vectors.

We will sees later:

For almost all
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
,
L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)_{td}
is the Hilbert sum of the td contained in it.

(6.10)

Let
I\subset S
.
FromÊ
[
34
]
, Proposition 8.14, one has:

The map
\mu_{I}
(cf. (
5.5
)) restricts to a map
\delta_{I}
from
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
to
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
and

\gamma_{\underline{X}}=\gamma_{\underline{X}_{I}}\circ{\delta_{I}}.

(6.11)

As
\mathfrak{a}_{X}
is a quotient of
\mathfrak{a}
,
\mathfrak{a}_{X}^{*}
identifies with a subspace of
\mathfrak{a}^{*}
. We will identify
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
to a subspace of
\mathfrak{a}_{X}
as follows. One introduces the little Weyl group
W_{X}
for
X
and the little Weyl group
W_{I}
for
X_{I}
, which act on
\mathfrak{a}_{X}^{*}
.
of
[
23
]
, section 9 ( see also
[
33
]
, Theorem 11.1). From
[
23
]
, Corollary 12.5,
W_{X}
(
W_{X_{I}}
is generated by the reflections around the simple spherical roots of
X
(resp.
X_{I}
) and is the Weyl group of a root system
<S>
(resp.
<I>
) whose basis is
S
(resp.
I
). But the simple spherical roots of
X_{I}
is precisely
I
(cf.
[
27
]
, Proposition 3.2 (4)).
Then
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
is equal to the space of invariant elements of
\mathfrak{a}_{X}
by
W_{I}
as it is the intersection of the kernels of the roots in
I
.
Using a
W_{I}
-invariant scalar product on
\mathfrak{a}_{X}
, one has an orthogonal decomposition:

\mathfrak{a}_{X}=\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{0}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{I}.

(6.12)

Actually one sees easily that
\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{0}
is the set of elements in
\mathfrak{a}_{X}

whose average under
W_{I}
is equal to zero, hence it does not depend on the scalar product. Hence one has canonically
\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}\subset\mathfrak{a}_{X}^{*}
.

One has
\mathfrak{j}\cap\mathfrak{h}=(\mathfrak{t}\cap\mathfrak{h})\oplus(\mathfrak{a}\cap\mathfrak{h})
as
(\mathfrak{m}\oplus\mathfrak{a})\cap\mathfrak{h}=(\mathfrak{m}\cap\mathfrak{h})\oplus(\mathfrak{a}\cap\mathfrak{h})
, which is seen by differentiating the result of
[
8
]
, Proposition B.2.

As
\mathfrak{a}_{\underline{X}}=(\mathfrak{j}/(\mathfrak{j}\cap\mathfrak{h})_{\mathbb{C}}
(cf. (
5.7
)), from (
6.12
) and the above canonical decomposition, one deduces a canonical identification of
\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
with a subspace of
\mathfrak{a}_{X}^{*}
hence of
\mathfrak{a}^{*}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{\underline{X}}^{*}\subset\underline{\mathfrak{j}}^{*}
.

(6.13)

We make the following conjecture, that we use in the sequel
, which is a slightly weaker real version of the Discrete Series Conjecture of Sakellaridis and Venkatesh
[
39
]
, section 9.4.6 in the
p
-adic case:

6.7 Conjecture
.

Let
I\subset S
. Let
i\mathfrak{a}_{I,conj}^{*}
be the set of
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
such that either
L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)_{td}=\{0\}
or a
td
contained in
L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)
has an infinitesimal character whose some Harish-Chandra parameter has an imaginary part equal to
\nu
. The conjecture asserts that the complementary set of
i\mathfrak{a}_{I,conj}^{*}
in
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
is of Lebesgue measure zero.

6.8 Remark
.

From Krötz, Kuit, Opdam, Schlichtkrull (cf.
[
30
]
, Theorem 1.1 (i)), the conjecture is true for
I=S
and
\mathfrak{a}_{S}=\{0\}
. From the real analogue of
[
39
]
, 9.4.11, it is true also for factorizable spaces with
I=S
(see l.c. 9.4.1 for the definition). Using the Unfolding (see l.c. section 9.5), it can be seen that it is true also for the real analogue of l.c. Examples 9.5.2, 9.5.3, 9.5.4.

I give some details to show that my conjecture is true in the factorizable case for
I=S
.

The group
A_{S}
might be viewed as a group of
G
-automorphism of
Z

there is no harm to assume that the connected center of
G
,
Z(G)^{0}
surjects onto
A_{S})
(
[
39
]
after Remark 2.1.1). We will do this in this part.
The upshot of the proof of 9.4.11 in the real case is the following.

There is a real vector space of unitary characters
\omega
,
D^{*}
, of
G
whose images by restriction to
Z(\underline{G})^{0}
admit a lift to characters of
\chi_{\omega}
of
A^{0}_{S}
. Moreover the image of
D^{*}
is surjective on the group of unitary characters of
A^{0}_{S}
.
Twisting by a suitable of
D^{*}
a given td, we get for a
td
in
L^{2}(X,0)
. But this is a discrete series for the quotient of
Z
by
A_{S}
. Hence, by
[
30
]
, it has a real infinitesimal character and satisfies the conjecture. Twisting by
\omega\in D^{*}
changes the infinitesimal character by
\omega
or
\chi_{\omega}
and they satisfy also the conjecture for
I=S
.

6.9 Lemma
.

There exists a finite union
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{I}
of
\underline{W}
-invariant lattices in
i\mathfrak{t}^{*}\oplus\mathfrak{a}^{*}
such that, for
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I,conj}^{*}
, the
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
-parameters of any
td
of
X_{I}
transforming under
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
by
\nu
is of the form
\Lambda+\nu
and
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{L}}_{I}:={\mathcal{L}}_{I}^{\prime}\cap(\rho_{\underline{X}}+\mathfrak{a}_{\underline{X}}^{*})\cap\mathfrak{a}_{I,C}^{\perp}
, where
\mathfrak{a}_{I,\mathbb{C}}^{\perp}
is the orthogonal of
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
in
\mathfrak{a}_{\underline{X}}^{*}
.

Proof.

First the action of
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
determines the parameter
\nu
on
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
. Hence a parameter of the
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
-eigenvalue of a
td
satisfying the hypothesis of the Lemma is of the form
\Lambda+\nu
with
\Lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{I,\mathbb{C}}^{\perp}\cap(\rho_{\underline{X}}+\mathfrak{a}_{\underline{X}}^{*})
.
But, by
[
30
]
, Theorem 1.1 (ii) and the above conjecture, a Harish-Chandra parameter of the infinitesimal character is of the form
\Lambda^{\prime}+\nu
with
\Lambda^{\prime}
in a finite union
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{I}
of lattices in
i\mathfrak{t}^{*}+\mathfrak{a}^{*}
invariant by
\underline{W}
: a finite union might be needed as there are several
G
-orbits in
X
. But from (
5.21
),
\Lambda+\nu
is a Harish-Chandra parameter of the infinitesimal character. By separating the imaginary parts of the Harish-Chandra parameters,
\nu
and
\nu+Im\Lambda
are
\underline{W}
-conjugate. Moreover
Im\Lambda
is orthogonal to
\nu
. For reasons of length,
Im\Lambda=0
and
\Lambda
is real. By separating the real parts of the Harish-Chandra parameters, one sees that
\Lambda
is
\underline{W}
-conjugated to
\Lambda^{\prime}
. By the
\underline{W}
-invariance of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{I}
, one sees that
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{L}}_{I}
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}_{I,td}:=\{\Lambda+\nu|\Lambda\in{\mathcal{L}}_{I},\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}\}\subset\rho_{\underline{X}}+{\mathfrak{a}_{\underline{X}}^{*}}
that we see as a borelian subset of the set of characters of
\mathfrak{Z}_{\mathbb{R}}(\underline{X}_{I})
.
Recall that we use Remark
5.16
for the spectral theory of
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
on
L^{2}(X_{I})
: this spectral theory follows from the spectral theory of a vector group that we denote
A_{\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I}})
whose action commutes with the action of
G
.

6.10 Proposition
.

Let
P_{I,td}
be the spectral projection for
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
in
L^{2}(X_{I})
corresponding to the subset
{\mathcal{A}}_{I,td}
of characters of
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
.

(i) For almost all
\nu
in
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
,
P_{I,td}L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)=L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)_{td}
and it is a Hilbert sum of
G
-invariant spaces whose spaces of
C^{\infty}
-vectors are eigenspaces for
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
. The spaces
L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)_{td}
vary measurably in
\nu
.

(ii) The image
L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}
of
P_{I,td}
is a

G\times A_{I}^{0}
-invariant closed subspace of
L^{2}(X_{I})
such that the isomorphism (
6.8
) induces an isomorphism:

L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}\simeq\int^{\oplus}_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)_{td}d\nu.

(6.14)

Moreover its space of smooth vectors is invariant by
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
.

If
O
is a
G
-orbit in
X_{I}
, we have similar results for
L^{2}(O)_{td}:=P_{I,td}L^{2}(O)
.

Proof.

One can reduce to prove the proposition for each
G
-orbit in
X_{I}
. Let us give the proof only when this orbit is
G/H_{I}
. Otherwise one has to change the base point. To simplify notation, we will do as if
X_{I}
is reduced to a single
G
-orbit.

First let us consider the spectral decomposition of
G\times\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
on
L^{2}(X_{I})
, following Proposition
5.15
(ii). Recall from (
5.8
), that the characters of
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
are parametrized by
\rho_{\underline{X}}+(\mathfrak{a}_{I,\mathbb{C}}^{\perp}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{I,\mathbb{C}}^{*})
. Notice that
\underline{W}_{{\underline{X}}_{I}}
acts trivially on
\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
(cf. Lemma
5.8
). As
A_{I}^{0}
is acting unitarily, the spectrum of
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
is contained in
\Omega_{I}+i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
, where
\Omega_{I}=(\rho_{\underline{X}}+\mathfrak{a}_{I,\mathbb{C}}^{\perp})/\underline{W}_{\underline{X}_{I}{}}.\\

There is a measure
\mu_{D}
on
{\hat{G}}\times(\Omega_{I}+i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*})
and, for
\mu_{D}
-almost all
(\pi,\Lambda+\nu)\in{\hat{G}}\times(\Omega_{I}+i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*})
, there is a subspace
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{I,\pi,\Lambda+\nu}
of
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{-\infty X_{I}}
, whose elements are eigenvectors for
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
for the parameter
\Lambda+\nu
, endowed with a scalar product
such that:

L^{2}(X_{I})\simeq\int^{\oplus}_{{\hat{G}}\times(\Omega_{I}+i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*})}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{I,\pi,\Lambda+\nu}d\mu_{D}(\pi,\Lambda+\nu).

such that
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{I,\pi,\Lambda+\nu}
is non zero for
\mu_{D}
-almost
(\pi,\Lambda+\nu)
. Hence there exists a measurable field
(\phi_{(\pi,\Lambda+\nu)})
in the above Hilbert integral such that
\|\phi_{(\pi,\Lambda+\nu)}\|=1
for
\mu_{D}
-almost
(\pi,\Lambda+\nu)
.

If
N\subset i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
has Lebesgue measure 0, from Proposition
9.1
applied to the Hilbert integral (
6.8
) and the groups
G
and
A_{I}^{0}
, one sees that the spectral projection for
A_{I}^{0}
corresponding to
N
is equal to zero. Applying again Proposition
9.1
to the above Hilbert integral, one sees that the spectral projection of
{\hat{G}}\times(\Omega_{I}+N)
is equal to zero. Computing the square norm of the measurable field
(\phi_{(\pi,\Lambda+\nu)})
restricted to
{\hat{G}}\times(\Omega_{I}+N)
one sees that the later is of
\mu_{D}
-measure zero. Hence the measure
\mu_{D}
admits a disintegration with respect to the projection of
{\hat{G}}\times(\Omega_{I}+i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*})\simeq{\hat{G}}\times\Omega_{I}\times i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
to
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}

into measures
\mu_{\nu}
,
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
.
Then

L^{2}(X_{I})\simeq\int^{\oplus}_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}\int^{\oplus}_{{\hat{G}}\times\Omega_{I}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{I,\pi,\Lambda+\nu}d\mu_{\nu}(\pi,\Lambda)d\nu

(6.15)

and
L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)
identifies with

\int^{\oplus}_{{\hat{G}}\times\Omega_{I}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{I,\pi,\Lambda+\nu}d\mu_{\nu}(\pi,\Lambda).

We fix
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{L}}_{I}
and we consider the spectral projection
P_{\Lambda}
for
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
on
L^{2}(X_{I})
with respect to the subset
\Lambda+i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
of
\Omega_{I}+i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
. On one hand, applying Proposition
9.1
to the Hilbert integral (
6.15
), first with
A_{I}^{0}
and
A_{\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})}
, and then to
G\times A_{\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})}

and
A_{\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})}
, one gets

P_{\Lambda}(L^{2}(X_{I}))\simeq\int^{\oplus}_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}\int^{\oplus}_{{\hat{G}}\times\{\Lambda\}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{I,\pi,\Lambda+\nu}d\mu_{\nu}(\pi,\Lambda)d\nu.

Hence for almost all
\nu
, using (
5.21
), one sees that
\mu_{\nu}
induces a measure on the finite set of (equivalence classes of) irreducible unitary representations
\pi
with infinitesimal character of Harish-Chandra parameter
\Lambda+\nu
. On the other hand, applying Proposition
9.1
to (
6.8
) for
A_{I}^{0}
and
A_{\mathfrak{Z}({\underline{X}}_{I})}
, one gets:

P_{\Lambda}(L^{2}(X_{I}))\simeq\int^{\oplus}_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}P_{\Lambda}(L^{2}(X_{I},\nu))d\nu.

Hence for almost all
\nu
:

P_{\Lambda}(L^{2}(X_{I},\nu))\simeq\int^{\oplus}_{{\hat{G}}\times\{\Lambda\}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{I,\pi,\Lambda+\nu}d\mu_{\nu}(\pi,\Lambda)

(6.16)

is a sum of irreducible unitary representations of
G
.
Hence for almost all
\nu
,

P_{\Lambda}(L^{2}(X_{I},\nu))\subset L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)_{td}.

(6.17)

Summing up over
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{L}}_{I}
, one sees that
P_{I,td}L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)\subset L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)_{td}
.
Let us prove the reverse inclusion. We observe that, from Lemma
5.9
,
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
acts on
L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)^{\infty}
. Moreover:

One has the analogous of Proposition
5.15
for
\mathbb{D}(\underline{X}_{I})
acting on
L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)^{\infty}
.

(6.18)

Let
C_{c}^{\infty}(X_{I},\nu)
the subspace of elements of
L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)^{\infty}
with compact support modulo
A_{I}
.
Let us show:

If
u\in\mathbb{D}(\underline{X}_{I})
is it is own formal adjoint as a differential operator on
C_{c}^{\infty}(X_{I})
, the same is true for
u
acting on
C_{c}^{\infty}(X_{I},\nu)
, for all
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
.

(6.19)

We prepare some notation. If
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
and
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(X_{I})
, let us define:

f_{\nu}(x)=\int_{A^{0}_{I}}f(xa_{I})a_{I}^{-\nu}da_{I}
. Then
f_{\nu}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(X_{I},\nu)
and using local sections of
G\to G/H_{I}A^{0}_{I}
one sees that the map
f\mapsto f_{\nu}
is surjective. We denote by
(.,.)
(resp
(.,.)_{\nu}
) the scalar product on
L^{2}(X_{I})
(resp.
L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)
).
Then one has for
f,g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(X_{I})
,

(f,g)=\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}(f_{\nu},g_{\nu})_{\nu}d\nu.

(6.20)

Moreover, by using Lebesgue dominated convergence, one sees that
\nu\mapsto(f_{\nu},g_{\nu})_{\nu}
is continuous and bounded. Let
u
as in (
6.19
)
and
\alpha\in C_{c}^{\infty}(A^{0}_{I})
. Then
(f\star\alpha)_{\nu}=\alpha_{\nu}f_{\nu}
where
\alpha_{\nu}=\int_{A^{0}_{I}}\alpha(a_{I})a_{I}^{-\nu}da_{I}
.
One has
(u(f\star\alpha),g)=(f\star\alpha,ug)
. As right translations by elements of
A^{0}_{I}
commutes to
\mathbb{D}(\underline{X}_{I})
(cf. (
5.6
)), one has
u(f\star\alpha)=(uf)\star\alpha
, hence
((uf)\star\alpha,g)=(f\star\alpha,ug)
. By differentiation under the integral, one sees that
(uf)_{\nu}=uf_{\nu}
. Then using (
6.20
) for
(uf\star\alpha,g)=(f\star\alpha,ug)
, one gets:

\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}\alpha_{\nu}[(uf_{\nu},g_{\nu})-(f_{\nu},ug_{\nu})]d\nu=0.

From the density of
C_{c}^{\infty}(A^{0}_{I})
in the Schwartz space of
A^{0}_{I}
, the same is true for its Fourier transform. Then, it follows from the last equality that for all
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
,
(uf_{\nu},g_{\nu})-(f_{\nu},ug_{\nu})
which proves (
6.19
).

Using (
6.18
), one sees that all
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
, one has a spectral decomposition for
G\times\mathfrak{Z}_{\mathbb{R}}(\underline{X}_{I})
acting on
L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)
. Hence the discrete part of the representation of
G
on
L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)
is a Hilbert sum of
td
(cf. Definition
6.6
). By Lemma
6.9
, for almost all
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
either
L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)_{td}=\{0\}
or the parameter of the
\mathfrak{Z}(X_{I})
-eigenvalues of these
td
are of the form
\Lambda+\nu
,
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{L}}_{I}
. This shows the reverse inclusion. Hence
P_{I,td}L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)_{td}=L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)_{td}
and this proves (i).

As
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
commutes to
G
and
A_{I}^{0}
, one gets the first part of (ii).

In order to get (
6.14
), one applies Proposition
9.1
to (
6.8
) for
A_{I}^{0}
and
A_{\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})}
.

Let
f\in L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}^{\infty}
. Then
f\in L^{2}(X_{I})^{\infty}
and for
D\in\mathfrak{Z}({\underline{X}}_{I})
,
Df\in L^{2}(X_{I})^{\infty}
(cf. Proposition
5.10
). Then, as
P_{I,td}
and
D
commute,
P_{I,td}Df=DP_{I,td}f=Df
.
Hence
Df\in L^{2}(X_{I})^{\infty}\cap ImP_{I,td}
. The later space is easily seen to be equal to
L^{2}(X_{I})^{\infty}_{td}
. This gives the second part of the proposition.

Recall that, from Proposition
5.17
, for
\mu
-almost all
\pi
,
\mathfrak{Z}({\underline{X}}_{I})
acts on
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}
.

6.11 Proposition
.

(i) For
\Lambda\in\Omega_{I},\nu\in\mathfrak{a}_{I,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
,
let
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\Lambda+\nu}
be the
\mathfrak{Z}({\underline{X}}_{I})
-eigenspace
of
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}
for the eigenvalue
\Lambda+\nu
.

For
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
, let
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\Lambda+\nu,td}:=\{\eta\in{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\Lambda+\nu}|\eta\in Hom_{G}({\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{\infty},L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)\}
. Then for almost all
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
, one has for
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{L}}_{I}
:

{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\Lambda+\nu}={\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\Lambda+\nu,td}.

Moreover, for almost all
\nu
in
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
, one has for
\Lambda\not\in{\mathcal{L}}_{I}
:

{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\Lambda+\nu,td}=\{0\}.

(ii) Let
O\in{\mathcal{O}}_{I}
and let
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,td}
(resp.
{\mathcal{M}}^{O}_{I,\pi,td}
) be equal to
P_{I,td}{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}
(resp.
P_{I,td}{\mathcal{M}}^{O}_{I,\pi}
). One has:

L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}\simeq\int^{\oplus}_{{\hat{G}}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,td}d\mu(\pi).

(6.21)

Proof.

(i) One deduces the first part of (i) from Proposition
6.10
(i).
The second part follows from Lemma
6.9
.

(ii) One deduces (ii) by using Proposition
6.10
(ii) and applying Proposition
9.1
to the groups
G
and
A_{\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})}
, to the spectral projection
P_{I,td}
and to the Plancherel decomposition of
L^{2}(X_{I})
.

6.12 Lemma
.

For
I\subset S
, let
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}=\{\eta\in{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,td}|\eta\in Hom_{G}({\mathcal{H}}_{\pi},L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)\}
.
There exists a subset
\hat{G}_{conj}
of
{\hat{G}}
whose complementary set is of
\mu
-measure zero such that:

For all
\pi\in\hat{G}_{conj}
, all
I\subset S
and all
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
such that
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}\not=0
, one has
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}^{*}_{I,conj}
.

Hence :

For all
\pi\in{\hat{G}}_{conj}
,

{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,td}=\oplus_{\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I,conj}^{*}}{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}
.

(6.22)

Proof.

Let us consider the spectral projection in
L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}
for
A_{I}^{0}
corresponding to
i\mathfrak{a}_{I,conj}^{*}
. As
i\mathfrak{a}_{I,conj}^{*}
has a complementary set in
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
of measure zero, from Conjecture
6.7
, this projection is equal to the identity. From Proposition
9.1
applied to the Hilbert integral (
6.21
), this implies that for
\mu
-almost all
\pi
, the spectral projection for
A_{I}^{0}
on
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,td}
corresponding to
i\mathfrak{a}_{I,conj}^{*}
is the identity.
This ensures the first assertion and the Lemma follows.

6.13 Proposition
.

There exists a disintegration of
L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}
in multiple of irreducible
G\times A_{I}^{0}
-represensations:

L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}=\int^{\oplus}_{{\hat{G}}\times i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi,\nu,td}d\mu^{I}(\pi,\nu),

with disintegrations of measures with respect with the two projections:

\mu^{I}=\int_{{\hat{G}}}\nu_{\pi}d\mu(\pi),\mu^{I}=\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}\mu_{\nu}d\nu,

such that
for
\mu
-almost all
\pi
, the measure
\nu_{\pi}
is the counting measure on the finite set of
\nu
such that
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}
is non zero.
Moreover
\mu_{\nu}
identifies with a measure on the countable set of
\pi
such hat
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}
is non zero.

If
f
is a function on
{\hat{G}}\times i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
, integrable for the measure
\mu^{I}
we denote

{\sum}^{\prime}_{\pi,{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}\not=\{0\}}f(\pi,\nu):=\mu_{\nu}(f).

We will write simply:

{\sum}^{\prime}_{\pi}f(\pi,\nu)
instead of
{\sum}^{\prime}_{\pi,{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}\not=\{0\}}f(\pi,\nu).

(ii) For any positive borelian function
\phi
on
{\hat{G}}\times i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
,
\pi\mapsto\sum_{\nu,{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}\not=0}\phi(\pi,\nu)
is
\mu
-measurable on
{\hat{G}}
,
\nu\mapsto{\sum}^{\prime}_{\pi}\phi(\pi,\nu)
is Lebesgue measurable on
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
and one has:

\int_{\hat{G}}\sum_{\nu,{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}\not=0}\phi(\pi,\nu)d\mu(\pi)=\int_{{\hat{G}}\times i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}\phi(\pi,\nu)d\mu^{I}(\pi,\nu)=\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}{\sum}^{\prime}_{\pi}\phi(\pi,\nu)d\nu.

(6.23)

Proof.

(i) From (
6.21
), one has.

L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}=\int_{{\hat{G}}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,td}d\mu(\pi).

If
\pi\in{\hat{G}}_{conj}
and
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
are such that
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}
is non zero, then
\nu\in{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}_{conj}
(cf. Lemma
6.12
) and from Conjecture
6.7
, it is conjugate under
\underline{W}
to the imaginary part of a Harish-Chandra parameter of the infinitesimal character of
\pi
.

From Lemma
6.12
, for
\mu
-almost all
\pi
,

{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,td}
is the Hilbert finite sum of the
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}

The first disintegration of
L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}
follows from Proposition
9.2
applied to the Hilbert integral (
6.21
) and the groups
G
and
A_{I}^{0}
, except for the existence of the disintegration of
\mu^{I}
with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
. Using Proposition
9.1
for
G
and
A_{I}^{0}
and the decomposition (
6.14
), one sees that the spectral projection corresponding to a neglectible set,
N
, of
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
is zero. This implies that
{\hat{G}}\times N
is of
\mu^{I}
-measure zero.
This ensures that the disintegration of
\mu^{I}
with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
exists. One proceeds similarly for the second disintegration of
\mu^{I}
.

(ii) follows from (i).

6.14 Lemma
.

(i) There exists an integer such that the number of unitary irreducible representations of
G
with a given infinitesimal character is bounded by this integer independent of the infinitesimal character.

(ii) Let us fix a
K
-type. There exists a constant such that for any unitary irreducible representation of
G
containing this
K
-type, the norm of the real part of the parameter of its infinitesimal character is bounded by this constant.

(iii) Let us fix a
K
-type. There exists an integer such that, for all
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}^{*}_{I,conj}
, the number of
\pi\in{\hat{G}}
containing this
K
-type and such that
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}\not=0
is bounded by this integer.

(iv) For all
\pi\in{\hat{G}}_{conj}
, the number of
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}^{*}_{I,conj}
such that
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}\not=0
is bounded by
Card\>\underline{W}
.

Proof.

(i) Let
\pi\in{\hat{G}}
with the given infinitesimal character. Using the Vogan classification, there is a standard parabolic subgroup
P^{\prime}
with Langlands decomposition
P^{\prime}=M^{\prime}A^{\prime}N^{\prime}
, a discrete series for
M^{\prime}
,
\delta
, with parameter of its infinitesimal character
\Lambda
,
\nu\in\mathfrak{a}^{\prime*}_{\mathbb{C}}

and a minimal
K
-type of the generalized principal series
\pi(\delta,\nu)
corresponding to these data, such that
\pi
is the subquotient of
\pi(\delta,\nu)
containing this
K
-type. Notice that there is a finite number of choices of
P^{\prime},M^{\prime},A^{\prime}
. Then
\Lambda+\nu
is in the orbit of the parameter of the selected infinitesimal character. Hence there is only finite possibilities for
\Lambda
and
\nu
whose number is bounded independently of the infinitesimal character. The number of discrete series of the connected component
M^{{}^{\prime}0}
of
M^{\prime}
with a given infinitesimal character is bounded independently of the infinitesimal character (see
[
19
]
, Theorems 12.21 and 9.20). To describe the discrete series of
M^{\prime}
, one has to describe the decomposition of a representation induced from a discrete series of
M^{{}^{\prime}0}
to
M^{\prime}
. There is at most
Card(M^{\prime}/M^{{}^{\prime}0})
irreducible subrepresentations. Then one has to choose a minimal
K
-type. The number of these minimal
K
-types is given by the number of elements of the
R
-group. But the
R
-group is a subgroup of the Weyl group
W(A^{\prime})
. This finishes to prove our claim.

(ii) Choose a
K
-type. Let us show that there is a finite number of data
(P^{\prime},\delta)
as above such that there exists
\nu\in\mathfrak{a}^{\prime*}_{\mathbb{C}}
such that
\pi(\delta,\nu)
contains this
K
-type. By Frobenius reciprocity,
\delta
has to contain an irreducible
K\cap M^{\prime}
-subrepresentation of the given
K
-type. But from
[
19
]
Theorems 12.21 and 9.20 c), the discrete series of
M^{\prime}
containing a given
K\cap M^{\prime}
-type have an infinetesimal character with bounded parameter. Hence their number is finite. Let
\pi
be a unitary irreducible representation containing this
K
-type. Then the Langlands classification asserts that there is a
(\delta,\nu)
as above such that
\pi
is a quotient of
