\pi(\delta,\nu)
. Then the real part of the infinitesimal character of
\pi
has a parameter equal to
\Lambda_{\delta}+Re\nu
, where
\Lambda_{\delta}
is a parameter of the infinitesimal character of
\delta
. Moreover the boundedness of the coefficients of
\pi
implies that the norm of
Re\nu
is bounded independently of
\pi
. This achieves to prove (ii).

(iii) Let
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I,conj}^{*}
. By (ii) the real part of the parameter of the infinitesimal character of a
\pi\in{\hat{G}}
containing this
K
-type and such that
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}\not=0
is bounded independently of
\nu
. But Conjecture
6.7
implies that
\nu
is the imaginary part of a parameter of the infinitesimal character. Moreover by
[
30
]
, Theorem 1.1, its real part is contained in a lattice and, by the above, is bounded. Hence the number of possibilities for the real part of infinitesimal character is bounded independently of
\nu
. Using (i) we get our claim.

(iv) Let
\pi\in{\hat{G}}_{conj}
. If
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}\not=0
, then from Lemma
6.12
,
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I,conj}^{*}
. From Conjecture
6.7
,
\nu\in
is
\underline{W}
-conjugated to the imaginary part of the Harish-Chandra parameter of the infinitesimal character of
\pi
. Our claim follows.

6.15 Lemma
.

Let
w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
,
O=Gw_{I}\simeq G/wH_{I}w^{-1}
,
O^{\prime}=Gw\simeq G/wHw^{-1}
. Let
\eta\in{\mathcal{M}}^{O}_{I,\pi,td}
be
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
-eigen for the eigenvalue
\Lambda+\nu
, with
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{L}}_{I}
and
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
. Then
j_{I,\pi}\eta
is a
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
-eigen element of
{\mathcal{M}}^{O^{\prime}}_{\pi}
for the eigenvalue
\Lambda+\nu
.

Proof.

First by changing the base point, one can reduce to
w=1
. By
\mathbb{C}
-linearity, it is enough to prove that for
u\in\mathfrak{Z}_{\mathbb{R}}(\underline{X})
, and
\eta
as above:

uj_{I,\pi}\eta=\gamma_{\underline{X}}(u)(\Lambda+\nu)j_{I,\pi}\eta.

Let us show:

The endomorphism of
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}
given by
\delta_{I}(u)
is selfadjoint.

(6.24)

Recall that
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}
is the linear span of
a.\eta^{\prime}_{I}=a.j^{*}_{\pi,I}\eta^{\prime}
,
a\in A_{I}^{0}
,
\eta^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
. Using Proposition
5.18
, and the definition of
\delta_{I}
(cf. (
6.11
)), one has:

\delta_{I}(u)j^{*}_{I,\pi}=j_{I,\pi}^{*}u.

(6.25)

As
u
is selfadjoint the eigenvalues of
u
on
{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
are real. If
\eta^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
is an eigenvector for
u
, from the above relation one sees that
\eta^{\prime}_{I}
is an eigenvector for a real eigenvalue of
\delta_{I}(u)
. But the actions of
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
and
A_{I}^{0}
commute. Hence it follows that
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}
is a sum of eigenspaces for real eigenvalues of
\delta_{I}(u)
. As different eigenspaces for
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
are orthogonal (cf. Proposition
5.17
), one sees that the eigenspaces of
\delta_{I}(u)
are orthogonal. This achieves to prove our claim.

Taking adjoints in (
6.25
) and using (
6.24
), one gets:

j_{I,\pi}\delta_{I}(u)=uj_{I,\pi}

Applying this to
\eta
, one gets the Lemma from (
6.11
).

6.16 Lemma
.

Let
O=Gw
be a
G
-orbit in
X
and
\eta\in{\mathcal{M}}^{O}_{\pi,td}
. Let us assume that
\eta
has an eigenvalue under
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
of parameter
\lambda\in\rho+\mathfrak{a}_{\underline{X}}^{*}
. With the notation of (
3.32
) where we replace
C
by
I=I(C)
, let
O^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{I}
. If
O^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{I,O}
then the component
\eta^{O^{\prime}}_{I}
of
\eta_{I}
, in the decomposition (
4.1
) for
X_{I}
, is a sum of generalized eigenvectors for
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
with eigenvalues among the set
\{\mathfrak{w}\lambda|\mathfrak{w}\in\underline{W}_{\underline{X}},w\lambda_{|\mathfrak{a}_{I}}\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}\}
. Otherwise it is zero.

Proof.

If one has a generalized eigencomponent of
\eta_{I}^{O^{\prime}}
for a character of
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
, this character determines by composition with
\delta_{I}
, a character of
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
. We denote by
\lambda^{\prime}
a parameter of this character of
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
. From Proposition
5.18
and (
6.11
), this character of
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
is equal to the character with parameter
\lambda
. Hence
\lambda^{\prime}
and
\lambda
are
\underline{W}_{\underline{X}}
-conjugate. But, as
\eta_{I}
is tempered,
\lambda^{\prime}_{|\mathfrak{a}_{I}}\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
. This proves the first part of the Lemma. If
O^{\prime}\not\in{\mathcal{O}}_{I,O}
, it is zero by Theorem
5.1
(ii).

The same proof applies to functions and give:

6.17 Corollary
.

Let
O\in{\mathcal{O}}
,
O^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{I}
.
Let
f\in{\mathcal{A}}_{temp}(O)
be an eigenfunction for
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X})
, for the eigenvalue of parameter
\lambda\in\rho_{\underline{X}}+\mathfrak{a}_{\underline{X}}^{*}
. Then the restriction
f^{O^{\prime}}_{I}
to
O^{\prime}
of
f_{I}
is zero if
O^{\prime}\notin{\mathcal{O}}_{I,O}
. Otherwise it is a sum of generalized
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
-eigenfunctions for the eigenvalues of paramaters
\mathfrak{w}\lambda,\mathfrak{w}\in\underline{W}_{\underline{X}}
with
\mathfrak{w}\lambda_{|\mathfrak{a}_{I}}\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
.

6.18 Lemma
.

We use the notation of Proposition
6.11
.

For
\mu
-almost all
\pi\in{\hat{G}}
, one has:

(i) every
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
-weight in
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,td}
is regular for the roots in
I
.

(ii) the space
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,td}
is a sum of eigenspaces for
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
, hence for
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
.

Proof.

(i) Simply use the spectral projection on
L^{2}(X_{I})
corresponding to the set of singular elements in
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
. It is zero by (
6.14
). Then apply Proposition
9.1
to the Hilbert integral (
6.21
) to the groups
G
and
A_{I}^{0}
.

(ii) follows from the definition of
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,td}
(cf. Proposition
6.11
).

6.19 Definition
.

Let
I,J\subset S
. Let
W_{J,I}
(resp.
\underline{W}_{J,I}
) be the set of bijective linear maps
i\mathfrak{a}_{J}^{*}\to i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
which are restrictions of elements of
W_{X}
(resp.
\underline{W}_{\underline{X}}
).

We identify
\mathfrak{a}_{X}^{*}
with
\mathfrak{a}_{X}
by using a
W_{X}
-invariant inner product. For
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{X}^{*}
and
\alpha\in S
, the sign of
\alpha(\lambda)
does not depend on the choice of the scalar product. Recall (cf. (
6.13
)) that
\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
is viewed canonically as a subspace
\mathfrak{a}_{X}^{*}
. In the above identification of
\mathfrak{a}_{X}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{X}^{*}
,
\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
identifies with
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
. Moreover
(\mathfrak{a}_{I})^{*--}:=\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{X}^{*}|\alpha(\lambda)=0,\alpha\in I,\alpha(\lambda)<0,\alpha\in S\setminus I\}
does not depend on the choice of the scalar product and identifies with
\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{--}
.

(6.26)

6.20 Lemma
.

Let
I,J\subset S
.

(i) An element of
W_{J,I}
is the restriction to
i\mathfrak{a}_{J}^{*}
of an element
\mathfrak{w}\in W_{X}
such that
\mathfrak{w}J=I
. We denote by
J\approx I
if such an element exists.

(ii)The union

\cup_{J,\mathfrak{w}\in W_{J,I}}i\mathfrak{w}\mathfrak{a}_{J}^{*--}

is disjoint, dense and of complementary set of Lebesgue measure zero in
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
.

(iii)

W_{J,I}=\underline{W}_{J,I}.

Proof.

(i)
Let
\mathfrak{u}
be an element of
W_{X}
which conjugate
i\mathfrak{a}_{J}^{*}
to
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
. Using the notation of (
5.8
), let
<I>
(resp.
<J>
) be the subroot system of
<S>
generated by
I
(resp.
J
). Then
\mathfrak{u}
conjugate these root systems. Then
\mathfrak{u}J
is a basis of
<I>
which is conjugate to
I
by an element
\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}
of
W_{I}
. Then
\mathfrak{w}=\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}\mathfrak{u}
has the same restriction to
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
than
\mathfrak{u}
, as
\mathfrak{u}
acts trivially on
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
. Moreover
\mathfrak{w}J=I
. This proves (i).

(ii) follows from the perfect tiling property of
[
39
]
, beginning of section 7.3.

(iii) Ê(
5.8
) implies that the elements of
W_{X}
are given by the restriction to
\mathfrak{a}_{X}
of certain elements of
\underline{W}_{\underline{X}}
. Hence
W_{J,I}
is a subset of
\underline{W}_{J,I}
. Let us show that :

the union of open sets in
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
,
\cup_{J,w\in\underline{W}_{J,I}}i\mathfrak{a}_{J}^{--}
is disjoint.

(6.27)

Let
\tilde{I}
( resp.
\tilde{J}
) be the set of elements of
\underline{S}
vanishing on
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
(resp.
\mathfrak{a}_{J}
). Let
\mathfrak{a}_{\tilde{I}}:=\{\underline{x}\in\mathfrak{a}_{\underline{X}}|\alpha(\underline{x})=0,\alpha\in\tilde{I}\}
. The set of roots in
<\underline{S}>
which vanish on
\mathfrak{a}_{\tilde{I}}
is easily seen to be a subsystem of
<\underline{S}>
that we denote by
<\tilde{I}>
. Let us show that its basis determined by the set positive roots of
<\underline{S}>
intersected with
<\tilde{I}>
is equal to
\tilde{I}
. In fact a positive root
\alpha
of
<\tilde{I}>
is in particular a positive root for
<\underline{S}>
, hence it is a linear combination with positive integers of certain elements of
\underline{S}
. By the above property of
S
, the restriction to
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
of elements of
\underline{S}\setminus\tilde{I}
to
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
are elements of
S\setminus I
hence negative on
\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{--}
. Hence
\alpha
is a linear combination with positive integers of certain elements of
\tilde{I}
. This implies our claim.

We have similar notation and result for
J
.
If
\mathfrak{w}\in\underline{W}_{J,I}
,
\mathfrak{w}
is the restriction of an element
\tilde{\mathfrak{w}}\in\underline{W}_{\underline{X}}
. Then
\mathfrak{w}(i\mathfrak{a}_{J})=i\mathfrak{a}_{I}
which implies
\tilde{\mathfrak{w}}(i\mathfrak{a}_{\tilde{J}}^{*})=i\mathfrak{a}_{\tilde{I}}^{*}
. Hence
\tilde{\mathfrak{w}}\in\underline{W}_{\tilde{J},\tilde{I}}
.
As
\mathfrak{a}_{J}^{--}\subset\mathfrak{a}_{\tilde{J}}^{-}-
, (
6.27
) follows from the perfect tiling property for
\underline{W}_{\underline{X}}
.

From the density in (ii), this shows that the union in (
6.27
) is the same as the one (ii). (iii) follows.

Let
{\mathcal{C}}
be a measurable subset of
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
. One can consider the spectral projections
P_{\mathfrak{a}_{I},{\mathcal{C}}}
(or simply
P_{\mathcal{C}}
if there is no ambiguity) corresponding to
{\mathcal{C}}
, for which we often forget the representation on which it acts. From (
6.14
) and Proposition
9.1
, one has:

P_{\mathcal{C}}(L^{2}(X_{I})_{td})=\int^{\oplus}_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}P_{\mathcal{C}}(L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)_{td})d\nu.

Notice that
P_{\mathcal{C}}
also acts on
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,td}
in the Plancherel formula for
X_{I}
by the identity on
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}
tensored with a projection still denoted
P_{\mathcal{C}}
defined on
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,td}
.

Recall (cf. (
6.13
)), that
\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
identifies with a subspace of
\mathfrak{a}^{*}_{X}\subset\mathfrak{a}^{*}_{\underline{X}}
.

6.21 Lemma
.

(i) There exists a finite family of disjoint measurable subsets of
{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}_{conj}
,
{\mathcal{C}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{C}}_{n}
in
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
such that:

(a) The complementary set in
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
of their union denoted
{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}_{,til}
is of Lebesgue measure zero.

(b) Each
{\mathcal{C}}_{j}
is contained in
i
times a Weyl chamber for
\underline{W}
, the Weyl group of
(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{j}_{\mathbb{C}})
.

(c) For each
{\mathcal{C}}_{j}
, there exist
J\subset S
with
J\approx I
and
\mathfrak{w}\in W_{J,I}
such that
{\mathcal{C}}_{j}\subset\mathfrak{w}i\mathfrak{a}_{J}^{*--}
.
We denote by
{\mathcal{A}}_{j}
the set of
\pi\in{\hat{G}}_{conj}
such that
P_{{\mathcal{C}}_{j}}{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,td}
is
non zero (cf. Conjecture
6.7
and Lemma
6.12
for notation). Then for all
\pi
in
{\mathcal{A}}_{j}
,
P_{{\mathcal{C}}_{j}}{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,td}
has exactly one
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
-exponent that we denote by
\nu_{\pi,j}
. Moreover
{\mathcal{A}}_{j}
is
\mu
-measurable and one has

P_{{\mathcal{C}}_{j}}(L^{2}(X_{I})_{td})=\int_{{\mathcal{A}}_{j}}^{\oplus}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu_{\pi,j},td}d\mu(\pi).

(6.28)

Moreover
\pi\mapsto\nu_{\pi,j}
is measurable on
{\mathcal{A}}_{I}
.

(ii) For all
\pi\in{\mathcal{A}}_{j}
,
i_{I,\pi}
restricted to
P_{{\mathcal{C}}_{j,td}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi,\nu_{\pi,j},td}
is an isometry.

(iii) For all
j
,
i_{I}
is an isometry when restricted to
P_{{\mathcal{C}}_{j}}L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}
.

Proof.

(i) One uses a covering of
\mathfrak{a}_{X}^{*}
by intersections of Weyl chambers for
\underline{W}
in
\mathfrak{a}^{*}
with Weyl chambers for
\underline{W}_{\underline{X}}
in
\mathfrak{a}^{*}_{\underline{X}}
. From this, we can construct a covering of
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
by measurable sets included in
i
times of one of these Weyl chambers and then a partition of
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
by measurable cones included in
i
-times of one of these Weyl chambers.
Now we consider the intersections of these sets with

{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}_{conj}
and
the

\mathfrak{w}i\mathfrak{a}_{J}^{*--}
for
J\approx I
and
\mathfrak{w}\in W_{J,I}
. These sets have the required properties.

Let
{\mathcal{C}}_{j}
be one of these sets. Let
\pi
be in
{\mathcal{A}}_{j}
.
From Lemma
6.9
, the possible
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
-eigenvalues of non zero elements of
P_{{\mathcal{C}}_{j}}{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,td}
have parameters of the form
\Lambda+\nu
with
\nu\in{\mathcal{C}}_{j}
and
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{L}}_{I}
. From (
5.21
),
\Lambda+\nu
is a Harish-Chandra parameter of the infinitesimal character of
\pi
. As
\Lambda
is real and
\nu
imaginary, all such
\nu
would be
\underline{W}
-conjugated.
As
i{\mathcal{C}}_{j}
is contained in a Weyl chamber for
\underline{W}
, there is at most one such
\nu
. We call it
\nu_{\pi,j}
. Our claim on the measurability of
{\mathcal{A}}_{j}
comes from the
\mu
-measurability of
{\hat{G}}_{conj}
(cf. Lemma
6.12
) and the
\mu
-measurability in
\pi
of the projectors
P_{{\mathcal{C}}_{j},\pi}
as it follows from Proposition
9.1
applied to the Hilbert integral (
6.21
) and the groups
G
and
A_{I}^{0}
. Then, the fact that
{\hat{G}}_{conj}
has a complementary set in
{\hat{G}}
of
\mu
-measure zero (cf. Lemma
6.12
) and (
6.22
) imply (
6.28
).
To get the last claim of (i), one remarks that the action of any
\underline{x}
in
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
on this Hilbert integral is given by the multiplication by the function

\pi\mapsto\nu_{\pi,j}(\underline{x})
.

(ii) It follows from Theorem
6.3
and from (i) that
i_{I,\pi}
restricted to
P_{{\mathcal{C}}_{j}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi,\nu_{\pi,j},td}
is an isometry for almost all
\pi
. This implies (ii) and (iii).

As
({\mathcal{C}}_{j})
is a partition of
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
, up to a set of Lebesgue measure 0, (
6.28
) implies the equality of
G\times\mathfrak{a}_{I}
- representations:

L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}=\oplus_{j=1,\dots,n}\int^{\oplus}_{{\mathcal{A}}_{j}\subset{\hat{G}}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu_{\pi,j},td}d\mu(\pi).

(6.29)

Notice that there is no reason for the
{\mathcal{A}}_{j}
to be disjoints.

7
Scattering operators

7.1
Composition of Bernstein maps and their adjoints

7.1 Definition
.

Let
i_{I,td}
be the restriction of
i_{I}
(cf. (
6.5
)) to

\int^{\oplus}_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,td}d\nu
.
Using the isomorphism
\overline{\alpha}_{I}
of Theorem
6.1
to identify the source of
i_{I}
to
L^{2}(X_{I})
, and (
6.21
) to identify the source of
i_{I,td}
to
L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}
, its adjoint
i_{I,td}^{*}
is the composition of
i_{I}^{*}
, from
L^{2}(X)
to
L^{2}(X_{I})
, with the orthogonal projection of

L^{2}(X_{I})
onto
L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}
.

7.2 Lemma
.

Let
I,J\subset S
.
If
\pi\in{\hat{G}}
, let
i_{I,\pi,td}
be the restriction of
i_{I,\pi}
to
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,td}
. Let
i^{*}_{J,\pi,td}
be the adjoint of
i_{J,\pi,td}
. One defines similarly
j_{I,\pi,td}
and
j^{*}_{J,\pi,td}
.

(i) One has:

i_{I,td}=\int^{\oplus}_{\pi\in{\hat{G}}}i_{I,\pi,td}d\mu(\pi),i_{J,td}^{*}=\int^{\oplus}_{\pi\in{\hat{G}}}i^{*}_{J,\pi,td}d\mu(\pi).

(ii) Let
\pi\in\hat{G}_{conj}
(cf. Lemma
6.12
for notation). Let
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
and
\eta\in{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,td}
of
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
-weight
\nu
. If
\eta^{\prime}:=j^{*}_{J,\pi,td}j_{I,\pi,td}\eta
is non zero, then any of its
\mathfrak{a}_{J}
-weight,
\nu^{\prime}
, is of the form
\mathfrak{w}\nu
for some
\mathfrak{w}
in
\underline{W}_{\underline{X}}
.

(iii) Let the notation be as in (ii). For almost all
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
, either
j^{*}_{J,\pi,td}j_{I,\pi,td}{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}=0
or for all
\nu^{\prime}\in i\mathfrak{a}_{J}^{*}
such that there exists a non zero
\mathfrak{a}_{J}
-weight vector in
j^{*}_{J,\pi,td}j_{I,\pi,td}{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}
with weight
\nu^{\prime}
, one has
\nu^{\prime}=\mathfrak{w}\nu
for some
\mathfrak{w}\in\underline{W}_{\underline{X}}
such that
\mathfrak{w}i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}=i\mathfrak{a}_{J}^{*}
.
We denote by
{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}_{,J}
the set of such
\nu
, whose complementary set in
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
is of Lebesgue measure zero.

Proof.

(i) The first equality of (i) follows from (
6.21
) and Definition
7.1
. The second is obtained by adjunction.

(ii) By linearity we may assume that

\eta
is
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
-eigen. As
\pi\in{\hat{G}}_{conj}
, one has
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}^{*}_{I,conj}
and from Lemma
6.9
,
\eta
transforms under
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{I})
by a character with parameter
\Lambda+\nu
for some
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{L}}_{I}
.

We want to look to the possible
\mathfrak{a}_{J}
-weights of
\eta^{\prime}
. As
\pi\in{\hat{G}}_{conj}
, one has
\nu^{\prime}\in i\mathfrak{a}_{J,conj}^{*}
(cf. Lemma
6.12
). From Lemmas
6.15
and
6.16
, one sees that the possible
\mathfrak{Z}(\underline{X}_{J})
-generalized eigenvalues of
\eta^{\prime}

are of parameters among the
\mathfrak{w}(\Lambda+\nu)
,
\mathfrak{w}\in\underline{W}_{\underline{X}}
. But as
\pi\in{\hat{G}}_{conj}
, one deduces from Lemma
6.12
, Conjecture
6.7
and Lemma
6.9
that one of this parameter might be of the form
\Lambda^{\prime}+\nu^{\prime}
,
\Lambda^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{J}
. As
\underline{W}_{\underline{X}}
is a subgroup of
\underline{W}
(cf. (
5.8
)), it preserves imaginary parts. Hence
\nu^{\prime}=\mathfrak{w}\nu
. This achieves the proof of (ii).

(iii) Let
\nu
,
\nu^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{w}
as in (ii). Let us assume that
\mathfrak{w}i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}\not=i\mathfrak{a}_{J}^{*}
. Then
\nu
is element of
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}\cap\mathfrak{w}^{-1}i\mathfrak{a}_{J}^{*}
which of zero measure.

7.3 Proposition
.

For
\mu
-almost all
\pi\in{\hat{G}}
:

(i) If
I
is not
W_{X}
conjugate to
J
, then
i^{*}_{J,\pi,td}i_{I,\pi,td}=0
.

(ii) If
I\approx J
, if
\nu\in\mathfrak{a}_{J}^{*}
either
j^{*}_{J,\pi,td}j_{I,\pi,td}{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}=0
or for any weight
\mathfrak{a}_{J}
-weight
\nu^{\prime}\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
of
j^{*}_{J,\pi,td}j_{I,\pi,td}{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}
,
\nu^{\prime}=\mathfrak{w}\nu
for some
\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}
.

(iii) If
i^{*}_{J,td}i_{I,td}
is non zero then
I\approx J
.

Proof.

Let
P_{I,J}
be the spectral projection for
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
acting on
L^{2}(X_{I},\nu)
corresponding to the set
i\mathfrak{a}_{I,J}^{*}
of Lemma
7.2
(iii). It is equal to the idendity.
By using Proposition
9.1
and using Lemma
7.2
(iii), (i) follows from Lemma
6.20
(iii) and (i). Similarly (ii) follows if one takes into account Lemma
6.20
(iii). Then (iii) is an immediate consequence of (i).

7.4 Proposition
.

(i) Let
I\subset S
and let
J\subset S
minimal, if it exists, for the property
i^{*}_{J}i_{I,td}\not=0
. Then

i^{*}_{J,td}i_{I,td}=i^{*}_{J}i_{I,td}\not=0.

(ii) Let
I,J\subset S
such that
i^{*}_{J}i_{I,td}\not=0
. There exists
J^{\prime}\subset J
such that
i^{*}_{J^{\prime},td}i_{I,td}=i^{*}_{J^{\prime}}i_{I,td}\not=0
. In particular
I\approx J^{\prime}
. Moreover
CardJ>CardI
or
I\approx J
.

Proof.

(i) Let
J^{\prime}
be a strict subset of
J
. Let us use the notation of (
6.7
). From the hypothesis, the characterization of
td
(cf.
[
8
]
, Theorem 6.12) and the transitivity of the constant term, one has:

(i^{J}_{J^{\prime},\pi})^{*}i^{*}_{J,\pi}i_{I,\pi,td}=i^{*}_{J^{\prime},\pi}i_{I,\pi,td}=0

for almost all
\pi\in{\hat{G}}
. Then
(i) follows from the the characterization of
td
(cf. l.c.).

(ii) Let
J^{\prime}
be a minimal subset of
J
such that
i^{*}_{J^{\prime}}(i_{I})_{td}\not=0
. Then, from (i),
J^{\prime}
has the required property. Then from Proposition
7.3
,
J^{\prime}\approx I
. The last assertion follows immediately.

7.5 Proposition
.

Let
I,J\subset S
. We will denote by
W_{I,J}
the set of maps
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}\to i\mathfrak{a}_{J}^{*}
of the form
\nu\mapsto\mathfrak{w}\nu_{|\mathfrak{a}_{J}}
, where
\mathfrak{w}\in\cup_{J^{\prime}}W_{I,J^{\prime}}
with
J^{\prime}\subset J
and
J^{\prime}\approx I
.

(i) For
\mu
-almost all
\pi\in{\hat{G}}
and all
\eta\in{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,td}
,

j^{*}_{J,\pi}j_{I,\pi,td}\eta
is zero or its
\mathfrak{a}_{J}
-exponents are among the
\mathfrak{w}\nu
, where
\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}
and
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
is such that
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}\not=0
.

(ii) For
\mu
-almost all
\pi\in{\hat{G}}
and all
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
, if
v_{\pi,\nu}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}
,
i^{*}_{J,\pi}i_{I,\pi,td}v_{\pi,\nu}
is zero or its
\mathfrak{a}_{J}
-exponents are of the form
\mathfrak{w}\nu,\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}
.

Notice that if
W_{I,J}
is not empty, one has
CardJ>CardI
or
I\approx J
.

Proof.

(i) Let
J^{\prime}\subset S
. From Lemma
7.2
, one has:

i^{*}_{J^{\prime},td}i_{I,td}=\int_{{\hat{G}}}^{\oplus}i^{*}_{J^{\prime},\pi,td}i_{I,\pi,td}d\mu(\pi).

It follows from Proposition
7.3
(i) that for
\mu
-almost all
\pi
in
\hat{G}
and
J^{\prime}\subset S
which is not
W_{X}
conjugate to
I
, one has
i_{J^{\prime},\pi,td}i_{I,\pi,td}=0
.

And from Proposition
7.3
(ii), for
\mu
-almost all
\pi
in
\hat{G}
and
J^{\prime}\approx I
, if
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
and
\eta\in{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}
, then any
\mathfrak{a}_{J^{\prime}}
-weight
\nu_{1}
of
i_{J^{\prime},\pi,td}i_{I,\pi,td}\eta
is of the form
\mathfrak{w}\nu
for some
\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J^{\prime}}
.

Let
\pi
be an element satisfying the two above conditions.
Let
\eta\in{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}
such that
j^{*}_{J,\pi}j_{I,\pi,td}\eta
is non zero. Let
\nu_{1}
be an
\mathfrak{a}_{J}
-weight of
j^{*}_{J,\pi}j_{I,\pi,td}\eta
and let
\eta_{1}
be the corresponding weight component.
As in the proof of the preceding Proposition, one finds
J^{\prime}\subset J
such that
\eta^{\prime}=(i^{J}_{J^{\prime},\pi,td})^{*}\eta_{1}
is non zero. But
\eta^{\prime}
is the
\mathfrak{a}_{J}
-weight component for the weight
\nu_{1}
of

i^{*}_{J^{\prime},\pi,td}i_{I,\pi,td}\eta
as the later is equal to
(i^{J}_{J^{\prime},\pi,td})^{*}i^{*}_{J,\pi}i_{I,\pi,td}\eta
. Then from our choice of
\pi
, one has
J^{\prime}\approx I
. Moreover
\nu_{1}
is the restriction to
\mathfrak{a}_{J}
of an
\mathfrak{a}_{J^{\prime}}
-weight
\nu^{\prime}
of
i^{*}_{J^{\prime},\pi,td}i_{I,\pi,td}\eta
. But from our choice of
\pi
,

\nu^{\prime}
is of the form
\mathfrak{w}\nu
for some
\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J^{\prime}}
. (i) follows.

(ii) follows from (i).

7.2
Definition of scattering operators

7.6 Theorem
.

Let
I,J\subset S
such that
I\approx J
. There is a decomposition

i^{*}_{J}i_{I,td}=i^{*}_{J,td}i_{I,td}=\sum_{\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}}S_{\mathfrak{w}},

where the
S_{\mathfrak{w}}
are continuous
G
-morphisms from
L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}
to
L^{2}(X_{J})_{td}
, such that, for the normalized right action of
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{J}
, denoted
r
, one has:

S_{\mathfrak{w}}r_{\underline{x}}f=r_{\mathfrak{w}\underline{x}}S_{\mathfrak{w}}f,\underline{x}\in\mathfrak{a}_{I},f\in L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}.

This decomposition is unique.

Proof.

It follows from the tiling property for
i\mathfrak{a}_{J}^{*}
that:

For
j
given, the union
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{j}:=\cup_{\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}}\mathfrak{w}{\mathcal{C}}_{j}\subset i\mathfrak{a}_{J}^{*}
is disjoint.

(7.1)

Let
j
be given. Let us introduce the spectral projection
P_{{\mathcal{C}}_{j}}
(resp.
P_{\mathfrak{w}{\mathcal{C}}_{j}}
,
P_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{j}}
) for
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
(resp.
\mathfrak{a}_{J}
) on
L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}
(resp.
L^{2}(X_{J})_{td}
) corresponding to
{\mathcal{C}}_{j}
(resp.
\mathfrak{w}{\mathcal{C}}_{j}
,
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{j}
). One has:

L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}=\oplus_{j}P_{{\mathcal{C}}_{j}}L^{2}(X_{I})_{td},P_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{j}}L^{2}(X_{J})_{td}=\oplus_{\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}}P_{\mathfrak{w}{\mathcal{C}}_{j}}L^{2}(X_{J})_{td}.

(7.2)

Let us show that

i^{*}_{J,td}i_{I,td}P_{{\mathcal{C}}_{j}}L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}\subset P_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{j}}L^{2}(X_{J})_{td}.

From Proposition
9.1
applied to the Hilbert integral (
6.21
) and to the groups
G
and
A_{I}^{0}
, it is enough to prove that, for
\mu
-almost all
\pi\in{\hat{G}}
, the image of

i^{*}_{J,td}i_{I,td}P_{{\mathcal{C}}_{j}}{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,td}
is a subspace of
P_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{j}}{\mathcal{M}}_{J,\pi,td}
. But this follows from Proposition
7.3
(ii).
Then one has

i^{*}_{J,td}{i_{I,td}}P_{{\mathcal{C}}_{j}}=\sum_{\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}}S_{\mathfrak{w},j},

where

S_{\mathfrak{w},j}=P_{\mathfrak{w}{\mathcal{C}}_{j}}i^{*}_{J,td}i_{I,td}P_{{\mathcal{C}}_{j}}.

(7.3)

It is clear that the
S_{\mathfrak{w},j}
are continuous
G
-morphisms which disintegrate as
\int^{\oplus}_{\hat{G}}S_{\mathfrak{w},j,\pi}d\mu(\pi)
. Similarly, for
\underline{x}\in\mathfrak{a}_{I}
,
r_{\underline{x}}
disintegrates as
\int^{\oplus}_{{\hat{G}}}r_{\underline{x},\pi}d\mu(\pi)
.

Let
\pi\in{\hat{G}}
and
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
such that the image
{\mathcal{M}}_{j,I,\pi,\nu,td}
of
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}
by
P_{{\mathcal{C}}_{j}}
is non zero. If
v_{\pi}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{j,I,\pi,\nu,td},v_{\pi}\not=0
, one has

S_{\mathfrak{w},j,\pi}r_{\underline{x},\pi}v_{\pi}=\nu(\underline{x})S_{\mathfrak{w},j,\pi}v_{\pi},\underline{x}\in\mathfrak{a}_{I}.

From Lemma
6.21
, one knows that
P_{{\mathcal{C}}_{j}}{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,td}
has at most an exponent which is necessarily equal to
\nu
. Then from Proposition
7.3
(ii), one sees that, for
\mu
-almost all
\pi
,
S_{\mathfrak{w},j,\pi}v_{\pi}
is of weight
\mathfrak{w}\nu
for
\mathfrak{a}_{J}
. Hence one has

r_{\mathfrak{w}\underline{x},\pi}S_{\mathfrak{w},j,\pi}v_{\pi}=\mathfrak{w}\nu(\mathfrak{w}\underline{x})S_{\mathfrak{w},j,\pi}v_{\pi}=\nu(\underline{x})S_{\mathfrak{w},j,\pi}v_{\pi}=S_{\mathfrak{w},j,\pi}r_{\underline{x},\pi}v_{\pi}.

(7.4)

From this it follows, by integrating over
\pi
, that:

S_{\mathfrak{w},j}r_{\underline{x}}f=r_{\mathfrak{w}\underline{x}}S_{\mathfrak{w},j}f,\underline{x}\in\mathfrak{a}_{I},f\in P_{{\mathcal{C}}_{j}}L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}.

Then (
7.2
) allows us to define

S_{\mathfrak{w}}:=\sum_{j}S_{\mathfrak{w},j}.

(7.5)

which are continuous
G
-morphisms from
L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}
to
L^{2}(X_{J})_{td}
. It has the covariance property of the theorem.

Also, one sees easily that
i^{*}_{J}i_{I,td}=i^{*}_{J,td}i_{I,td}
is the sum of the
S_{\mathfrak{w}}
. Let us consider an other decomposition of
i^{*}_{J}i_{I,td}=i^{*}_{J,td}i_{I,td}
as a sum of
S^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{w}}
.
From the covariance property of
S^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{w}}
, one sees that
P_{\mathfrak{w}{\mathcal{C}}_{j}}i^{*}_{J,td}i_{I,td}P_{{\mathcal{C}}_{j}}=S^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{w}}P_{{\mathcal{C}}_{j}}
and, by definition, it is equal to
S_{\mathfrak{w},j}
. Summing over
j
, one gets the unicity.

8
Scattering theorem

8.1
An intermediate technical theorem

Let
I
be a subset of
S
. We want to define a denumerable partition of
{\hat{G}}\times i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
up to sets of measure zero.
First we choose a partition
((i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*})_{q})_{q\in\mathbb{N}}
of
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
, up to sets of measure zero, by cubes of size
1
.
We use an increasing sequence of
\mu
-measurable subsets
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}_{n}
of
{\hat{G}}
of
\mu
-measure less or equal to
n\in\mathbb{N}
, whose union is equal to
{\hat{G}}
. Let
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}={\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}_{0}
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{n+1}={\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}_{n+1}\setminus{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}_{n}
. Then

({\mathcal{A}}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a partition of
{\hat{G}}
. We may choose
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
such that all
\pi\in{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
satisfies:

|\pi({\mathcal{C}}_{G})|\leq n,\|\beta_{J,\pi}\|_{HS}\leq n,J\subset S,

(8.1)

where
{\mathcal{C}}_{G}
is a Casimir element for
\mathfrak{g}
and
\beta_{J}
has been defined in Theorem
6.1
.
We define a partition, up to sets of measure zero, of
{\hat{G}}\times i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}\times((i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*})_{q}\cap{\mathcal{C}})
, where
n,q\in\mathbb{N}

and
{\mathcal{C}}
varies in the family
({\mathcal{C}}_{j})
of
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
given in Lemma
6.21
. Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be the set of
(\gamma,n,q,{\mathcal{C}})
for
\gamma\in\hat{K}
and
(n,q,{\mathcal{C}})
as above. For
m\in{\mathcal{M}}
, let
{\mathcal{H}}_{m}
be the image of the isotypic component
L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}^{\gamma}
by the spectral projection corresponding to
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}\times((i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*})_{q}\cap{\mathcal{C}})
. One has:

L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}=\hat{\oplus}_{m\in{\mathcal{M}}}{\mathcal{H}}_{m}.

Our goal is to prove that the scattering operators are unitary.
For this we first prove, as in
[
39
]
, Theorem 14.3.1, the following technical but important intermediate theorem.

8.1 Theorem
.

Let
I^{\prime}\approx I
. We use the notation of Theorem
7.6
. For all
m\in{\mathcal{M}}
,
\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,I^{\prime}}
and
v\in{\mathcal{H}}_{m}
,
one has:

\|S_{\mathfrak{w}}(v)\|\geq\|v\|.

Beginning of the proof:

Suppose it is false. Then:

There exist
I\subset S
,
m=(\gamma,n,q,{\mathcal{C}})\in{\mathcal{M}}
, a non zero
v_{0}\in{\mathcal{H}}:={\mathcal{H}}_{m}
,
\delta>0
,
I^{\prime}\approx I
and
\mathfrak{w}_{0}\in W_{I,I^{\prime}}
such that
\|S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}(v_{0})\|<(1-\delta)\|v_{0}\|
.

(8.2)

For the rest of the proof, we fix such data and try to get a contradiction. We write
f_{0}=\beta(i_{I}v_{0})
, which has the same norm than
v_{0}
as it follows from Lemma
6.21
(ii). The idea is to choose a suitable
a\in A_{I}
such that, for
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
large enough,
\|a^{n}f_{0}\|^{2}<\|v_{0}\|^{2}
. This will furnish the desired contradiction as
\|a^{n}f_{0}\|^{2}=\|f_{0}\|^{2}=\|v_{0}\|^{2}
.
One will use special coverings
(U_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}})
(cf. Lemma
3.9
), but we will have possibly to reduce the
\varepsilon_{C}
, proceeding by downward induction in
Card\check{C}
(as the elements of the covering at the next step might change).
Before continuing the proof of the Theorem we need to establish some properties of elements of
{\mathcal{H}}
.

8.2
Properties of elements of
{\mathcal{H}}

Let
\pi\in{\hat{G}}
and
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
. Let us denote by
p_{\pi,\nu}
the orthogonal projection of
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,td}
onto
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}
which commutes to the action of
G
.

Before stating the next proposition, we recall that, for
N\in\mathbb{R}
, the semi norm
q_{N}
has been defined in (
4.12
) and for
r\in\mathbb{N}
,
q_{N,r}
denotes a Sobolev norm. We denote by
q_{N}^{I}
the corresponding seminorm for functions on
X_{I}
. We will use the notation of section
4.1
. In particular we recall the maps
\alpha
,
\beta
for
L^{2}(X)
and their analogues
\alpha_{I}
,
\beta_{I}
for
L^{2}(X_{I})
. And we have the analogue for
X_{I}
of the the maps of
\beta_{\pi}
and
\alpha_{\pi}
(cf. (
4.22
)).

We use the identification of the beginning of section
7.1
.

8.2 Proposition
.

We fix in the following
N\in\mathbb{N}
greater than
N_{X}
(cf. (
4.17
) for the definition of
N_{X}
). Let
m=(\gamma,n,q,{\mathcal{C}})\in{\mathcal{M}}
,
J\subset S
and

v\in{\mathcal{H}}_{m}
.

(i) We define

f=\beta(i_{I}v)\in L^{2}(X).

If
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
and
\pi\in{\hat{G}}
, let
v_{\pi,\nu}=p_{\pi,\nu}v_{\pi}
and
f_{\pi,\nu}=\beta_{\pi}i_{I,\pi}v_{\pi,\nu}
.

We use the notation of Proposition
6.13
.
For
\nu\in{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}_{,conj}
, let:

f_{\nu}:={\sum}^{\prime}_{\pi\in{\hat{G}},{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}\not=0}f_{\pi,\nu},

where the number of non zero terms in the sum is finite due to Lemma
6.14
(iii). If
\nu\notin{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}_{,conj}
, let
f_{\nu}=0
.

The map
(\pi,\nu)\mapsto f_{\pi,\nu}
(resp.
\nu\mapsto f_{\nu}
) is a
\mu^{I}
-measurable function on
{\hat{G}}\times i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
(resp. Lebesgue measurable function on
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
) with values in
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X)
.

Moreover, there exist constants
c_{r},r\in\mathbb{N}
, independent of
v\in{\mathcal{H}}
, such that:

\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}q_{-N,r}(f_{\nu})d\nu<c_{r}\|v\|.

(8.3)

This ensures that, for all
x\in X
, the integral
\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}f_{\nu}(x)d\nu
is absolutely convergent. Moreover the function on
X
that it defines is equal to
f
.

(ii) There exist constants
c^{\prime}_{r},r\in\mathbb{N}
, independent of
v\in{\mathcal{H}}
, such that:

\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}q^{J}_{-N,r}((f_{\nu})_{J})d\nu<c^{\prime}_{r}\|v\|,r\in\mathbb{N},

where
q_{-N}^{J}
is the seminorm (
4.12
) relative to
X_{J}
.
One has:

(\beta_{J}i_{J}^{*}i_{I}v)(x)=\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}(f_{\nu})_{J}(x)d\nu,x\in X_{J},

where the above inequality ensures that these integrals are absolutely convergent.

(iii)

(a) For
\mu
-almost
\pi\in{\hat{G}}
and all
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
, the
\mathfrak{a}_{J}
-weights of
g_{\pi,\nu}:=(f_{\pi,\nu})_{J}
and
g_{\nu}:=(f_{\nu})_{J}
are of the form
\mathfrak{w}\nu,\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}
.

(b) For almost all
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
, the
\mathfrak{w}\nu,\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}
, are distinct.

(c) For
\mu
-almost all
\pi
and for such
\nu
,
g_{\pi,\nu}
(resp.
g_{\nu}
) admits a decomposition
\sum_{\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}}g_{\pi,\nu,\mathfrak{w}}
(resp.
\sum_{\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}}g_{\nu,\mathfrak{w}}
) in
\mathfrak{a}_{J}
-weights vectors for the weights
\mathfrak{w}\nu
.

(d) For all
\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}
, there exist constants
c_{\mathfrak{w},r},r\in\mathbb{N}
, independent of
v\in{\mathcal{H}}
, such that:

\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}q^{J}_{-N,r}((g_{\nu,\mathfrak{w}})d\nu<c_{\mathfrak{w},r}\|v\|,r\in\mathbb{N}.

Then one can define

g_{\mathfrak{w}}(x):=\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}g_{\nu,\mathfrak{w}}(x)d\nu,x\in X_{J},

and one has:

\beta_{J}i_{J}^{*}i_{I}v=\sum_{\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}}g_{\mathfrak{w}}.

(e) There exists a square integrable function on
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
,
\nu\mapsto\phi(v,\nu)
, with support in
(i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*})_{q}\cap{\mathcal{C}}
, and for
r\in\mathbb{N}
,
c^{\prime\prime}_{r}>0
such that, for all
\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}
, one has for almost all
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
:

q^{J}_{-N,r}(g_{\nu,\mathfrak{w}})\leq c^{\prime\prime}_{r}\phi(v,\nu)

and

\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}|\phi(v,\nu)|^{2}d\nu=\|v\|^{2},\phi(av,\nu)=\phi(v,\nu),a\in A_{I}^{0}.

Proof.

(i) From the definition of
{\mathcal{H}}_{m}
, for
\mu^{I}
-almost all
(\pi,\nu)
,
v_{\pi,\nu}
is of type
\gamma\in\hat{K}
. Hence it is a
C^{\infty}
-vector. Let us first prove that
(\pi,\nu)\mapsto f_{\pi,\nu}
is a measurable function with values in
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X)
. Recalling the convention (
4.22
), one has

f_{\pi,\nu}=\beta_{\pi}i_{I,\pi}(v_{\pi,\nu})
. Hence from (
4.23
),
f_{\pi,\nu}
is in
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X)
.
One wants to apply (
10.5
). Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed as in (
4.22
). Let
f^{\prime}\in L^{2}_{N,k}(X)
, which is an Hilbert space, be of type
\gamma
. One has:

(f_{\pi,\nu},f^{\prime})=(i_{I,\pi}v_{\pi,\nu},\alpha_{\pi}(f^{\prime}))=(v_{\pi,\nu},i_{I,\pi}^{*}\alpha_{\pi}(f^{\prime}))=(v_{\pi,\nu},P_{I,td}i_{I,\pi}^{*}\alpha_{\pi}(f^{\prime})).

From the measurability of the fields of operators
i^{*}_{I,\pi}
(cf. section
6.2
and Proposition
10.3
), one sees that the field
v^{\prime}_{\pi}=P_{I,td}i_{I,\pi}^{*}\alpha_{\pi}(f^{\prime})
is
\mu
-measurable.
If
e\in L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}
, one has disintegrations in the Hilbert integrals of Lemma
6.13
and Theorem
6.1
:

e=\int^{\oplus}_{{\hat{G}}\times i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}e_{\pi,\nu}d\mu^{I}(\pi,\nu),e=\int^{\oplus}_{{\hat{G}}}e_{\pi}d\mu(\pi),

where
e_{\pi,\nu}
( resp.
e_{\nu}
) is
\mu^{I}
(resp.
\mu
) measurable. From Proposition
6.13
, one sees that for
\mu^{I}
-almost all
(\pi,\nu)
,
p_{\pi,\nu}e_{\pi}=e_{\pi,\nu}
. It follows that
(\pi,\nu)\mapsto v_{\pi,\nu}
,
(\pi,\nu)\mapsto v^{\prime}_{\pi,\nu}=p_{\pi,\nu}v^{\prime}_{\pi}
and

(\pi,\nu)\mapsto(f_{\pi,\nu},f^{\prime})=(v_{\pi,\nu},v^{\prime}_{\pi,\nu})
are
\mu^{I}
-measurable. Then from (
10.2
),
(\pi,\nu)\mapsto f_{\pi,\nu}
is
\mu^{I}
-measurable as a map in
L^{2}_{-N,-k}(X)
, and from (
10.5
), it is
\mu^{I}
-measurable as a map in
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X)
.

We use Fubini’s Theorem for disintegration of measures (cf. Proposition
6.13
(ii)) to conclude that
\nu\mapsto(f_{\nu},f^{\prime})
is mesurable. As this is true for all
f^{\prime}
in the dual
L^{2}_{N,k}(X)
of the Hilbert space
L^{2}_{-N,-k}(X)
,
\nu\mapsto f_{\nu}
is weakly measurable hence measurable as a map into
L^{2}_{-N,-k}(X)
. Then one can apply (
10.5
) to conclude that
\nu\mapsto f_{\nu}
is Lebesgue-measurable with values in
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X)
.

From the measurability of
\nu\mapsto f_{\nu}
as a map into
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X)
and, as evaluation in a point is a continuous linear form on
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X)
, one sees that for all
x\in X
,
\nu\mapsto f_{\nu}(x)
is measurable.

Taking into account (
4.25
), one sees that there exists
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for
\mu
-almost all
\pi\in{\hat{G}}
and all
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
and hence for
\mu^{I}
-almost all
(\pi,\nu)
:

p_{-N}(f_{\pi,\nu})\leq\|\beta_{\pi}\|\|\Delta_{1}^{k^{\prime}}i_{I,\pi}v_{\pi,\nu}\|.

As
v\in{\mathcal{H}}_{m}
, one has from (
8.1
) that
|\pi(C_{\mathfrak{g}})|\leq n
if
f_{\pi,\nu}\not=0
which is of type
\gamma
under
K
. Hence there exists
C_{1}>0
, independent of
v\in{\mathcal{H}}_{m}
, such that
\pi(\Delta)v_{\pi,\nu}=c_{\pi,\nu}v_{\pi,\nu}
, where
|c_{\pi,\nu}|
is bounded by
C_{1}
. Hence one has, for
\mu^{I}
-almost all
(\pi,\nu)
and for all
r_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
, a bound for
p_{-N}(\Delta_{1}^{r_{1}}f_{\pi,\nu})
. Hence from Lemma
4.2
, one gets a bound for

p_{-N,r}(f_{\pi,\nu})
.

From the equivalence of the families of norms
p_{-N,r}
and
q_{-N,r}

one gets a bound for a bound for
q_{-N,r}(f_{\pi,\nu}
for
r\in\mathbb{N}
given. Namely, there exists a constant independent of
v\in{\mathcal{H}}_{m}
, say
c_{1,r}
, such that, for
\mu^{I}
-almost all
(\pi,\nu)
one has:

q_{-N,r}(f_{\pi,\nu})\leq c_{1,r}\|\beta_{\pi}\|\|i_{I,\pi}\|\|v_{\pi,\nu}\|.

(8.4)

For
\mu
-almost all
\pi
,

\|i_{I,\pi}\|\leq\|i_{I}\|

and

\|\beta_{\pi}\|\leq\|{\beta}_{\pi}\|_{HS}.

(8.5)

One considers the map
\psi(\pi,\nu)=c_{1,r}\|\beta_{\pi}\|_{HS}\|i_{I}\|\|v_{\pi,\nu}\|
.
It is measurable as it is the case for the families of operators
i_{I,\pi}
(see section
6.2
) and
\beta_{\pi}
(from properties of
\alpha_{\pi}
, cf. (
4.22
)).
Taking into account the definition of
f_{\nu}
, one has

\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}q_{-N,r}(f_{\nu})d\nu\leq\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}{\sum}^{\prime}_{\pi}\psi(\pi,\nu)d\nu

and (see Proposition
6.13
(ii)):

\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}{\sum}^{\prime}_{\pi}\psi(\pi,\nu)d\nu=\int_{{\hat{G}}\times i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}\psi(\pi,\nu)d\mu^{I}(\pi,\nu)=c_{1,r}\int_{\hat{G}}\|\beta_{\pi}\|_{HS}\|i_{I}\|(\sum_{\nu,{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi,\nu,td}\not=0}\|v_{\pi,\nu}\|)d\mu(\pi).

By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and Lemma
6.14
(iv), one has:,

\sum_{\nu,{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi,\nu,td}\not=0}\|v_{\pi,\nu}\|\leq(Card\>\underline{W})^{1/2}\|v_{\pi}\|.

Using again the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, one gets:

\int_{{\hat{G}}}\|\beta_{\pi}\|_{HS}\|v_{\pi}\|d\mu(\pi)\leq(\int_{\hat{G}}\|\beta_{\pi}\|^{2}_{HS})^{1/2}d\mu(\pi)\|v\|,

where the integral in the right side is finite (cf (
4.21
)).
The existence of
c_{r}
follows and one has proved (
8.3
).

This implies that the integral on
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
of
\nu\mapsto f_{\nu}(x)
is absolutely convergent for all
x\in X
.

Let
f_{1}
be the function on
X
defined by

f_{1}(x)=\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}f_{\nu}(x)d\nu
. We want to prove that
f=f_{1}
. From the first part of the proof, we have established (
8.3
). Hence (cf. after (
4.12
))
p_{-N}(f_{1})
is finite. Let
h\in C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
.
We want to prove that

\int_{X}f(x)h(x)dx=\int_{X}f_{1}(x)h(x)dx.

Let
<f,h>=\int_{X}f(x)h(x)dx
.
From
[
5
]
, section 1.4 and (
4.20
), and the definition of
f
(cf. (i)), one has:

<f,h>=\int_{{\hat{G}}}<\beta_{\pi}i_{I,\pi}v_{\pi},h>d\mu(\pi),

where
\beta_{\pi}i_{I,\pi}v_{\pi}\in L^{2}_{-N,-k}(X)
is viewed as a distribution on
X
. Decomposing
{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,td}
in
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
-weight spaces, we get

<f,h>=\int_{{\hat{G}}}\sum_{\nu,{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu,td}\not=0}<\beta_{\pi}i_{I,\pi}v_{\pi,\nu},h>d\mu(\pi).

Let
(\pi,\nu)\mapsto\psi^{\prime}(\pi,\nu)=<\beta_{\pi}i_{I,\pi}v_{\pi,\nu},h>
, which is
\mu^{I}
-measurable as
(\pi,\nu)\mapsto f_{\pi,\nu}
is
\mu^{I}
-measurable with values in
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X)
(see above).
Using the seminorms (
4.12
) on
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X)
, it is bounded by a constant, depending only on
h
, times
q_{-N}(\beta_{\pi}i_{I,\pi}v_{\pi,\nu})
.
Using (
8.4
), (
8.5
) and proceeding as above one sees that
\psi^{\prime}
is
\mu^{I}
-integrable. Then, on one hand, using Fubini’s Theorem for disintegration of measures (cf. Proposition
6.13
(ii)), one has:

<f,h>=\int_{{\hat{G}}\times i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}\psi^{\prime}(\pi,\nu)d\mu^{I}(\pi,\nu).

On the other hand, using Fubini’s Theorem, which is allowed by (
8.3
), one gets

<f_{1},h>=\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}<f_{\nu},h>d\nu.

Using again the Fubini’s Theorem for disintegration of measures one gets:

<f_{1},h>=\int_{{\hat{G}}\times i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}\psi^{\prime}(\pi,\nu)d\mu^{I}(\pi,\nu).

Hence
<f,h>=<f_{1},h>
for all
h\in C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
and
f=f_{1}
as wanted.

(ii) The proof is essentially the same than the one of (i). One has to change
i_{I}
in
i_{J}^{*}i_{I}
,
\beta
in
\beta_{J}

and use the following equality which follows from the definitions and (
4.4
):

\beta_{J,\pi}i^{*}_{J,\pi}(u)=(\beta_{\pi}(u))_{J},u\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}^{\infty}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}.

(8.6)

(iii) (a) follows from Proposition
7.5
(ii).

(b) is obvious as two linear maps defined on
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
and equal on a set of non zero measure are equal.

(c) The space
{\mathcal{M}}_{J,\pi}
is a sum of eigenspaces for
A_{J}^{0}
, as
A^{0}_{J}
acts unitarily, hence also for
\mathfrak{a}_{J}
. Hence the generalized eigenvectors are eigenvectors.

This implies (c).

(d) is proved like (i):
one has to introduce first the orthogonal projection
p_{\mathfrak{w},\pi,\nu}
on the
\mathfrak{a}_{J}
-weight space for the weight
\mathfrak{w}\nu
of
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{J,\pi}
. Applying
[
39
]
, Corollary 10.4.3, to the
\mathfrak{a}_{J}
-action on
{\mathcal{M}}_{J,\pi}
twisted by
-\mathfrak{w}\nu
, one gets that
p_{\mathfrak{w},\pi,\nu}
is measurable in
(\pi,\nu)
. Then replace in (i)
i_{I,\pi}
by

p_{\mathfrak{w},\pi,\nu}i^{*}_{J,\pi}i_{I,\pi}
.

If
g_{\pi,\nu,\mathfrak{w}}:=\beta_{J,\pi}p_{\mathfrak{w},\pi,\nu}i_{J,\pi}^{*}i_{I,\pi}v_{\pi,\nu}
, one has:

g_{\nu,\mathfrak{w}}:={\sum}^{\prime}_{\pi}g_{\pi,\nu,\mathfrak{w}}.

(8.7)

To get the bounds of (d), one proceeds as above, using that
p_{\mathfrak{w},\pi,\nu}
is of norm less or equal to 1. The last equality of (d) follows from (c).

(e) Defining
\theta(v,\nu):={\sum}^{\prime}_{\pi}\|\beta_{J,\pi}\|_{HS}\|v_{\pi,\nu}\|
, one sees as in the end of the proof of (i) that there is a constant
c^{\prime\prime}_{1,r}
such that

q^{J}_{-N,r}(g_{\pi,\nu,\mathfrak{w}})\leq c^{\prime\prime}_{1,r}\theta(v,\nu).

From Lemma
6.14
(iii) and as
v
is of type
\gamma
, the number of non zero terms in
{\sum}^{\prime}_{\pi}
is bounded independently of
\nu
and
v
and, from (
8.1
),
\|\beta_{J,\pi}\|_{HS}\leq n
.
Hence, applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, one sees that there exists
c>0
such that

\theta(v,\nu)\leq c\phi(v,\nu)
,
where

\phi(v,\nu):=({\sum}^{\prime}_{\pi}\|v_{\pi,\nu}\|^{2})^{1/2}.

From the Fubini’s Theorem for the disintegration of measures (cf. Proposition
6.13
(ii)), one sees that this function has the required properties.

8.3
f_{\mathfrak{c}}^{C,D}
and the main inequality revisited

Let us use the notation of the preceding section. We consider a special covering of
X
(cf. Lemma
3.9
)
(U_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}})_{C\in{\mathcal{F}},\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}}
. Following the notation of Theorem
5.7
we define:

Let
D\in{\mathcal{F}}
and
f\in{\mathcal{A}}_{temp}(X_{D})
. For
D\subset C
,
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}
, we define a function
f^{C,D}_{\mathfrak{c}}
on
X_{P_{\mathfrak{c}}}
,

f^{C,D}_{\mathfrak{c}}:=\sum_{C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}},D\subset C^{\prime}\subset C}(-1)^{Card\check{D}-Card\check{C}^{\prime}}f_{C^{\prime}}\circ e_{P_{\mathfrak{c}},C^{\prime}}.

(8.8)

We take the notation of Theorem
8.1
and (
8.2
). Let
v\in{\mathcal{H}}
and
f\in L^{2}(X)
be as in Proposition
8.2
.

Let
J:=I(D)
. We want to apply the Main Inequality (cf. Theorem
5.7
) to the function
(f_{\pi,\nu})_{D,\mathfrak{w}}
of Proposition
8.2
(iii) where
\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}
and
f_{\pi,\nu}
is defined in (i) of this Proposition. We use the notation there to define
f_{\nu}
,
f
. From Proposition
7.5
,
(f_{\pi,\nu})_{D,\mathfrak{w}}
is zero for
\mu
-almost all
\pi
unless
CardJ>CardI
or
I\approx J
. Moreover it is
Z(\mathfrak{g})
-eigen as it is equal (cf. (
8.7
)) to
(\beta_{J,\pi}i^{*}_{J,\pi}i_{I,\pi}v_{\pi,\nu})_{\mathfrak{w}}
and all maps involved here are
G
-equivariant.

We use the notation of (
3.41
), (
3.43
) and (
3.45
). Using the Main Inequality (cf.
Theorem
5.7
) for these functions, Proposition
8.2
(iii) (e) and the equality:

(f_{\nu})_{J,\mathfrak{w}}={\sum}^{\prime}_{\pi\in{\hat{G}},{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\nu}\not=0}(f_{\pi,\nu})_{J,\mathfrak{w}},

one gets that there exists a square integrable function
\nu\mapsto\phi(v,\nu)
on
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
, with support in
(i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*})_{q}
,
C_{1}
and
\alpha>0
both independent of
v\in{\mathcal{H}}
and
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
, such that:

|f^{C,D}_{\nu,D,\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{c}}(\mathfrak{c}\omega a_{C}w)|^{2}\leq C_{1}a_{C}^{2\rho}e^{-2\alpha\|\log a_{C,D}\|}|\phi(v,\nu)|^{2},(\omega,a_{C})\in\Omega_{C}\times clA_{C}^{-}(D),w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}},\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C},

(8.9)

where

f^{C,D}_{\nu,D,\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{c}}:=(((f_{\nu})_{D,\mathfrak{w}}))_{\mathfrak{c}}^{C,D},

(8.10)

and
\phi
is as in Proposition
8.2
(iii) (e).

In particular one has

\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}|\phi(v,\nu)|^{2}=\|v\|^{2},\phi(av,\nu)=\phi(v,\nu),a\in A_{I}^{0}.

(8.11)

For
\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,I(D)}
, using Proposition
8.2
(iii) (d), one can define:

f^{C,D}_{D,\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{c}}:=\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}f^{C,D}_{\nu,D,\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{c}}d\nu\in C^{\infty}(X_{P_{\mathfrak{c}}}).

(8.12)

We define similarly
f^{C,D}_{\nu,D,\mathfrak{c}}=((f_{\nu})_{D})^{C,D}_{\mathfrak{c}}
and
f^{C,D}_{D,\mathfrak{c}}=\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}f^{C,D}_{\nu,D,\mathfrak{c}}d\nu
.

Let us show that, for all
C\in{\mathcal{F}}
that one has the equality of functions on
X_{P_{\mathfrak{c}}}
:

f_{\mathfrak{c}}=\sum_{D\in{\mathcal{F}},D\subset C}f^{C,D}_{D,\mathfrak{c}}.

(8.13)

In fact

\sum_{D\in{\mathcal{F}},D\subset C}f^{C,D}_{\nu,D,\mathfrak{c}}=\sum_{D\in{\mathcal{F}}}(\sum_{C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}},D\subset C^{\prime}\subset C}(-1)^{Card\check{D}-Card\check{C}^{\prime}}f_{\nu,C^{\prime},\mathfrak{c}})

and for
C^{\prime}
given,
\sum_{D\subset C^{\prime}}(-1)^{Card\check{D}}
is equal to 0 if
C^{\prime}
is not reduced to
\{0\}
and to 1 if
C^{\prime}=\{0\}
. Our claim follows from
f_{\nu,\mathfrak{c}}=f_{\nu,\{0\},\mathfrak{c}}
and by integration on
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
.

Using Proposition
8.2
(iii) (d), one gets:

f_{\mathfrak{c}}=\sum_{D,C\in{\mathcal{F}},D\subset C}(\sum_{\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,I(D)}}f^{C,D}_{D,\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{c}}),

(8.14)

with the convention that an empty sum is zero.

8.4
Beginning of the proof of Theorem
8.1
: upper bound for
\|f\|_{L^{2}(X)}

One will use notation and results of section
8.3
.

Let
v\in{\mathcal{H}}
and
f=\beta i_{I}v
.

If
C\in{\mathcal{F}}
such that
I(C)\approx I
and
\mathfrak{a}_{C}=\mathfrak{a}_{I(C)}
, we introduce a partition
(\overline{U}_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}})
of the union of the
U_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}}
such that
\overline{U}_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}}\subset U_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}}
.
Let

I_{C,\mathfrak{c}}:=(\int_{U_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}}}|f(x)|^{2}dx)^{1/2}

and, if
I(C)\approx I
and
\mathfrak{a}_{C}=\mathfrak{a}_{I(C}
, let

\overline{I}_{C,\mathfrak{c}}:=(\int_{\overline{U}_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}}}|f(x)|^{2}dx)^{1/2}

and

\overline{I}_{C}:=(\int_{\cup_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}}\overline{U}_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}}}|f(x)|^{2}dx)^{1/2}.

Our starting point is:

\|f\|_{L^{2}(X)}\leq I+\overline{I}

where
I
is the sum of the
I_{C,\mathfrak{c}}
, when
I(C)\not\approx I
or
I(C)\approx I
but
\mathfrak{a}_{C}\not=\mathfrak{a}_{I(C)}
and
\overline{I}
is the sum of the
\overline{I}_{C}
when
C\approx I(C)
and
\mathfrak{a}_{C}=\mathfrak{a}_{I(C)}
.
The dependence in
v
is implicit in the notation.

(8.15)

We use the notation of (
8.12
) to define for
D\subset C
and
\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,I(D)}
:

I^{C,D}_{\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{w}}:=(\int_{U_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}}}|f^{C,D}_{D,\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{c}}(x)|^{2}dx)^{1/2}.

and, if
I(C)\approx I
and
\mathfrak{a}_{C}=\mathfrak{a}_{I(C)}
,
\mathfrak{w}\in W(I,I(C))
:

\overline{I}^{C,C}_{\mathfrak{w}}:=(\sum_{{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}}}\int_{\overline{U}_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}}}|f^{C,C}_{C,\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{c}}(x)|^{2}dx)^{1/2}.

The following inequalities follows from Proposition
8.2
(iii) (d) and (
8.14
).

(a) If
I(C)\not\approx I
or if
I(C)\approx I
but
\mathfrak{a}_{C}\not=\mathfrak{a}_{I(C)}
, one has:

I_{C,\mathfrak{c}}\leq\sum_{D\subset C,\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I(C),I(D)}}I^{C,D}_{\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{c}}.

(b) If
I(C)\approx I
and
\mathfrak{a}_{C}=\mathfrak{a}_{I(C)}
one has

\overline{I}_{C}\leq(\sum_{\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,I(C)}}\overline{I}^{C,C}_{\mathfrak{w}}+\sum_{D\subset C,D\not=C,\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I(C),I(D)}}I^{C,D}_{\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{c}}).

(8.16)

If
v
is changed in
v_{n}
, we introduce a lower index
n
in the notation.
One will proceed by downward induction
on
Card\check{C}
to control
I_{C,\mathfrak{c}}
,
I^{C,D}_{\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{c}}
and
\overline{I}^{C,C}_{\mathfrak{w}}
when
D\subset C
,
\mathfrak{c}
and
\mathfrak{w}
vary.

It follows from Proposition
7.4
that:

f^{C,D}_{D,\mathfrak{c}}=0
unless
CardI(D)>CardI
or
I(D)\approx I
.

(8.17)

8.3 Lemma
.

Let
D\subset C
and
\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,I(D)}
. From the definition of
W_{I,I(D)}
(cf. Proposition
7.5
), there exist
J^{\prime}\subset I(D)
with
J^{\prime}\approx I
and
\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}\in W_{I,J^{\prime}}
such that
\mathfrak{w}\nu=\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}\nu_{|\mathfrak{a}_{J}}
for all
\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
.
We choose a
W_{X}
-invariant inner product on
\mathfrak{a}_{X}
. Let
\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{\perp}
be the orthogonal of
\mathfrak{a}_{D}
in
\mathfrak{a}_{J^{\prime}}
. One writes
i\mathfrak{a}_{J^{\prime}}^{*}=i\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{*}\oplus i\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{\perp*}
. Recall the definition of
\Omega_{C}
(cf. (
3.43
)) and
A_{CD}
(cf. beginning of section
5.3
).

There exist constants
c>0
and
\alpha>0
, such that for all

,
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}
,
\varepsilon_{C}>0
:

(i) If
D\not=C
,

I^{C,D}_{\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{c}}\leq c(\int_{A_{C,D}^{-}(\varepsilon_{C})}e^{-\alpha\|\log a_{C,D}\|}da_{C,D})\|v\|.

(ii) If
D=C
,

I^{C,D}_{\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{c}}\leq c\int_{\Omega_{C}{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{*}}|(\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{\perp*}}f_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime-1}(\lambda+\mu),C,\mathfrak{w}}(\mathfrak{c}\omega)d\mu)|^{2}d\lambda d\omega.

Proof.

(i) Let
v\in{\mathcal{H}}
. From (
3.46
) and using translation by
\mathfrak{c}
, one has:

I^{C,D}_{\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{c}}=(\int_{\Omega_{C}{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}}\int_{A_{C}^{-}(\varepsilon_{C})}a_{C}^{-2\rho}|\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}f^{C,D}_{\nu,D,\mathfrak{w}}(\mathfrak{c}\omega a_{C})d\nu|^{2}da_{C}d\omega)^{1/2},

where
\Omega_{C}
is relatively compact in
U(X)MA_{C^{c}}
and
where the measure
d\omega
on
\Omega_{C}{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
is the product of
of a bounded mesure on
\Omega_{C}
with the counting measure on
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Recall that
A_{C,2}
is the set of elements of
A_{C}
with square equal to 1. Recall the definition of
A_{C}^{-}(\varepsilon_{C})
(cf (
3.38
)).
Then
A^{-}_{C}(\varepsilon_{C})
is the set of
a_{D}a_{C,D}
where
a_{C,D}\in A_{C,2}A^{-}_{C,D}(\varepsilon_{C})
(cf. beginning of section
5.3
for notation) and
a_{D}
is in the set
A^{0}_{D}\cap A_{D}^{-}(\varepsilon_{C})
.
Using
(a_{C,D}a_{D})^{\rho}=a_{C,D}^{\rho}a_{D}^{\rho}
and the fact that
f^{C,D}_{\nu,C,\mathfrak{w}}
is of weight
\mathfrak{w}\nu
for the normalized action of
A^{0}_{D}
, hence transforms under
a_{D}\in A^{0}_{D}
by
a_{D}^{\rho+\mathfrak{w}\nu}
, one gets by Fubini’s theorem:

I^{C,D}_{\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{c}}=\int_{\Omega_{C}{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\int_{A_{C,2}A^{-}_{C,D}(\varepsilon_{C})}a_{C,D}^{-2\rho}\int_{A^{0}_{D}\cap A_{D}^{-}(\varepsilon_{C})}|\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}f^{C,D}_{\nu,D,\mathfrak{w}}(\mathfrak{c}\omega a_{C,D})a_{D}^{\mathfrak{w}\nu}d\nu|^{2}da_{D}da_{C,D}d\omega)^{1/2}.

Replacing integration on
A^{0}_{D}\cap A_{D}^{-}(\varepsilon_{C})
by an integration on
A^{0}_{D}
, we get:

I^{C,D}_{\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{c}}\leq(\int_{\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\int_{A_{C,2}A_{C,D}^{-}(\varepsilon_{C})}a_{C,D}^{-2\rho}\int_{A^{0}_{D}}|\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}f^{C,D}_{\nu,D,\mathfrak{w}}(\mathfrak{c}\omega a_{C,D})a_{D}^{\mathfrak{w}\nu}d\nu|^{2}da_{D}da_{C,D}d\omega)^{1/2}

Let
J^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}\in W_{I,J^{\prime}}
be as in the statement of the Lemma. We change
\nu
by
\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}\nu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{J^{\prime}}^{*}
and write
\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}\nu=\lambda+\mu
with
\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{*}
,
\mu\in\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{\perp*}
. One has:

I^{C,D}_{\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{c}}\leq\int_{\Omega_{C}{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\int_{A_{C,2}A_{C,D}^{-}(\varepsilon_{C})}a_{C,D}^{-2\rho}\int_{A^{0}_{D}}|\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{*}}(\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{\perp*}}f^{C,D}_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime-1}(\lambda+\mu),D,\mathfrak{w}}(\mathfrak{c}\omega a_{C,D})d\mu)a_{D}^{\lambda}d\lambda|^{2}da_{D}da_{C,D}d\omega)^{1/2}

By the Plancherel-Parseval formula, if
\varphi
is a square integrable function on
i\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{*}
, and
\hat{\varphi}
is its Fourier transform, that we view as a function on
A^{0}_{D}
, one has:

\|\hat{\varphi}\|_{|L^{2}(A^{0}_{D})}^{2}=\|\varphi\|_{|L^{2}(i\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{*})}^{2}.

Applying this to

\varphi(\lambda)=\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{\perp*}}f^{C,D}_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime-1}(\lambda+\mu),D,\mathfrak{w}}(\mathfrak{c}\omega a_{C,D})d\mu,

for almost all
\mu
, one gets:

I^{C,D}_{\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{c}}\leq(\int_{\Omega_{C}{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}}\int_{A_{C,2}A_{C,D}^{-}(\varepsilon_{C})}a_{C,D}^{-2\rho}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{*}}|(\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{\perp*}}f^{C,D}_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime-1}(\lambda+\mu),D,\mathfrak{w}}(\mathfrak{c}\omega a_{C,D})d\mu)|^{2}d\lambda da_{C,D}d\omega)^{1/2}.

(8.18)

First we assume
C\not=D
.
We apply the main inequality (cf. (
8.9
)) to
f^{C,D}_{\nu,D,\mathfrak{w}}
, with
\nu=\mathfrak{w}^{\prime-1}(\lambda+\mu)\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
. Then

|f^{C,D}_{\mathfrak{w}^{-1}(\lambda+\mu),D,\mathfrak{w}}(\mathfrak{c}\omega a_{C,D})|\leq C_{1}a_{C,D}^{\rho}e^{-\alpha\|\log a_{C,D}\|}\phi(v,\mathfrak{w}^{\prime-1}(\lambda+\mu)).

This leads to the bound

I^{C,D}_{\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{c}}\leq C_{1}vol(\Omega_{C}{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}})(\int_{A_{C,2}A_{C,D}^{-}(\varepsilon_{C})}e^{-2\alpha\|\log a_{C,D}\|}da_{C,D})^{1/2}(\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{*}}|\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{\perp*}}\phi(v,\mathfrak{w}^{\prime-1}(\lambda+\mu))d\mu|^{2}d\lambda)^{1/2}.

We refer to Proposition
8.2
(iii) (e) for the properties of
\phi
. Using that
\nu\mapsto\phi(v,\nu)
has a support in
(i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*})_{q}
, there exists a compact set in
i\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{*\perp}
independent of

such that the map on
i\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{*\perp}
,
\mu\mapsto\phi(v,\mathfrak{w}^{\prime-1}(\lambda+\mu))
has support in this compact. Hence we can apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to

\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{\perp*}}\phi(v,\mathfrak{w}^{\prime-1}(\lambda+\mu))d\mu
. Integrating the inequality on
i\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{*}
and using the properties of
\phi
, we get (i).

(ii) is an immediate consequence of (
8.18
) .

We will make a choice of an element
a
of
A^{0}_{I}
. Recall our choice of

,

and if

(8.20)

Let us use the notation of the preceding Lemma, in particular the definition of

and
\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
. Let us show :

One may and will choose

Let

8.4 Lemma
.

Let
J^{\prime}
be as in Lemma
8.3
. Let
\Omega=\Omega_{C}{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
. Let
\phi\in L^{2}(\Omega\times i\mathfrak{a}_{J^{\prime}}^{*})
with support in
i\mathfrak{a}_{J^{\prime}}^{*}
contained in a given cube. Let
a\in(A^{\perp}_{D})^{0}
,
a\not=1
. Then

I(\phi,n):=\int_{\Omega}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{*}}|(\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{\perp*}}\phi(\omega,\lambda+\mu)(a^{n})^{\mu}d\mu)|^{2}d\lambda d\omega

tends to zero if
n
tends to
\infty
.

Proof.

Using Cauchy-Schwarz inequality applied to the internal integral, using the hypothesis on the support of
\phi
and then using Fubini’s Theorem, one sees that there exists
c>0
such that, for all
\phi
as above,

I(\phi,n)\leq c\|\phi\|^{2}_{L^{2}},n\in\mathbb{N}.

(8.22)

To prove the Lemma, one first treats the case where
\phi
is a linear combination of tensor products of a function on
\Omega
and of a tensor product of characteristic functions of bounded intervals in coordinates of

\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{*}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{\perp*}
. Then by an explicit computation, one sees that the Lemma is true for such
\phi
. Now take
\phi
general and let
\varepsilon>0
. One can take a function
\phi_{\varepsilon}
as above such that:

\|\phi-\phi_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}<\varepsilon.

(8.23)

Then one has

I(\phi,n)=I(\phi-\phi_{\varepsilon}+\phi_{\varepsilon},n)

=I(\phi-\phi_{\varepsilon},n)+I(\phi_{\varepsilon},n)+

2Re\int_{\Omega\times i\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{*}}[\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{\perp*}}(\phi-\phi_{\varepsilon})(\omega,\lambda+\mu)(a^{n})^{\mu}d\mu][\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{\perp*}}\phi_{\varepsilon}(\omega,\lambda+\mu)(a^{n})^{\mu}d\mu]d\lambda d\omega.

Applying Cauchy-Schwarz to the integral on
\Omega_{C}\times i\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{*}
, one gets:

I(\phi,n)\leq I(\phi-\phi_{\varepsilon},n)+I(\phi_{\varepsilon},n)+2I(\phi-\phi_{\varepsilon})^{1/2}I(\phi_{\varepsilon},n)^{1/2}.

Applying (
8.22
) to
\phi-\phi_{\varepsilon}
and using (
8.23
), one gets

I(\phi,n)\leq c\varepsilon^{2}+I(\phi_{\varepsilon},n)+2c^{1/2}\varepsilon I(\phi_{\varepsilon},n)^{1/2}.

Hence, as
\phi_{\varepsilon}
satisfies the Lemma,
I(\phi,n)
is less than a constant times
\varepsilon
for
n
large. This achieves to prove the Lemma.

8.5 Proposition
.

If
C\in{\mathcal{F}}
. Let
\eta>0
. There exists a special covering
(U_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}})_{C\in{\mathcal{F}},\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}}
such that for all
C,D\in{\mathcal{F}}
with
D\subset C
and
\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,I((D)}
, one has, for
n
large enough:

(i) If
D\not=C
,
I^{C,D}_{\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{c},n}
is smaller than
\eta\|v\|
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(ii) If
D=C
and
I(C)\not\approx I
or
D=C
,
I(C)\approx I
and
\mathfrak{a}_{C}\not=\mathfrak{a}_{I(C)}
,

I^{C,D}_{\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{c},n}
is smaller than
\eta\|v\|
for large
n
.

Proof.

We proceed by downward induction on
Card\check{C}
.

(i) First if
\check{C}
is of maximal dimension Let
C
be given as in (i) i.e.
D\not=C
, we use Lemma
8.3
(i) for
f_{n}
and we use the ”reduction of
\varepsilon_{C}
” as in Lemma
3.8
(ii) to make the integral

\int_{A_{C,D}^{-}(2\varepsilon_{C})}e^{-\alpha\|\log a_{C,D}\|}da_{C,D}
small enough. Then using that
\|v_{n}\|=\|v\|
, our claim follow. Possibly, one has to change for
C^{\prime}\in\check{{\mathcal{F}}}
with
Card(\check{C^{\prime}})<Card(\check{C^{\prime}})
, the set
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
and the
(U_{C^{\prime},\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C^{\prime}}})
.

Now assume
C,D
as in (ii). One has
\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{\perp}\not=\{0\}
: indeed if
\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{\perp}=\{0\}
one has
J^{\prime}=I(D)=I(C)

,
\mathfrak{a}_{D}=\mathfrak{a}_{C}=\mathfrak{a}_{J^{\prime}}
which is not true. We take into account our choice of
\underline{x}
(cf. (
8.21
)). We apply Lemma
8.3
(ii) to
v_{n}
, taking into account that

(f_{n})_{C,\mathfrak{w}^{\prime-1}(\lambda+\mu),\mathfrak{w}}=(a_{n})^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime-1}(\lambda+\mu)}f_{C,\mathfrak{w}^{\prime-1}(\lambda+\mu),\mathfrak{w}}.

Let us write
a_{n}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime-1}(\lambda+\mu)}=a_{n}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime-1}(\lambda)}a_{n}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime-1}(\mu)}
. The factor
a_{n}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime-1}(\lambda)}
goes out from the first integral in Lemma
8.3
for
v_{n}
and reduces to 1 due to the square in the second. Let
\underline{y}
be the projection of
\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}\underline{x}
on
\mathfrak{a}_{D}^{\perp}
:
\underline{y}
is non zero due to our choice of
\underline{x}
(cf. (
8.21
)) and
a_{n}^{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime-1}(\mu)}=e^{\mu(n\underline{y})}
. One can apply the preceeding Lemma to get the result.

End of the proof of Theorem
8.1

From the preceeding Proposition and using, it remains to control the terms

with

and

From Theorem

, if

. As

and

, it follows from the definition of scattering operators (cf. Theorem

f_{C}=\sum_{\mathfrak{w}\in W(I,I(C))}S_{\mathfrak{w}}v_{n}.

(8.24)

8.6 Lemma
.

Let
C
be as above and
\eta>0
.

(i)
If
\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,I(C)},\mathfrak{w}\not=\mathfrak{w}_{0}
, then by taking
\varepsilon_{C}
small enough and
n
large, one has
\overline{I}^{C,C}_{\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{w},n}:=(\int_{\overline{U}_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}}}|(S_{\mathfrak{w}}v_{n})(e_{P_{\mathfrak{c}},C}(x))|^{2}dx)^{1/2}<\eta\|v\|
.

(ii) The same is true for

\overline{I}^{C,C}_{\mathfrak{w},n}
which we recall is equal to
(\sum_{{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}}}\int_{\overline{U}_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}}}|f^{C,C}_{C,\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{c}}(x)|^{2}dx)^{1/2}.

Proof.

We will use the following elementary fact:
If
{\mathcal{C}}
is a convex open cone in a finite dimensional real vector space
E
and
e\in E
, then the sequence of characteristic functions of
{\mathcal{C}}-ne=n({\mathcal{C}}-e)
converges simply to
1
(to
0
) if
e
is in the interior
{\mathcal{C}}^{0}
of
{\mathcal{C}}
(resp.
e
is not in this
cl({\mathcal{C}})
).
In the first case
{\mathcal{C}}-e
is an open set containing 0. In the second case
(cl({\mathcal{C}})-e)
is a closed set which does not contain
0
, hence it is contained in the complementary set of a neighborhood of
0
in
E
. Our claim follows.
One has
(S_{\mathfrak{w}}v_{n})(x)=(S_{\mathfrak{w}}v_{0})(x.\mathfrak{w}(a^{n}))
and making a change of variables, one gets:

Ê

(\int_{\overline{U}_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}}}|f^{C,C}_{C,\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{c}}(x)|^{2}dx)^{1/2}\leq(\int_{U_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}}.\mathfrak{w}a^{n}}|(S_{\mathfrak{w}}v_{0})(e_{P_{\mathfrak{c}},C}(x))|^{2}dx)^{1/2}

\leq(\int_{X_{P_{\mathfrak{c}}}}|(S_{\mathfrak{w}}v_{0})(e_{P_{\mathfrak{c}},C}(x))|^{2})dx)^{1/2}.

(8.25)

By using that
e_{P_{\mathfrak{c}},C}
is measure preserving, one has:

\int_{X_{P_{\mathfrak{c}}}}|(S_{\mathfrak{w}}v_{0})(e_{P_{\mathfrak{c}},C}(x))|^{2})dx)\leq(\int_{X_{C}}|S_{\mathfrak{w}}v_{0}(x_{C})|^{2}dx_{C}<+\infty

If
\mathfrak{w}
is as in the Lemma, from (
8.20
),
\mathfrak{w}\underline{x}
is not in the closure of
C
.
Using translations of cones and Lebesgue dominated convergence for the integrals on the sets
U_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}}\mathfrak{w}a^{n}
, one gets (i).

(ii) follows immediately from (i).

8.7 Lemma
.

There exists a constant
c>0
such that for all
v\in{\mathcal{H}}
,
\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,I(C)}
,
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}
, and

small enough, one has:

Proof.

One may reduce to the case
\mathfrak{c}=1
and
\varepsilon_{C}
will be denoted
\varepsilon
here. Possibly changing the base point, one reduces the proof to
x
in
Px_{0}\cap U_{C,1,\varepsilon}
.

We identify
A^{-}(\varepsilon)
to
(]-\varepsilon,\varepsilon[)^{\check{C}}
by
j^{\prime}_{C}
(cf. (
3.38
)). We use the notation of (

) in the definition of

. As

is good, one has from Lemma

, for

e_{C}(x)=e_{C,P}(x)expu(x)

where

is a

function with values in

and which is defined on

, with value

on

, where

(resp.
