) is open and relatively compact and contains the closure of
V
(resp.
B^{C}
).
By bounding the differential of
u
on the closure of
VMB^{C}(]-\varepsilon,\varepsilon[)^{\check{C}}
, and using a mean value inequality, one gets the existence of
c_{1}>0
such that:

\|u(vmba_{C})\|\leq c_{1}\|a_{C}\|,v\in V,m\in M,b\in B^{C^{m}},a\in A_{C}^{-}(\varepsilon).

(8.26)

where

\|a_{C}\|=Sup_{\check{\alpha}\in\check{C}}|a_{C,\check{\alpha}}|.

Let
\Omega=VMB^{C^{m}}
and
d\omega
the measure
dvdmdb
on
\Omega
, which is of total mass finite due to the relative compacity of
\Omega
.
One is reduced to study:

I:=(\int_{\Omega A_{C}^{-}(\varepsilon)}a_{C}^{-2\rho}|f_{C,\mathfrak{w}}(\omega a_{C})-f_{C,\mathfrak{w}}(\omega a_{C}exp(u(\omega a_{C})))|^{2}d\omega da_{C})^{1/2}.

We use on
\mathfrak{a}_{C}
the norm given by the supremum of the absolute values of the coordinates in the basis
\check{\alpha}\in\check{C}
.
One introduces, for
\omega\in\Omega
,
a_{C}\in A_{C}^{-}(\varepsilon)
and
\underline{x}\in\mathfrak{a}_{C}
of norm 1 fixed, a scalar valued, differentiable function of one real variable:

t\mapsto\phi_{\omega a_{C},\underline{x}}(t):=f_{C,\mathfrak{w}}(\omega a_{C}exp(t\underline{x}).

We want to find a bound
M
of the absolute values of the derivatives of this map for
t\leq\varepsilon
when
\omega,a_{C}
and
\underline{x}
vary as, due to (
8.26
) and the mean value theorem:

|f_{C,\mathfrak{w}}(\omega a_{C})-f_{C,\mathfrak{w}}(\omega a_{C}exp(u(\omega a_{C})))|\leq\varepsilon M.

(8.27)

One has using left and right regular representations:

d/dt(\phi_{\omega a_{C},\underline{x}}(t))=R_{X}f_{C,\mathfrak{w}}(\omega a_{C})=L_{-Ad(\omega)\underline{x}}f_{C,\mathfrak{w}}(\omega a_{C}expt\underline{x}).

Fix a basis of the Lie algebra of
G
,
(Y_{j})
. One has:

-Ad(\omega)\underline{x}=\sum_{j}\phi_{j}(\omega,\underline{x})Y_{j},

where the
\phi_{j}
are
C^{\infty}
.
From Proposition
8.2
(iii) (d) and using derivatives under the integral sign in order to express
L_{Y_{j}}f_{C,\mathfrak{w}}
as an integral of functions, one gets:

L_{Y_{j}}f_{C,\mathfrak{w}}(\omega a_{C}expt\underline{x})=\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}}g_{j,\nu,C,\mathfrak{w}}(\omega a_{C}exp\underline{x})d\nu

(8.28)

where
g_{j,\nu,C,\mathfrak{w}}
transforms by the normalized action of
A_{C}
by the character
\mathfrak{w}\nu
.
Moreover by Proposition
8.2
(iii) (e), using the notation there with
r=0
and by the relative compacity of
\Omega
, there exists
C_{j}>0
such that, for almost all
\nu
in
i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
:

|g_{j,\nu,C,\mathfrak{w}}(\omega)|\leq C_{j}\phi(v,\nu),\omega\in\Omega.

(8.29)

Also there exists
C^{\prime}_{j}>0
such that

|\phi_{j}(\omega,\underline{x})|\leq C^{\prime}_{j},\omega\in\Omega,\underline{x}\in\mathfrak{a}_{C},\|\underline{x}\|\leq 1.

One considers the map on
\Omega\times i\mathfrak{a}_{C}^{*}
defined by:

g_{j}(\omega,\mu)=g_{j,\mathfrak{w}^{-1}\mu,C,\mathfrak{w}}(\omega),\Omega\in\Omega,\mu\in i\mathfrak{a}^{*}_{C}

which is in
L^{2}(\Omega\times i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*},d\omega d\mu)
as
\Omega
has bounded volume for
d\omega
and due to the property of square integrability of
\nu\mapsto\psi(v,\nu)
. One can considers the Fourier transform in the second variable of
g_{j}
, that will be denoted
\hat{g}_{j}(\omega,a_{C})
, and which is in
L^{2}(\Omega\times A_{C},d\omega da_{C})
. One has, for
\mu=\mathfrak{w}\nu
:

g_{j,\nu,C,\mathfrak{w}}(\omega a_{C}expt\underline{x})=a_{C}^{\rho}(expt\underline{x})^{\rho}g_{j}(\omega,\mu)(a_{C}^{\mu}(expt\underline{x})^{\mu}).

From (
8.28
), this implies:

d/dt(\phi_{\omega a_{C},\underline{x}}(t))=\sum_{j}a_{C}^{\rho}(expt\underline{x})^{\rho}\hat{g}_{j}(\omega,a_{C}expt\underline{x}).

Let
C_{\rho}=Sup_{\underline{x}\in\mathfrak{a}_{C},\|\underline{x}\|\leq 1}(expt\underline{x})^{-2\rho}
.
Using (
8.27
), one has

I\leq\varepsilon C_{\rho}\sum_{j}(\int_{\Omega\times A_{C}^{-}(\varepsilon)}|\phi_{j}(\omega,\underline{x})\hat{g}_{j}(\omega,(a_{C}expt\underline{x}))|^{2}da_{C}d\omega)^{1/2}

By enlarging the domain of integration and using
C^{\prime}_{j}
, one gets:

I\leq\varepsilon C_{\rho}\sum_{j}(C^{\prime}_{j})(\int_{\Omega\times A_{C}}|\hat{g}_{j}(\omega,(a_{C}expt\underline{x}))|^{2}da_{C}d\omega)^{1/2}

By a change of variable from
a_{C}
to
a_{C}expt\underline{x}
and using Plancherel formula for
A_{C}
, one deduces:

I\leq\varepsilon C_{\rho}\sum_{j}(C^{\prime}_{j})(\int_{\Omega\times i\mathfrak{a}_{C}^{*}}|g_{j}(\omega,\mu)|^{2}d\mu d\omega)^{1/2}

From (
8.29
) one has for almost all
\mu\in i\mathfrak{a}_{C}^{*}
:

|g_{j}(\omega,\mu)|\leq C_{j}\phi(v,\mathfrak{w}^{-1}\mu).

The properties
\phi
(cf. Proposition
8.2
(iii) (e)) imply:

I\leq\varepsilon C_{\rho}(\sum_{j}(C^{\prime}_{j})C_{j})\|v\|.

This achieves to prove the Lemma.

Recall our choice of
C_{0}
(cf. (
8.19
)). To simplify notation we will denote it here simply by
C
. We will give a bound for

\overline{I}^{C,C}_{\mathfrak{w}_{0},n}:=(\sum_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}_{0}}\int_{\overline{U}_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}}}|(S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}v_{n})(e_{P_{\mathfrak{c}},C}(x))|^{2}dx)^{1/2}.

8.8 Lemma
.

Let
\eta>0
. By reducing
\varepsilon_{C}
one has

I^{C,C}_{\mathfrak{w}_{0},n}\leq(1+\eta)(1-\delta)\|v\|+\eta.

Proof.

Let
U_{0}=\cup_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}}\overline{U}_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}}
.By the preceding Lemma: one has:

By reducing
\varepsilon_{C}
,
I^{C,C}_{\mathfrak{w}_{0},n}
is arbitrary close to

J^{C,C}_{\mathfrak{w}_{0},n}:=(\sum_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}}\int_{\overline{U}_{C,\mathfrak{c},\varepsilon_{C}}}|(S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}v_{n})(e_{C}(x))|^{2}dx)^{1/2}.

(8.30)

It suffices now to get a suitable bound for
J^{C,C}_{\mathfrak{w}_{0},n}
.

One has

J^{C,C}_{\mathfrak{w}_{0},n}=(\int_{U_{0}}|(S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}v_{n})(e_{C}(x))|^{2}dx)^{1/2}.

One looks to the image by
e_{C}
of the measure
dx
on
U_{0}\subset X
. Using Lemma
3.5
and coordinates, one sees that, by reducing
\varepsilon_{C}
, that this measure on
U^{\prime}_{0}:=e_{C}(U_{0})\subset X_{C}
is bounded by
(1+\eta)^{2}dx_{C}
where
dx_{C}
is given the invariant measure on
X_{C}
.
Hence

J^{C,C}_{\mathfrak{w}_{0},n}\leq(1+\eta)(\int_{U^{\prime}_{0}}|S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}v_{n}(x_{C})|^{2}dx_{C})^{1/2}\leq(1+\eta)\|S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}v_{n}\|=(1+\eta)\|S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}v\|,

the last equality being a consequence of the properties of the scattering operators (cf. Theorem
7.6
) and the unitarity of the action of
A_{I^{\prime}}^{0}
.
It follows from (
8.2
) that:

J^{C,C}_{\mathfrak{w}_{0},n}\leq(1+\eta)(1-\delta)\|v_{0}\|.

Then the Lemma follows from (
8.30
).

End of the proof of Theorem
8.1
.:

Our goal is to get a bound for
\|f\|_{L^{2}(X)}
and to get a contradiction. Our starting point is (
8.15
) and we use (
8.16
).

We summarize our results. Proposition
8.5
shows that the terms on the right hand side of the inequality (a) of (
8.16
) for
v_{n}
and the terms
I^{C,D}_{\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{c},n}
in the right hand side of the inequality (b) of (
8.16
) for
v_{n}
, are arbitrary small by reducing
\varepsilon_{C}
or taking
n
large.

From Lemma
8.6
, the terms
I^{C,C}_{\mathfrak{w},n}
for
\mathfrak{w}\not=\mathfrak{w}_{0}
in the right hand side of the inequality (b) of (
8.16
) for
v_{n}
are arbitrary small by taking
n
large.

Taking into account Lemma
8.8
, one gets a bound for

I^{C_{0},C_{0}}_{\mathfrak{w}_{0},n}
by reducing
\varepsilon_{C}
.

Proceeding by downward induction on
Card\check{C}
and using (
8.15
) and (
8.16
), this gives a bound for
\|f_{n}\|_{L^{2}(X)}=\|v_{n}\|=\|v\|
. By taking
\eta
small enough in Lemma
8.8
, one gets a contradiction:
\|v\|<\|v\|
. This achieves to prove Theorem
8.1
.

8.6
Scattering Theorem for
X
and its
G
-orbits

In
[
39
]
, section 14.3, Theorem 7.3.1 is deduced from Theorem 14.3.1.
Theorem
8.1
is the analogous of Theorem 14.3.1 of l.c.. Arguing as in l.c., we will deduce:

8.9 Theorem
.

(i) If
I,J,K\subset S
,
I\approx J\approx K
, one has:

S_{\mathfrak{w}}\>is\>an\>isometry,\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}.

i_{J,td}\circ S_{\mathfrak{w}}=i_{I,td},\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}.

S_{\mathfrak{w}}\circ S_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}=S_{\mathfrak{w}\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}},\mathfrak{w}\in W_{J,K},\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}\in W_{I,J}.

i^{*}_{J,td}\circ i_{I,td}=\sum_{\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}}S_{\mathfrak{w}}.

(ii) Let
c(I)
be equal to
\sum_{J\approx I}CardW_{I,J}
.Then the map

\sum_{I\subset S}\frac{i^{*}_{I,td}}{\sqrt{c(I)}}:L^{2}(X)\to\oplus_{I\subset S}L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}

is an isometric isomorphism onto the subspace of

(f_{I})\in\oplus_{I\subset S}L^{2}(X_{I})_{disc}

satysfying :

S_{\mathfrak{w}}f_{I}=f_{J},\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}.

Proof.

Let
I,J\subset X
with
I\approx J
and let
\mathfrak{w}_{0}\in W_{I,J}
.
Then, from Proposition
7.4
and Theorem
7.6
, one has

i^{*}_{J}i_{I,td}=i^{*}_{J,td}i_{I,td}=\sum_{\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}}S_{\mathfrak{w}}.

With the notation of Theorem
8.1
, let
m=(\gamma,n,q,{\mathcal{C}})
be any element of
{\mathcal{M}}
. Recall that
{\mathcal{H}}_{m}
is the image of the isotypic component
L^{2}(X_{I})^{\gamma}
by the spectral projection for
G\times A_{I}^{0}
corresponding to
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}\times((i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*})_{q}\cap{\mathcal{C}})
. Let
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{m}
be the image of the isotypic component
L^{2}(X_{J})_{td}^{\gamma}
by the spectral projection corresponding to
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}\times\mathfrak{w}_{0}((i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*})_{q}\cap{\mathcal{C}})
.
One has
{\mathcal{C}}
equal to some
{\mathcal{C}}_{j}
of (
7.1
). Then, from (
7.5
), for
\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}
, the restriction of
S_{\mathfrak{w}}
and
S_{\mathfrak{w},j}
to
{\mathcal{H}}_{m}
are equal. It follows from (
7.3
):

{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{m}
contains the image of
{S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}}_{|{\mathcal{H}}_{m}}
.

(8.31)

Let

p
be the natural inclusion
p:{{\mathcal{H}}}^{\prime}_{m}\to L^{2}(X_{J})_{td}
. Then one can write

(i_{J}p)^{*}{i_{I,td}}_{|{\mathcal{H}}_{m}}=p^{*}i_{J}^{*}{i_{I,td}}_{|{\mathcal{H}}_{m}}={S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}}_{|{\mathcal{H}}_{m}}+\sum_{\mathfrak{w}\not=\mathfrak{w}_{0}}p^{*}{S_{\mathfrak{w}}}_{|{\mathcal{H}}_{m}}.

One has
p^{*}{S_{\mathfrak{w}}}_{|{\mathcal{H}}_{m}}=0
if
\mathfrak{w}\not=\mathfrak{w}_{0}
from (
7.3
) and the fact that
{\mathcal{H}}_{m}
has orthogonal images by the different
S_{\mathfrak{w}}
: if
\mathfrak{w}\not=\mathfrak{w}_{0}
,
\mathfrak{w}{\mathcal{C}}\cap\mathfrak{w}_{0}{\mathcal{C}}=\emptyset
from (
7.1
). Therefore:

(i_{J}p)^{*}{i_{I,td}}_{|{\mathcal{H}}_{m}}={S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}}_{|{\mathcal{H}}_{m}}.

(8.32)

From Lemma
6.21
(ii), the map
{i_{I}}_{|{\mathcal{H}}_{m}}
is an isometry as well as
i_{J}p
.
Let us show that:

Let
a,b
be isometries such that
b^{*}a
is defined and
\|b^{*}av\|\geq\|v\|
for all
v
. Then
b^{*}a
is an isometry and
Ima\subset Imb
.

(8.33)

First
b^{*}
is an isometry on the image of
b
which is the orthogonal of the kernel of
b^{*}
. Moreover
bb^{*}
is the identity on
Imb
. Then as
\|av\|=\|v\|
, one has
\|b^{*}av\|\leq\|v\|
which implies, from our hypothesis,
\|b^{*}av\|=\|v\|
and
av\in Imb
. This proves our claim.

Using Theorem
8.1
and (
8.32
), one deduces from this, applied to
a={i_{I,td}}_{|{\mathcal{H}}_{m}}
,
b=i_{J}p
, that
{S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}}_{|{\mathcal{H}}_{m}}
is an isometry ,
i_{I}({\mathcal{H}}_{m})\subset i_{J}({\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{m})
and
(i_{J}p)(p^{*}i_{J}^{*})
is the identity on
i_{I}({\mathcal{H}}_{m})
. Using (
8.32
), one gets, as
pp^{*}=p^{*}
:

i_{J}{S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}}_{|{\mathcal{H}}_{m}}=(i_{J})(p^{*}i_{J}^{*}){i_{I}}_{|{\mathcal{H}}_{m}}=(i_{J}p)(p^{*}i_{J}^{*}){i_{I}}_{|{\mathcal{H}}_{m}}={i_{I}}_{|{\mathcal{H}}_{m}}.

(8.34)

Summing over
m\in{\mathcal{M}}
the inclusion
i_{I}({\mathcal{H}}_{m})\subset i_{J}({\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{m})
, one gets that
i_{I}(L^{2}(X_{I})_{td})
is contained in the closure of
i_{J}(L^{2}(X_{J})_{td})
, which is equal to
i_{J}(L^{2}(X_{J})_{td})
by
[
13
]
, Lemma 11.6: the image of
i_{I,\pi}
is closed as
{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
is finite dimensional and
i_{i,\pi}=Id_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}}\otimes j_{I,\pi}
.

By symmetry one gets:

i_{I}(L^{2}(X_{I})_{td})=i_{J}(L^{2}(X_{J})_{td}).

If
m\not=m^{\prime}
,
{\mathcal{H}}_{m}
(resp.
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{m}
) and
{\mathcal{H}}_{m^{\prime}}
(resp.
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}
) are orthogonal and one has

L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}=\hat{\oplus}_{m\in{\mathcal{M}}}{\mathcal{H}}_{m},L^{2}(X_{J})_{td}=\hat{\oplus}_{m\in{\mathcal{M}}}{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{m},

(8.35)

the sums being orthogonal. As
{S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}}_{|{\mathcal{H}}_{m}}
is an isometry with image in
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{m}
(cf. (
8.31
)), one deduces from this that

S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}
itself is an isometry which sends
{\mathcal{H}}_{m}
in
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{m}
for all
m\in{\mathcal{M}}
.
Taking the adjoint in Theorem
7.6
and using unicity of the scattering operators, one sees that
S^{*}_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}=S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}^{-1}}
. Hence
S^{*}_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}
is also isometric and
S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}
is unitary. Summarizing, one has proved:

S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}
is unitary and sends
{\mathcal{H}}_{m}
onto
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{m}
for all
m\in{\mathcal{M}}
.

(8.36)

Let us show that:

The closure
{\mathcal{L}}
of the
A^{0}_{J}
-span of
i^{*}_{J,td}L^{2}(X)
is equal to
L^{2}(X_{J})_{td}
.

(8.37)

Let
{\mathcal{C}}
be one of the
{\mathcal{C}}_{j}
of (
7.1
). Let
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{C}}
be the image of
P_{\mathcal{C}}
. Then
{\mathcal{L}}
contains the closure of the
A^{0}_{J}
-span of
i^{*}_{J,td}i_{I,td}({\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{C}}})
. As spectral projections preserve subrepresentations,
{\mathcal{L}}
contains
P_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}{\mathcal{C}}}i^{*}_{J,td}i_{I,td}({\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{C}}})
, which is equal to
S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{C}})
from the definition of
S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}
(see (
7.3
) and (
7.5
)). Writing
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{C}}
as the Hilbert sum of the
{\mathcal{H}}_{m}
with
m=(\gamma,n,q,{\mathcal{C}})
,
{\mathcal{L}}
contains
S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{C}}
, which, from (
8.36
), is equal to the Hilbert sum of the corresponding
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{m}
. Summing over
{\mathcal{C}}
, one gets (
8.37
).

From Proposition
7.3
(iii), one sees that
L^{2}(X)_{I}:=i_{I}(L^{2}(X_{I})_{td})
(which is closed, see above) is orthogonal to
L^{2}(X)_{J}:=i_{J}(L^{2}(X_{J})_{td})
unless
I\approx J
, in which case one has seen that they are equal.
Hence we have the refinement of
[
13
]
, Theorem 11.1:

L^{2}(X)=\oplus_{I/\approx}L^{2}(X)_{I},

the sum being orthogonal.

Let us show that, if
I\approx J
, one has:

i_{J,td}S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}=i_{I,td}

(8.38)

It is enough to check this on every
{\mathcal{H}}_{m}
. This follows from (
8.34
), where one can replace
i_{I}
(resp.
i_{J}
) by
i_{I,td}
(resp.
i_{J,td}
) as
{\mathcal{H}}_{m}\subset L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}
and
S_{\mathfrak{w}_{0}}{\mathcal{H}}_{m}={\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{m}\subset L^{2}(X_{J})_{td}
.

Let
I,J,K\subset S
as in (i),
\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}
and
\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}\in W_{J,K}
.
By (
8.38
) applied to
\mathfrak{w}
instead of
\mathfrak{w}_{0}
, one has:

i^{*}_{K}i_{J,td}{S_{\mathfrak{w}}}=i^{*}_{K}i_{I,td}.

Hence the
\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}\mathfrak{w}
-equivariant part of
i^{*}_{K}i_{I,td}
coincides with the
\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
-equivariant part of
i^{*}_{K}i_{J,td}
composed with
S_{\mathfrak{w}}
.
From this it follows:

S_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}S_{\mathfrak{w}}=S_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}\mathfrak{w}}.

(8.39)

Define the endomorphism
S
of
\oplus_{J\approx I}L^{2}(X_{J})_{td}
by :

S:=\sum_{J_{1}\approx J_{2}\approx I,\mathfrak{w}\in W_{J_{1},J_{2}}}S_{\mathfrak{w}}.

Let
c(I)=\sum_{J\approx I}CardW_{I,J}
. Then it is easily seen, using (
8.39
), that
S^{2}=c(I)S
. In particular the operator
\overline{S}=S/c(I)
is a projection. Moreover its image is the ”space of
\mathfrak{w}
-invariants”, i.e the space of the
(f_{J})\in\oplus_{J\approx I}L^{2}(X_{J})_{td}
such that for all
J_{1},J_{2}\approx I
and
\mathfrak{w}\in W_{J_{1},J_{2}}
,
S_{\mathfrak{w}}f_{J_{1}}=f_{J_{2}}
: the image of

\overline{S}
is easily seen to be contained in ” the space of
\mathfrak{w}
-invariants” and an element of the
\mathfrak{w}
-invariants is its own image by
\overline{S}
.

Set
T:=\oplus_{J\approx I}i_{J,td}
as an operator
\oplus_{J\approx I}L^{2}(X_{J})_{td}\to L^{2}(X)
. Hence
T^{*}=\oplus_{J\approx I}i^{*}_{J,td}
and
T^{*}T=S
. It follows
that the image of
T^{*}
contains ”the space of
\mathfrak{w}
-invariants”.
But, taking the adjoints in (
8.38
), one sees that
\overline{S}T^{*}=T^{*}
. It follows that the image of
T^{*}
is precisely ” the space” of
\mathfrak{w}
-invariants.

Let
f
be in the ”the space” of
\mathfrak{w}
-invariants, i.e.
\overline{S}f=f
. Hence:

\frac{T^{*}}{\sqrt{c(I)}}\frac{T}{\sqrt{c(I)}}f=\overline{S}f=f.

This implies (ii).
This achieves to prove the theorem.

We have the following corollary of (i) of the Theorem
8.9
and Theorem
7.6
, by desintegrating the
S_{\mathfrak{w}}
, which completes the Maass-Selberg relations (cf. Theorem
6.3
):

8.10 Corollary
.

Let
I,J\subset S
with
I\approx J
. Then for
\mu
-almost all
\pi\in{\hat{G}}
, the map
j^{*}_{J,\pi,td}j_{I,\pi,td}:{\mathcal{M}}^{I}_{\pi,td}\to{\mathcal{M}}^{J}_{\pi,td}
is a sum of isometries
s_{\mathfrak{w},\pi},\mathfrak{w}\in W_{I,J}
such that
for all
\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
and
\eta\in{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi,\lambda,td}
, on has
s_{\mathfrak{w},\pi}\eta\in{\mathcal{M}}_{J,\pi,\mathfrak{w}\lambda,td}
.

If
O
is a
G
-orbit in
X
and
I\in S
, one has defined (cf. (
3.32
)) a subset
{\mathcal{O}}_{I,O}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{I}
and we define
X_{I,O}\subset X_{I}
to be the union of the
G
-orbits
O^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{I,O}
. From Lemma
3.2
(v), the richt action of
A_{I}^{0}
preserves the
G
-orbits.
Hence we have a decomposition in
G\times A_{I}^{0}
-representations:

L^{2}(X_{I})_{td}=\oplus_{O\in{\mathcal{O}}}L^{2}(X_{I,O})_{td}.

One has also a decomposition into
G
-representations:

L^{2}(X)=\oplus_{O\in{\mathcal{O}}}L^{2}(O).

It follows from Lemma
6.16
that
i_{I,td}^{*}L^{2}(O)\subset L^{2}(X_{I,O})
and from the definition of
i_{I,td}
(cf. (
6.4
)) that
i_{I,td}(L^{2}(X_{I,O})_{td})\subset L^{2}(O)
. Hence these maps preserve the decompositions above.
It is the same for the
S_{\mathfrak{w}}
as it is already the case for the spectral projections for
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
an
\mathfrak{a}_{J}
used in the proof of their existences.
So one can define operators
i_{I,O,td}
,
i^{*}_{I,O,td}
,
S_{\mathfrak{w},O}
and this leads to a Theorem for
L^{2}(O)
entirely analogous to Theorem
8.9
for each
O\in{\mathcal{O}}
.

9
Spectral projections and Hilbert integrals of unitary representations

Let
G
be a locally compact group of type I. Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a Borel subset of
\hat{G}
. Let
\rho
be a continuous unitary representation of
G
in a separable Hilbert space
H
. Let us consider a central disintegration of
(\rho,H)
. Then
\rho
identifies with to
(\int_{\hat{G}}^{\oplus}\rho_{\pi}d\mu(\pi),\int_{\hat{G}}^{\oplus}H_{\pi}d\mu(\pi))
, where
(\rho_{\pi},H_{\pi})
is a representation of
G
which is a multiple of
\pi
. We may and will choose
\mu
as a probability measure as well for other measures of this kind. We define the spectral projector
P_{{\mathcal{A}},\rho}
as the orthogonal projection on
\int_{\mathcal{A}}^{\oplus}H_{\pi}d\mu(\pi)
. This projection does not depend on the choice of the central disintegration.

9.1 Proposition
.

Let
G,G^{\prime}
be locally compact groups of type I and let
(\rho,H)
be a continuous unitary representation of
G\times G^{\prime}
with central disintegration with respect to
G
,
(\int_{\hat{G}}^{\oplus}\rho_{\pi}d\mu(\pi),\int_{\hat{G}}^{\oplus}H_{\pi}d\mu(\pi))
. Then for
\mu
-almost all
\pi\in{\hat{G}}
,
G^{\prime}
acts on
H_{\pi}
by a representation denoted
\rho^{\prime}_{\pi}
. Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a Borel subset of
{\hat{G}}^{\prime}
.
Then the family of projectors
\pi\mapsto P_{{\mathcal{A}},\rho^{\prime}_{\pi}}
is
\mu
-measurable and
P_{{\mathcal{A}},\rho}=\int_{{\hat{G}}}^{\oplus}P_{{\mathcal{A}},\rho^{\prime}_{\pi}}d\mu(\pi)
.

Proof.

One considers a central disintegration of
\rho
as a representation of
G\times G^{\prime}
. Hence we can identify
(\rho,H)
with

(\int_{{\hat{G}}\times{\hat{G}}^{\prime}}^{\oplus}\rho_{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}d\hat{\mu}(\pi,\pi^{\prime}),\int_{{\hat{G}}\times{\hat{G}}^{\prime}}^{\oplus}H_{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}d\hat{\mu}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})).

Let
\mu_{1}
(resp.
\mu^{\prime}_{1}
) be the image of the measure
\hat{\mu}
by the first projection
p:{\hat{G}}\times{\hat{G}}^{\prime}\to{\hat{G}}
(resp. second projection:
p^{\prime}:{\hat{G}}\times{\hat{G}}^{\prime}\to{\hat{G}}^{\prime}
) ). Then
\hat{\mu}
admits a disintegration with respect to
\mu_{1}
(resp.
\mu_{1}^{\prime}
) in measures
\mu^{\prime}_{\pi}
on
{\hat{G}}\times{\hat{G}}^{\prime}
(resp
\mu_{\pi^{\prime}}
on
{\hat{G}}\times{\hat{G}}^{\prime}
). Moreover
\mu^{\prime}_{\pi}
is zero outside
\{\pi\}\times{\hat{G}}^{\prime}
(resp.
\mu_{\pi^{\prime}}
is zero outside
{\hat{G}}\times\{\pi^{\prime}\}
), hence might be viewed as a measure on
{\hat{G}}^{\prime}
(resp.
{\hat{G}}
).
Then there are natural isomorphisms

i:\int_{{\hat{G}}\times{\hat{G}}^{\prime}}^{\oplus}H_{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}d\hat{\mu}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\to\int^{\oplus}_{{\hat{G}}}(\int^{\oplus}_{{\hat{G}}^{\prime}}H_{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}d\mu^{\prime}_{\pi}(\pi^{\prime}))d\mu_{1}(\pi).

(9.1)

i^{\prime}:\int_{{\hat{G}}\times{\hat{G}}^{\prime}}^{\oplus}H_{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}d\hat{\mu}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\to\int^{\oplus}_{{\hat{G}}^{\prime}}(\int^{\oplus}_{{\hat{G}}}H_{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}d\mu_{\pi^{\prime}}(\pi))d\mu^{\prime}_{1}(\pi^{\prime}).

(9.2)

Then the right hand of (
9.1
) (resp. (
9.2
)) is a central disintegration of
\rho
viewed as a representation
G
(resp.
G^{\prime}
) and
\mu_{1}
is a spectral measure for the representation
\rho
of
G
. There is no harm to prove the Proposition for this central disintegration of
\rho
with respect to
G
.
Let
v\in H
. Looking to the isomorphism
i^{\prime}
, one sees that
P_{{\mathcal{A}},\rho}v
is equal to
v_{\mathcal{A}}
defined by

v_{\mathcal{A}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})=v(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
if
\pi^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{A}}
and 0 otherwise. Then
i(v_{{\mathcal{A}}})
is a measurable field and has the required form.

9.2 Proposition
.

One keeps the notation of the preceding proposition and its proof.
We assume that, for
\mu
-almost all
\pi\in\hat{G}
,
H_{\pi}
, as a representation of
G\times G^{\prime}
, is a representation of the form
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\hat{\otimes}{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
, where
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}
is a representation of
G
unitarily equivalent to
\pi
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
is a finite sum of unitary irreducible representations of
G^{\prime}
. Let
{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}=\oplus_{\pi^{\prime}\in A(\pi)}{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}
be its decomposition in multiple of irreducible representations of
G^{\prime}
:
A(\pi)
is a finite subset of
{\hat{G}}^{\prime}
possibly empty if
{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}=\{0\}
. Then there is a central disintegration of the representation
\rho
of
G\times G^{\prime}
, with corresponding measure
\hat{\mu}^{c}
on
{\hat{G}}\times{\hat{G}}^{\prime}
, such that, for
\mu
-almost all
\pi
,
(\hat{\mu}^{c})^{\prime}_{\pi}
is the counting measure on
A(\pi)
and
H_{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}={\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\hat{\otimes}{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}
if
\pi^{\prime}\in A(\pi)
and zero otherwise.

Proof.

One takes a central disintegration of the representation
\rho
of
G\times G^{\prime}
, as in the proof of the preceding Proposition with spectral measure
\hat{\mu}
(cf. (
9.1
)). We use the notation of this proof. As the right hand side of (
9.1
) is a central disintegration for the representation
\rho
of
G
(see the proof above),
\mu_{1}
is a spectral measure for this representation. Then
\mu
and
\mu_{1}
are equivalent. By multiplying
\hat{\mu}
by a measurable function of
\pi\in\hat{G}
, one can assume that
\mu_{1}=\mu
. We will assume this in the sequel. Hence from the unicity of the central disintegration, for
\mu
-almost all
\pi\in{\hat{G}}
one has:

\int^{\oplus}_{{\hat{G}}^{\prime}}H_{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}d\mu^{\prime}_{\pi}(\pi^{\prime})\simeq\oplus_{\pi^{\prime}\in A(\pi)}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\hat{\otimes}{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}.

(9.3)

The set
{\hat{G}}_{1}
of
\pi
such that
A(\pi)
is not empty is
\mu
-measurable as it is the set of
\pi
such that
\mu^{\prime}_{\pi}\not=0
.
For
\pi\in{\hat{G}}_{1}
,
\mu^{\prime}_{1,\pi}:=\mu^{\prime}_{\pi}
is an atomic measure with atoms the elements of
A(\pi)
, otherwise it is zero.

As
G^{\prime}
is of type I, the Borel structure on
{\hat{G}}^{\prime}
is given by the Borel structure of a standard Borel set in which the finite sets are closed hence borelian.
One wants to modify
\hat{\mu}
, up to equivalence, in order that for
\mu
-almost
\pi
,
\mu^{\prime}_{\pi}
is the counting measure on
A(\pi)
. If it has this property, it satisfies the statement of the Proposition by uniqueness of the central disintegration of
{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
and (
9.3
).

Let us first show that
A(\pi)\subset{\hat{G}}^{\prime}
varies weakly measurably in
\pi\in{\hat{G}}
, i.e. if
U
is a measurable subset of
{\hat{G}}^{\prime}
,
the set
V:=\{\pi\in{\hat{G}}|U\cap A(\pi)\not=\emptyset\}
is
\mu
-measurable.

In fact, from the definitions,

V=\{\pi\in{\hat{G}}|\mu^{\prime}_{\pi}({\hat{G}}\times U)\not=0\}
which is
\mu
-measurable as the family of measure
(\mu^{\prime}_{\pi})
is
\mu
-measurable by the disintegration theorem.

Then using the measurable selection theorem of Kuratowski-Ryll-Nardzewski, one can define a
\mu
-measurable map
\alpha_{1}:{\hat{G}}_{1}\to{\hat{G}}^{\prime}
such that
\alpha_{1}(\pi)\in A(\pi)
for
\mu
-almost all
\pi
.

Let
A
be a Borel subset of
{\hat{G}}\times{\hat{G}}^{\prime}
. Let
\nu_{1,\pi}=\delta_{(\pi,\alpha_{1}(\pi))}
be the Dirac measure at
(\pi,\alpha_{1}(\pi))
on
{\hat{G}}\times{\hat{G}}^{\prime}
and zero if
\pi\in{\hat{G}}\setminus{\hat{G}}_{1}
. Then if
\pi\in{\hat{G}}_{1}

\nu_{1,\pi}(A)=1_{A}(\pi,\alpha_{1}(\pi)),

where
1_{A}
is the characteristic function of
A
and zero otherwise. Hence, using composition of measurable functions one sees that
(\nu_{1,\pi})
is a
\mu
-measurable family of measures on
{\hat{G}}\times{\hat{G}}^{\prime}
.

Let us observe that the set
({\hat{G}}\times{\hat{G}}^{\prime})_{1}:=\{(\pi,\alpha_{1}(\pi))|\pi\in{\hat{G}}_{1}\}
is a measurable subset of
{\hat{G}}\times{\hat{G}}^{\prime}
as a graph of a measurable map is measurable. Let
d
be a continuous distance function on the standard Borel set whose Borel structure gives the Borel structure on
{\hat{G}}^{\prime}
. Then
(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\mapsto d(\pi^{\prime},\alpha_{1}(\pi))
is measurable on
{\hat{G}}\times{\hat{G}}^{\prime}
and its zero set is
({\hat{G}}\times{\hat{G}}^{\prime})_{1}
.

Then one defines

\mu^{\prime}_{1,\pi}:=\mu^{\prime}_{\pi},

\theta_{1,\pi}:=1_{({\hat{G}}\times{\hat{G}}^{\prime})_{1}}\mu^{\prime}_{1,\pi},

\mu^{\prime}_{2,\pi}=\mu^{\prime}_{1,\pi}-\theta_{1,\pi}.

These are
\mu
-measurable family of measures on
\hat{G}\times\hat{G}^{\prime}
.

The set of atoms of
\mu^{\prime}_{2,\pi}
is empty if
\pi\notin{\hat{G}}_{1}
, and contained in
\{(\pi,\pi^{\prime})|\pi^{\prime}\in A(\pi)\setminus\{\alpha_{1}(\pi)\}
if
\pi\in{\hat{G}}_{1}
.
Proceeding inductively one finds a finite sequence of
\mu
-measurable families of measures such
\nu_{n,\pi}
on
{\hat{G}}\times{\hat{G}}^{\prime}
such that for
\mu
-almost all
\pi\in{\hat{G}}
,

\sum_{n}\nu_{n,\pi}
is the counting measure
\mu^{\prime c}_{\pi}
on
\{\pi\}\times A(\pi)
. Hence this family of counting measures is
\mu
-measurable.

We define

\hat{\mu}^{c}:=\int_{\hat{G}}\mu^{\prime c}_{\pi}d\mu(\pi).

It has the required property of disintegration. Using disintegration of measures and the fact that
\mu^{\prime}_{\pi}
and
\mu^{{}^{\prime}c}_{\pi}
are equivalent one sees that the measures
\hat{\mu}
and
\hat{\mu}^{c}
have the same borelian set of measure zero. Hence they are equivalent.
Then
\hat{\mu}^{c}
satisfies the properties of the Proposition.

10
A measurability property

Let
(Y,m)
a measure space.
If
F
is a Fréchet space, we define a step function on
Y
with values in
F
as a function which is constant on each of a finite number of disjoint measurable sets whose union is the whole space. A function
f
on
Y
with values in
F
is said measurable if it is the strong limit almost everywhere of a sequence of step functions.
Actually one has:

If
F
is a separable Banach space,
f
is measurable if and only it is approximated uniformly, outside a set of measure zero, by a sequence of countably valued measurable functions (cf.
[
37
]
, Corollary 1.12).

(10.1)

There is an obvious definition of weak measurability of a map on
Y
with values in
F
: the complex valued function given by composition with any continuous linear form has to be measurable.
We will prove:

For functions in separable Fréchet spaces, weak measurability implies measurability.

(10.2)

For Banach spaces, this is due to Pettis (cf.
[
37
]
, Theorem 1.1).

Let
F
be a separable Fréchet space whose topology is given by a sequence of norms
p_{n}
that we can assume to be increasing. Let
F_{n}
be the Banach completion of
F
endowed with the norm
p_{n}
which is separable as
F
is separable. Let
f
be a function from
Y
to
F
which is weakly-measurable. Then, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, it is also weakly measurable hence measurable as a function in
F_{n}
by Pettis’ theorem. By (
10.1
), for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a countably valued measurable function on
Y
,
g_{n}
, with values in
F_{n}
, such that outside a set of measure zero,
p_{n}(g_{n}(y))-f(y))<1/2n
. Using first the definition of measurability for
F_{n}
and then the density of
F
in
F_{n}
, one can find for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, a step function
f_{n}
on
Y
with values in
F
such that
p_{n}(f_{n}(y)-g_{n}(y))<1/2n
outside a set of measure zero. Using that
(p_{n})
is increasing, one sees that for
k\leq n
,

p_{k}(f_{n}(y)-f(y))<1/n
outside a set of measure zero. Hence the sequence
(f_{n})
of functions in
F
converges strongly almost everywhere to
f
. Hence
f
is a function in
F
which is measurable. This proves our claim.

Let us show:

The space
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
is separable.

(10.3)

This might be seen as follows.
Let
(f_{n})
be a dense sequence in
L^{2}(X)
of compactly supported functions. Let
(g_{n})
be a Dirac sequence in
C_{c}^{\infty}(G)
. Then one sees easily that
(f_{n,p})=(g_{p}\star f_{n})
is dense in
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
.

We use the notation of section
4.2
.

Let
p,k\in\mathbb{N},N\in\mathbb{R}
. As
L^{2}_{-N,p}(X)\subset L^{2}_{-N}(X)
, one may view
L^{2}_{-N,p}(X)
as a subspace of
L^{2}_{-N,-k}(X):=(L^{2}_{N,k}(X))^{\prime}
.
Let us show:

The space
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
is a dense subspace of the continuous dual
L^{2}_{N,-p}(X)
of
L^{2}_{-N,p}(X)
.

(10.4)

Notice that
L^{2}_{-N,p}(X)
is an Hilbert space, hence is reflexive.
To prove the density of
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
, one looks to the orthogonal of
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
in
L^{2}_{-N,p}(X)\subset L^{2}_{-N}(X)
which is clearly reduced to zero. Our claim follows.

Let
p,k\in\mathbb{N}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
. Let
(Y,m)
be a measured space. Let
f:Y\to L^{2}_{-N,p}(X)(resp.\>C^{\infty}_{temp,N}(X))\>\subset L^{2}_{-N,-k}(X)
be a measurable function as a function into

L^{2}_{-N,-k}(X)
.
Then it is measurable as a function into

L^{2}_{-N,p}(X)
(resp.
C^{\infty}_{temp,N}(X)
).

(10.5)

Let us prove the first claim. If
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
, the scalar map on
Y
,
<\phi,f>
is measurable.
Let
T
be an element of the dual
L^{2}_{N,-p}(X)
of
L^{2}_{-N,p}(X)
. By (
10.4
),
T
can be approximated by a sequence of elements of
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
,
\phi_{n}
. Hence
<T,f>
is measurable as a simple limit of a sequence of measurable scalar functions. Using Pettis theorem for Banach spaces, one gets the first claim. Let us recall (cf. (
4.13
)) that

C^{\infty}_{temp,N}(X)=L^{2}_{-N}(X)^{\infty}.

The second claim follows from the first one and from the theorem of Pettis for Fréchet spaces, as any continuous linear form on
L^{2}_{-N}(X)^{\infty}
extends continuously to

L^{2}_{-N,p}(X)
for some
p\in\mathbb{N}
.

We use the notation of section
4.1
and we make the choice of
N
and
k
as in (
4.22
).

10.1 Lemma
.

Let
v=\int_{\hat{G}}^{\oplus}v_{\pi}d\mu(\pi)\in\int_{\hat{G}}^{\oplus}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}d\mu(\pi)
be
K
-finite, Then:

(i)
For
\mu
-almost all
\pi
, one has
f_{\pi}:=\beta_{\pi}(v_{\pi})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X)
.

(ii)
\pi\mapsto f_{\pi}
is
\mu
-measurable as a map from
{\hat{G}}
to
L^{2}_{-N,p}(X)
for all
p\in\mathbb{N}
.

(iii)
\pi\mapsto f_{\pi}
is
\mu
-measurable as a map from
{\hat{G}}
to
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X)
.

Proof.

(i) follows from (
4.24
) and from the fact that
K
-finite elements in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}
are
C^{\infty}
-vectors.

(ii) Let us show the map
\pi\mapsto f_{\pi}
is measurable as a function into
L^{2}_{-N,-k}(X)
. By Pettis Theorem it is enough to prove that it is weakly measurable. As
L^{2}_{N,k}(X)
is a Hilbert space, the continuous dual of
L^{2}_{-N,-k}(X)
is
L^{2}_{N,k}(X)
. If
h\in L^{2}_{N,k}(X)
,

<h,f_{\pi}>=<h,\beta_{\pi}(v_{\pi})>=<\alpha_{\pi}(h),v_{\pi}>,

which is measurable as
\pi\mapsto\alpha_{\pi}(f)
is a measurable field (cf. (
4.19
)). Our claim follows. Then (ii) follows from the first claim of (
10.5
).

(iii) follows from the second claim of (
10.5
).

10.2 Lemma
.

Let
\mathbb{X}
be the set of characters of
Z(\mathfrak{g})
which have a Harish-Chanda parameter in the set
\Lambda\in\mathfrak{j}_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}|(\rho_{\underline{X}}+\Lambda)_{|\mathfrak{j}_{H}}=0\}
. If
\mathbb{Y}\subset\mathbb{X}
, let
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{Y}}(X))
be the set of functions on
X
which are non zero elements of
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X)
and which are eigen for a character of
Z(\mathfrak{g})
belonging to
\mathbb{Y}
. Then
\mathbb{X}
is the union of a finite family of borelian subsets of
\mathbb{X}
such that, for any
\mathbb{Y}
in this family, the constant term map,
f\mapsto f_{I}
, is continuous from
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{Y}}(X)
, endowed with the topology of
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X)
, to
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N^{\prime}}(X_{I})
for
N^{\prime}
sufficiently large and independent of
\mathbb{Y}
.

Proof.

We start with functions which are zero outside
G/H
. For other orbits, one has only to change the base point.

Let us first show:

Let
\mathbb{Y}\subset\mathbb{X}
and
(f_{n})
be a converging sequence in
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{Y}}(X)
to
f\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{Y}}(X)
. If the sequence of the constant terms of the
f_{n}
,
(f_{n,I})
, converges simply to
f_{I}
, and similarly for the left derivatives by elements of
U(\mathfrak{g})
, then it converges also in
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N^{\prime}}(X_{I})
for
N^{\prime}
sufficiently large and independent of
\mathbb{Y}
.

(10.6)

Let us recall that one has defined seminorms
q_{-N},p_{-N}
for functions on
X
(cf (
4.12
)). We denote by
q_{-N}^{I},p_{-N}^{I}
the corresponding seminorms for functions on
X_{I}
. As
(f_{n})
converges, it is bounded in
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X)
. Then it follows from Theorem
5.4
(ii) that
(p_{-N-N_{I}}^{I}(f_{n,I}))
is bounded. This is a uniform bound for the functions
f_{n,I}
. Using (
4.11
), this implies that, for
N^{\prime}
sufficiently large, the sequence
(p_{-N^{\prime}}^{I}(f_{n,I}))
is bounded. Moreover from our hypothesis of simple convergence of the sequence
(f_{n,I})
and our uniform bound, one can use again (
4.11
) and apply Lebesgue dominated convergence to get that the sequence
(p_{-N^{\prime}}^{I}(f_{n,I}-f_{I}))
tends to zero. Applying this to derivatives, this proves our claim.

One covers
\mathbb{X}
by the finite family of
\mathbb{X}_{\Lambda_{j}}
,
j=1,\dots,s
, of
[
8
]
Lemma 8.4, which are borelian (cf. l.c. Lemma 8.3). In l.c. Lemma 8.4, for each
\Lambda\in\mathbb{X}_{\Lambda_{j}}
a representation
\rho_{\Lambda}
of
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
in a fixed space
U_{j}
is defined. Let
\Gamma_{\Lambda}(\underline{x})={}^{t}\!\rho_{\Lambda}(\underline{x}),\underline{x}\in\mathfrak{a}_{I}
. A joint eigenvalue,
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{I,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, of the
\Gamma_{\Lambda}(\underline{x}),\underline{x}\in\mathfrak{a}_{I}
is called an eigenvalue of
\Gamma_{\Lambda}
.

Let
\underline{x}_{1},\dots,\underline{x}_{p}
, be a basis of
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
. We fix
j
. Then (cf. l.c. Lemma 8.4 (i) )
\Gamma_{\Lambda}(\underline{x}_{k})
(or
\rho_{\Lambda}(\underline{x}_{k})
) is varying continuously for
\Lambda\in\mathbb{X}_{\Lambda_{j}}
. From l.c. Lemma 8.4 (ii), there exist continuous functions on
\mathbb{X}_{\Lambda_{j}}
,
\lambda_{1}(\Lambda),\dots,\lambda_{n}(\Lambda)
such that the set
J(\Lambda)
of eigenvalues of
\Gamma_{\Lambda}(\underline{x}_{1})
is contained in
I(\Lambda):=\{\lambda_{1}(\Lambda),\dots,\lambda_{n}(\Lambda)\}
.

Let
{\mathcal{I}}\subset\{1,\dots,n\}
. The set of
\mathbb{X}_{j,{\mathcal{I}}}
of
\Lambda\in\mathbb{X}_{\Lambda_{j}}
such that
I(\Lambda)=\{\lambda_{i}(\Lambda)|i\in{\mathcal{I}}\}
is closed in
\mathbb{X}_{\Lambda_{j}}
, as the set of roots of the characteristic polynomials of
\Gamma_{\Lambda}(\underline{x}_{1})
vary continuously (use the elementary symmetric functions of the roots). Moreover the set
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}_{j,{\mathcal{I}}}
of
\Lambda\in\mathbb{X}_{j,{\mathcal{I}}}
for which the
\lambda_{i}(\Lambda),i\in{\mathcal{I}}
are all distincts is open
\mathbb{X}_{j,{\mathcal{I}}}
. Hence the set
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}_{j,{\mathcal{I}}}
is borelian. Then
\Gamma_{\Lambda}(\underline{x}_{1})
is continuous in
\Lambda\in\mathbb{X}^{\prime}_{j,{\mathcal{I}}}
and the same is true for the spectral projection of
\Gamma_{\Lambda}(\underline{x}_{1})
corresponding to
\lambda_{i}(\Lambda)
, as it is seen from the description of the spectral projections as a contour integral of the resolvent. When
{\mathcal{I}}
varies, the
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}_{j,{\mathcal{I}}}
’s form a finite covering of
\mathbb{X}_{\Lambda_{j}}
by borelian sets.

Doing the same for every
\underline{x}_{k}
and taking intersections, we get a covering of
\mathbb{X}_{\Lambda_{j}}
by borelian subsets on which the spectral projections of
\Gamma_{\Lambda}
are continuous.

Let
\mathbb{Y}
be one of the sets of this covering. Let
(f_{n})\subset{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{Y}}(X)
be a convergent sequence to
f\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{Y}}(X)\subset{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X)
.

Let
\chi_{n}
(resp.
\chi
) be the infinitesimal character of
f_{n}
(resp.
f
). Let
z\in Z(\mathfrak{g})
. Then
(L_{z}f_{n})=(\chi_{n}(z)f_{n})
converges to
L_{z}f=\chi(z)f
. Hence
((\chi_{n}(z)-\chi(z))f_{n})
converges to zero. Taking the seminorm
q_{-N}
, going to the limit and using that
f
is non zero, we get that
(\chi_{n}(z))
tends to
\chi(z)
. This implies that the parameters
\Lambda_{n}\in\mathfrak{j}^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}/W
of
\chi_{n}
tend to the parameter
\Lambda
of
\chi
.

Let us describe how
f_{I}(1)
is obtained in
[
8
]
.

One constructs (cf. l.c.(5.24) and (5.27)), by some linear combinations of some derivatives of
f
and for
\underline{x}_{I}\in\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{--}
, two functions
\Phi_{f}
,
\Psi_{f,\underline{x}_{I}}
on
X
with values on the space of the representation
\Gamma_{\Lambda}
. Then (cf. l.c.(5.35) and (5.36)), if
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*}
is a unitary eigenvalue of
\Gamma_{\Lambda},
we apply the corresponding spectral projection
E_{\lambda}
to
\Phi_{f}
to define
\Phi_{f,\lambda}=E_{\lambda}\Phi_{f}
and

\Phi_{f,\lambda,\infty}(1,\underline{x}_{I}):=\Phi_{f,\lambda}(1)+\int_{0}^{\infty}E_{\lambda}\exp^{-s\Gamma_{\Lambda}(\underline{x}_{I})}\Psi_{f,\underline{x}_{I}}(\exp s\underline{x}_{I})ds,

which does not depend on
\underline{x}_{I}
and is denoted by
\Phi_{f,\lambda,\infty}(1)
(cf. l.c. before Lemma 5.19). Then (cf. lc. section 6.1), there is a linear form on the space of
\Gamma_{\Lambda}
, denoted by
l_{\Lambda}
, such that

f_{I}(1)=\sum_{\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{*},\lambda\>eigenvalue\>of\>\Gamma_{\Lambda}}<l_{\Lambda},\Phi_{f,\lambda,\infty}(1)>.

But the space of
\Gamma_{\Lambda}
is independent of
\Lambda\in\mathbb{Y}
(cf. our choice of
\mathbb{Y}
and l.c (8.4)) and the same is true for
l_{\Lambda}
.

From the tempered embedding theorem (cf l.c. Theorem 7.2) and
[
30
]
(see Lemma
6.9
), the real parts of the characters
\Lambda_{n}
vary in a discrete set. Hence, if
n
is large enough, these real parts are independent of
n
. From the properties of
\mathbb{Y}
, the unitary eigenvalues of
\Gamma_{\Lambda_{n}}
vary in families
(\lambda_{n})
converging to some
\lambda
, and the corresponding spectral projections converge, by the properties of
\mathbb{Y}
. Then
(\Phi_{f_{n},\lambda_{n}}(1))
tends to
\Phi_{f,\lambda}(1)
: Lemma 5.8 (ii) of l.c. allow to deal with the convergence of the integrals in the definition of
\Phi_{f_{n},\lambda,\infty}(1,\underline{x}_{I})
.

For the remaining term which defines
f_{n,I}(1)
, one has to study the sequence of integrals

I_{n}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}E_{\lambda}e^{-\Gamma_{\Lambda_{n}}(\underline{x}_{I})}\Psi_{f_{n},\underline{x}_{I}}(\exp s\underline{x}_{I})ds.

The integrands converge simply. A uniform bound is given by the boundedness of the sequence
(f_{n})
in
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X)
and l.c. Lemma 5.8 (ii). Hence this sequence of integrals converges. Altogether, it follows that
(f_{n,I}(1))
tends to
f_{I}(1)
. By
G
-equivariance, the same is true if one changes
1
into any
x
in the
G
-orbit
G/H
. One can do the same for the left derivatives of
f
by elements of
U(\mathfrak{g})
. The derivatives of
f
and of the
f_{n}
might vanish. But the fact that the
f_{n}
and
f
are non zero is used only to prove the convergence of
\Lambda_{n}
to
\Lambda
, which can now be used.

Then the Lemma follows from (
10.6
).

10.3 Proposition
.

Let
v\in{\mathcal{H}}_{I}:=\int^{\oplus}_{\hat{G}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}d\mu(\pi)
.
Then
\pi\mapsto i_{I,\pi}v_{\pi}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
is a measurable field.

Proof.

One can reduce to
v
in a fixed
K
-isotypic component of
{\mathcal{H}}_{I}
,
{\mathcal{H}}_{I}^{\gamma}:=\int^{\oplus}_{\hat{G}}{\mathcal{H}}^{\gamma}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}d\mu(\pi)
, for a
K
-type
\gamma\in\hat{K}
. We have the analogue for
X_{I}
of the the maps of
\beta_{\pi}
and
\alpha_{\pi}
(cf. (
4.22
)). Let
N^{\prime}
as in the previous Lemma. Let us first assume that
v=\alpha_{I}(f)
with
f\in L^{2}_{N^{\prime},k}(X_{I})^{\gamma}
.
Let
(e_{q})
be a field of orthonormal basis (cf.
[
15
]
, Part II, Chapter 1, Proposition 1) in the Hilbert integral
{\mathcal{H}}^{\gamma}:=\int^{\oplus}_{\hat{G}}{\mathcal{H}}^{\gamma}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}d\mu(\pi)
.

Let us show that:

\pi\mapsto i_{I,\pi}v_{\pi}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\pi}
is a measurable field.

(10.7)

It is enough to show (cf
[
15
]
, Part II, Chapter 1, Proposition 2) that, for all
q
,

\pi\mapsto(i_{I,\pi}v_{\pi},e_{q,\pi})
is measurable on a countable family of measurable sets covering
{\hat{G}}
.

One has:

(i_{I,\pi}v_{\pi},e_{q,\pi})=(\alpha_{I,\pi}(f),i_{I,\pi}^{*}e_{q,\pi}).

Unwinding the definitions we get:

(i_{I,\pi}v_{\pi},e_{q,\pi})=(f,(\beta_{\pi}(e_{q,\pi}))_{I}).

(10.8)

First
\pi\mapsto(i_{I,\pi}v_{\pi},e_{q,\pi})
is measurable on the set
V_{0}
of
\pi
such that
\beta_{\pi}(e_{q,\pi})=0
, which is a measurable subset of
{\hat{G}}
. Let
V_{0}^{c}
be its complementary set in
{\hat{G}}
.

Let
\mathbb{Y}
be one of the sets of the previous Lemma. Let
{\hat{G}}_{\mathbb{Y}}
be the set of
\pi\in{\hat{G}}
whose infinitesimal character is contained in
\mathbb{Y}
. As
\mathbb{Y}
is borelian,
[
3
]
implies that
{\hat{G}}_{\mathbb{Y}}
is a borelian subset of
{\hat{G}}
. The map
\pi\mapsto\beta_{\pi}(e_{q,\pi})
on
{\hat{G}}_{\mathbb{Y}}\cap V_{0}^{c}
has its values in
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{Y}}(X)
. Moreover from Lemma
10.1
(iii), it is measurable with values in
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{Y}}(X)\subset{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X)
.

Then on each subset
{\hat{G}}_{\mathbb{Y}}\cap V_{0}^{C}
and for all
q\in\mathbb{N}
, the map
\pi\mapsto(\beta_{\pi}(e_{q,\pi}))_{I})
is measurable with values in
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N^{\prime}}(X_{I})
, by Lemma
10.2
, as the composition of a continuous map with a measurable map is measurable. Hence, as
f\in L^{2}_{N^{\prime},k}(X)
is in the topological dual of
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N^{\prime}}(X_{I})
,
\pi\mapsto(f,(\beta_{\pi}(e_{q,\pi}))_{I})
is measurable on
{\hat{G}}_{\mathbb{Y}}\cap V_{0}^{c}
. By (
10.8
), this is also true for
\pi\mapsto(i_{I,\pi}v_{\pi},e_{q,\pi})
. Altogether we have proved (
10.7
) with our hypothesis on
v
.

Now take any
v\in\int^{\oplus}_{\hat{G}}{\mathcal{H}}^{\gamma}_{\pi}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{I,\pi}d\mu(\pi)
. It is the limit of some sequence of
v_{n}
as above.
Then, replacing
(v_{n})
by a subsequence and using
[
15
]
, Part II, Chapter 1, Proposition 5, we can assume also that for
\mu
-almost all
\pi
,
v_{n,\pi}
converges to
v_{\pi}
. Using the continuity of
i_{I,\pi}
, one sees that for
\mu
-almost all
\pi
,
i_{I,\pi}v_{n,\pi}
converges to
i_{I,\pi}v_{\pi}
.
The measurability of
\pi\mapsto i_{I,\pi}v_{\pi}
follows. This implies the Proposition

11
A property of the constant term

11.1
Equivalence of weights

We have the notion of norm on an affine algebraic variety defined over
\mathbb{R}
defined by R. Kottwitz in
[
29
]
section 18.2, p.468. If
Y
is the set of real points of such a variety, one has the notion of log-norm defined in
[
7
]
section 1.2: it is a function of the form
1+\log\|.\|
, where
\|.\|
is a norm on
Y
.
Notice that, from l.c. they are all equivalent in the sense of (
2.1
). We will choose such a log-norm
\sigma_{Y}
on
Y
for each
Y
.

Let
X
be as in the main body of the article. Then
\sigma_{X}
is a weight on
X
: this follows from
[
29
]
, Proposition 18.1 (7).
The proof of the following Lemma was communicated to me by R. Beuzart-Plessis.

11.1 Lemma
.

Let
\mathbf{w}
be as in (
4.10
). The weight
1+\mathbf{w}
is equivalent to
\sigma_{X}
. In particular its equivalence class does not depend on any choice.

Proof.

One has the polar decomposition of
X
(cf. (
4.9
)),
X=\Omega A_{X}^{-}{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
, where
\Omega
is compact. From properties of weights, it is enough to prove the equivalence on
A_{X}^{-}{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
and, by changing the base point, on
A_{X}^{-}
. From
[
24
]
, Proposition 3.4 (2) the restriction of
1+\mathbf{w}
is equivalent to
\sigma_{A_{X}}
on
A_{X}^{-}
. To finish the proof of the Lemma, one has only to prove:

\sigma_{A_{X}}(x)\approx\sigma_{X}(x),x\in A_{X}^{-}.

(11.1)

Let
\overline{X}_{P}
(resp.
X_{P}
) be the union of the open
P
-orbits in the
G
-orbits of
\overline{X}
(resp.
X
). Then
X_{P}=X\cap\overline{X}_{P}
.

Let
Y
be as above before the Lemma. Let us show that if
U,V
are open in
Y
, one has:

\sigma_{U\cap V}(x)\approx max(\sigma_{U}(x),\sigma_{V}(x)),x\in U\cap V,

as this follows from
[
29
]
Proposition 18.1 (1), (4) and from the fact the natural map
U\cap V\to U\times V
is an immersion.
Hence

\sigma_{X_{P}}(x)\approx max(\sigma_{X}(x),\sigma_{\overline{X}_{P}}(x)),x\in X_{P}.

By LST,
A_{X}^{-}
is relatively compact in
\overline{X}_{P}
, hence
\sigma_{\overline{X}_{P}}
is bounded on it (cf. lc. Proposition 18.1 (3) and this implies:

\sigma_{X_{P}}(x)\approx\sigma_{X}(x),x\in A_{X}^{-}.

But as
A_{X}
is closed in
X_{P}
, one has from
[
29
]
, Proposition 18.1 (2):

\sigma_{A_{X}}(x)\approx\sigma_{X_{P}}(x),x\in A_{X}.

Then (
11.1
) follows. This achieves the proof of the Lemma.

11.2 Remark
.

The Lemma implies that the definition of
C^{\infty}_{temp,N}(X)
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp,N}(X)
(cf. section
4.2
) does not depend on any choice: one can replace in its definition
1+\mathbf{w}
by any log-norm on
X
.

11.2
Independence of the constant term of all choices

The following Theorem and its proof were suggested a long time ago by Yiannis Sakellaridis. We thank him warmly for his suggestion.

11.3 Theorem
.

Let
C\in{\mathcal{F}}
.

The map
f\mapsto f_{C}
is the unique
G
-equivariant map
{\mathcal{A}}_{temp}(X)\to{\mathcal{A}}_{temp}(X_{C})
such that for all

C^{2}
-path
\gamma:[0,\varepsilon[\to\overline{X}
such that:

(1)
\gamma(t)\in X
for
t>0
,
\gamma(0)=y\in Y_{C}
.

(2) The normal derivative in
0
, with respect to
Y_{C}
, of
\gamma
is equal to
v
with
(y,v)\in X_{C}

and for all
C^{2}
-path
\gamma_{C}:[0,\varepsilon[\to N_{Y_{C}}(\overline{X})
such that:

(1’)
\gamma_{C}(t)\in X_{C}
, for
t>0
,
\gamma_{C}(0)=(y,0)
.

(2’) Its normal derivative in
0
, with respect to
Y_{C}
, is equal to
v
,

one has:

\lim_{t\to+\infty}a_{0}(t)^{-\rho}((f(g\gamma(t))-f_{C}(g\gamma_{C}(t)))=0,g\in G.

Proof.

The unicity follows from the unicity in Theorem
5.1
where a base point has been fixed and special paths are used.
One has to verify that it satisfies the asymptotic property of the theorem. By
G
-equivariance it is enough to prove the property for
(y,v)
in a set of representatives of the
G
-orbits in
X_{C}
. Thus from (
3.29
), we can assume
(y,v)=wx_{C},w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
. We will treat only the case
w=1
, the other cases are analogous by changing the base point. In particular
(y,v)=x_{C}
, hence
y=y_{C},v=e_{C}
(cf. Lemma
3.1
(iii) for notation).

Let
\gamma
and
\gamma_{C}
be as in the statement of the Theorem with
y=y_{C}
and
v=e_{C}
.

Let
I=I(C)
. Let
a_{0}(t)=\exp(-(\log t)e_{C})\in A_{C}
. Then, let us show that
e_{C}\in\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{--}
. First it is in
\mathfrak{a}_{I}^{-}
as
C
is contained in
\mathfrak{a}_{I}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{X}^{-}
. Moreover as
I=I(C)
, if
\alpha\in S\setminus I
, there exists
\check{\alpha}\in\check{C}
with
\alpha({\check{\alpha}})<0.
Our claim follows.

Let
\gamma_{0}(t)=a_{0}(t)x_{0}
for
t>0
and
\gamma_{0}(0)=y_{C}
, which is a path in
\overline{X}
. Using the identifications of the open
P
-orbits (cf (
3.28
), (
3.30
)), let
\gamma_{0,C}
be the corresponding path in
X_{C}
.

11.4 Lemma
.

(i) There exist
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
and a
C^{1}
-function,
p:[0,\varepsilon^{\prime}[\to P
, with
p(0)=1
, such that

\gamma(t)=p(t)\gamma_{0}(t).

In particular for
t>0
small,
\gamma(t)\in Gx_{0}
.

(ii) There exist
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
and a
C^{1}
-function
p_{C}:[0,\varepsilon^{\prime}[\to P
, with
p(0)=1
, such that

\gamma_{C}(t)=p_{C}(t)\gamma_{0,C}(t)

In particular, for
t>0
small,
\gamma(t)\in Gx_{C}
.

Proof.

(i) Let
C_{0}\in{\mathcal{F}}
of maximal dimension and containing
C
and let
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
be the subfan of
{\mathcal{F}}
which consists of the faces of
C_{0}
. It has been shown in the proof of Lemma
3.1
(i) that
\overline{A}_{X}({\mathcal{F}}_{0})
is open in
\overline{A}_{X}=\overline{A}_{X}({\mathcal{F}})
. Moreover one has
y_{C}\in\overline{A}_{X}({\mathcal{F}}_{0})
. Thus for
t
small one can has
\gamma(t)\in U(X)M\overline{A}_{X}({\mathcal{F}}_{0})
.

One has the identification of
A_{X}
(resp.
\overline{A}_{X}({\mathcal{F}}_{0})
) with

\mathbb{R}^{*\check{C}_{0}}
(resp.
\mathbb{R}^{\check{C}_{0}}
),
x_{0}
with
(1,\dots,1)
,
y_{C}
with
((0,\dots 0),(1,\dots,1))\in\mathbb{R}^{\check{C}}\times\mathbb{R}^{*\check{C_{0}}\setminus\check{C}}
. The group
A_{X}^{0}
(resp
A_{C}^{0}
) identifies with
\mathbb{R}^{*+\check{C_{0}}}
(resp.
\mathbb{R}^{*+\check{C}}\times\{(1,\dots,1)\}
).
Notice first that
a_{0}(t)_{\check{\alpha}}=t
for
\check{\alpha}\in\check{C}
and
1
for
\check{\alpha}\in\check{C}_{0}\setminus\check{C}
.

Using (
3.13
) and a local section of the projection
M/M_{H}\times\underline{A}_{X}\to M/M_{H}\times_{F_{M}}\underline{A}_{X}
, one can write
\gamma(t)=u(t)m(t)a_{1}(t)
for
0<t<\varepsilon^{\prime}
, where
u
(resp.
m
) is
C^{2}
with values in
U(X)
with
u(0)=1
(resp. in
M/M_{H}
with value
m(0)=1
) and
a_{1}
is
C^{2}
with values in
\overline{A}_{X}
,
a_{1}(0)=y_{C}
and its normal derivative in
0
, for the orbit
V_{C}
of
A_{X}
in
\overline{X}
, is equal to
e_{C}
. The
\check{C}_{0}\setminus\check{C}
coordinates of
a_{1}
go to 1 when
t
tends to zero and are
C^{2}
and for
\check{\alpha}\in\check{C}
,
a_{1}(t)_{\check{\alpha}}
tends to zero and is
C^{2}
, has derivative 1 in 0. Hence for
\check{\alpha}\in\check{C}
,
a_{1}(t)_{\check{\alpha}}=t(1+c_{\check{\alpha}}t+o(t))
for
c_{\check{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{R}
. We deduce from this that

a_{1}(t)=a_{2}(t)\gamma_{0}(t)
where
a_{2}(t)_{\check{\alpha}}=a_{1}(t)_{\check{\alpha}}
if
{\check{\alpha}}\in\check{C}_{0}\setminus\check{C}
and
a_{2}(t)_{\check{\alpha}}=(1+c_{\check{\alpha}}t+o(t))
if
{\check{\alpha}}\in\check{C}
, which is
C^{1}
. As
a_{2}(t)_{\check{\alpha}}>0
for
t>0
small, one has
a_{2}(t)\in A_{X}^{0}
for
t
small. Using a supplementary space to
\mathfrak{a}\cap\mathfrak{h}
in
\mathfrak{a}
, one constructs a morphism
s
of Lie groups from
A^{0}_{X}
to
A^{0}
which is a section of the projection
A^{0}\to A_{X}^{0}
. The proof of (i) follows from

\gamma(t)=p(t)\gamma_{0}(t),

where
p(t)=u(t)m(t)s(a_{2}(t))
.

(ii) With the above identifications,
V_{C}
identifies with
\{(0,\dots,0)\}\times\mathbb{R}^{*\check{C_{0}}\setminus\check{C}}
,
N_{V_{C}}\overline{A}({\mathcal{F}}_{0})
with
\mathbb{R}^{\check{C}}\times\mathbb{R}^{*\check{C_{0}}\setminus\check{C}}
and
X_{C}\cap\overline{\underline{A}}_{X}({\mathcal{F}}_{0})
with
\mathbb{R}^{*\check{C}}\times\mathbb{R}^{*\check{C_{0}}\setminus\check{C}}
. Then one proceeds as in (i) using coordinates.

11.5 Lemma
.

With the notation of the previous Lemma, one has:

(i) For all
f\in{\mathcal{A}}_{temp}(X)

lim_{t\to 0}a_{0}(t)^{-\rho}(f(\gamma(t))-f(\gamma_{0}(t)))=0.

(ii) For all
f\in{\mathcal{A}}_{temp}(X_{I})

lim_{t\to 0}a_{0}(t)^{-\rho}(f(\gamma_{C}(t))-f(\gamma_{0,C}(t)))=0.

Proof.

(i) Write
\gamma(t)=p(t)\gamma_{0}(t)
as in the above lemma.
For
t
small one can write
p(t)=\exp\underline{x}(t)
with
\underline{x}
a
\mathfrak{p}
-valued
C^{1}
function with value
0
in
0
. Hence there exist
0<\varepsilon^{\prime}<1
and
C>0
such that
\|\underline{x}(t)\|\leq Ct,\|\underline{x}^{\prime}(t)\|\leq C,t\in[0,\varepsilon^{\prime}[
.

Let
x\in X
. Applying the mean value theorem to the function
t\mapsto f((\exp t\underline{x})x)
with
t\in[0,\varepsilon^{\prime}[
, one gets:

|f(p(t)x)-f(x)|\leq Ct\sup_{\underline{x},\underline{y}\in\mathfrak{p},\|\underline{x}\|\leq 1,\|\underline{y}\|\leq Ct}|({L_{\underline{x}}}f)((\exp\underline{y})x)|,x\in X

Let
I(t)=a_{0}(t)^{-\rho}(f(\gamma(t))-f(\gamma_{0}(t))
. Then for
t\in[0,\varepsilon^{\prime}[
, one has:

|I(t)|\leq a_{0}(t)^{-\rho}|\sup_{\underline{x},\underline{y}\in\mathfrak{p},\|\underline{x}\|\leq 1,\|\underline{y}\|\leq C\varepsilon^{\prime}}|Ct({L_{\underline{x}}})f((\exp\underline{y})\gamma_{0}(t))|

Writing the finiteness of the tempered seminorms
q_{-N,1}(f)
and using property (
4.8
) of weights, one gets a constant
C_{1}>0
such that

|I(t)|\leq C_{1}ta_{0}(t)^{-\rho}\mathbf{v}^{-1/2}(\gamma_{0}(t))(1+\mathbf{w}(\gamma_{0}(t)))^{N}.

But from
[
24
]
, Proposition 4.3, which gives a lower bound for the volume, this implies
the existence of
C_{2}>0
such that:

|I(t)|\leq C_{2}t(1+\mathbf{w}(\gamma_{0}(t)))^{N}.

From l.c., Proposition 3.4 (2), which gives an upper bound for
\mathbf{w}
, there exists
C_{3}>0
such that
1+\mathbf{w}(\gamma_{0}(t))\leq C_{3}(1+\|\log(a_{0}(t))\|)
. But
\log a_{0}(t)=-(\log t)e_{C}
. As
t\log t
tends to zero when
t
tends to zero, our claim follows.

The proof of (ii) is similar.

From Theorem
5.1
, the constant term satisfies the asymptotic property of Theorem
5.1
for the paths
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{0,C}
. Using the two preceding Lemmas, one sees that it satisfies also the property for
\gamma
and
\gamma_{C}
. this achieves the proof of Theorem
11.3
.

11.6 Remark
.

Theorem
5.1
shows that the constant term map is independent on all choices. Notice that this independence is not used in the main body of the article.
